From the development of these marvelous Arts, the <term>Natural History of Speech<note>In the 18th century, "Natural History" referred to a systematic description and classification of any subject—not just plants or animals—tracing its origins and development through observation.</note></term> is born; & it is this

# NATURAL HISTORY OF SPEECH.

History we are about to trace, by stripping away all the technical disputes that usually follow it. This Essay will therefore be composed of three parts: **ETYMOLOGY**, **WRITING**, & **GRAMMAR**.

The first teaches us the reason <note>Here, "reason" refers to the logic, origin, and underlying meaning (ratio) of words.</note> for words; the second, how to paint them for the eyes <note>An 18th-century metaphor for writing as a visual representation of spoken sounds.</note>; the third, how to unite them.

But before entering into the details of these various subjects, let us say a word about Speech & its excellence.

Speech is the painting of our ideas through the sounds of the vocal instrument. As a fundamental part of the essence & glory of man, it distinguishes him from those Beings with whom he shares the fruits of the earth & with whom all the phenomena of animal life are common. All are born, eat, drink, and sleep like him; all are equally sensitive to pleasure, to pain, to the revolutions of time <note>In this context, the "revolutions of time" refers to the passing of seasons, the cycle of the year, or the aging process.</note>: all desire, like him, to communicate with their own kind; but they exhale only an inarticulate cry, a cry as limited in its effects as in its

# BOOK ONE.

## ON ETYMOLOGY.

### CHAPTER ONE.

### EVERY WORD HAS A REASON.

As we have said, <term>Etymology</term> <note>From the Greek *etymos* (true) and *logos* (word/study). The author treats it not just as the history of words, but as the logic behind why certain sounds represent certain things.</note> teaches us the reason for every word; it teaches us why a particular sound awakens a particular idea in us; it shows us the necessary relationships that exist between them; it follows them through that multitude of variations they have experienced & continue to experience in all Languages; and it provides the very reason for these variations which seem <unclear>to be the</unclear> sole effect of chance & inconstancy.

It is this exact relationship between names & the objects they designate that constitutes the force & energy of words <note>In 18th-century grammar, "energy" refers to the expressive power and clarity of a word—how effectively it mirrors the nature of the thing it describes.</note>; which maintained them at the moment they were established, which obliged all men to adopt them, & prevented them from

## CHAPTER II.

->*The rationale for every word is its relationship to the object it designates.*<-

If words are not arbitrary, if there was some motive for imposing one name on an object rather than another, this motive was necessarily the relationship seen between that name and the object one wished to name. Indeed, when we bestow a name, it is to recall to our mind the object it designates; it is to paint it <note>The author uses "paint" (*peindre*) to evoke the 18th-century idea that language acts as a mirror or a landscape of human thought.</note> for our imagination. But will we not be more certain of producing this effect when the name of this object is truly a depiction of it, rather than when it bears no relationship to it?

This is precisely what was done by the first people who gave names to things: they did not invent these names; one invents nothing, and what weight would a language of whim have carried? What man could have been the first to say: SUCH A WORD SHALL SIGNIFY SUCH A THING?

<vocab>arbitrary, motive, relationship, name, depiction, Language, whim, to impose, sign</vocab>

<summary>The author argues that language is not an arbitrary invention but is based on a natural connection between a word's sound and the object it represents. He suggests that the first creators of language chose names that acted as "paintings" or depictions of things to make communication effective and logical.</summary>
<keywords>origin of language, linguistic representation, arbitrary signs, etymology, naming, philosophy of speech</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page 10, moving from the rejection of "whim" in language to a comparison between linguistic rules and social order. It concludes by explaining how primitive man chose sounds based on their descriptive power.</meta>

<page-num>11</page-num>
<header>ORIGIN OF LANGUAGE 11</header>

And how would he have made himself understood? The arbitrary <note>In this context, "the arbitrary" refers to the philosophical concept that words have no natural connection to the things they represent—a theory the author is arguing against.</note> has no authority and could never become law, in words just as in the conduct of Peoples and Families: do not even Young People revolt against the arbitrary? Is it not necessary to make them feel the reason for everything prescribed to them, so that they might submit with pleasure, seeing that it is order, and not a despotic will, that they obey?

The first who spoke therefore designated beings by sounds that painted their qualities; this was all the easier because, in the beginnings, there were few objects to paint <note>The author uses "paint" (*peindre*) to describe how a sound can evoke the sensory characteristics of an object.</note>, these objects were striking, and one could choose from a multitude of sounds; thus, one necessarily preferred sounds that were as striking as the objects themselves.

# CHAPTER III.

### *Words have different qualities.*

This presupposes that words, or the sounds of which they are composed, possess distinct qualities, and thus cannot be applied interchangeably to the same objects; that a single sound can only depict <note>original: "peindre" (to paint). The author uses this artistic term to describe how sounds create a sensory representation of an object in the mind.</note> a certain class of beings; and that it cannot suit two beings who have no relationship to one another. It is the same with the sounds of Speech as it is with the sounds of Instruments: here, are the sounds of alarm the same as those of rejoicing?

It is a physical and incontestable truth that each of the sounds produced by the vocal instrument <note>The "vocal instrument" refers to the human throat, tongue, and lips, viewed through the Enlightenment lens of the body as a biological machine.</note> has qualities that are unique to it, and which differ essentially from the qualities noticed in others. They are not all equally pleasant, equally soft, equally fast: some are slow, others rapid; some harsh, others flattering; some somber, others

<vocab>Speech, sounds, qualities, vocal instrument, rejoicing</vocab>

<summary>The author argues that sounds possess inherent physical properties—such as speed, tone, and texture—that naturally limit which objects they can describe. He compares the human voice to musical instruments, noting that different sounds are required to evoke different emotions like alarm or joy.</summary>
<keywords>phonetic symbolism, vocal instrument, acoustics, linguistics, sensory language, natural properties of sound</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 12 regarding the inherent qualities of sounds. While the previous page established that sounds have physical traits like speed and texture, this page argues that these traits must be matched to the objects they describe to create a functional language.</meta>

<header>ORIGIN OF LANGUAGE.</header>

---

sonorous. One must therefore choose between them, or resolve to be a poor painter <note>The author continues the metaphor of the "painter" from the previous page, suggesting that a speaker "paints" an image in the listener's mind using sounds instead of colors.</note>, to never be understood, to form a language without harmony, without grace, without energy, always contrary to nature; a Language, in a word, such as cannot exist.

Is it necessary to observe that to name an object or to paint it through vocal sounds, it suffices to paint it by the relationships it has with those sounds—and that this is the only way one could do so; that it would be absurd to seek any other?

There is no object that does not have a more or less close relationship with vocal sounds, and which cannot be painted by these sounds.

Animals, for example, are distinguished by cries that are unique to them; and we recognize them by these cries: one will therefore only have to imitate these cries, and they will form the painting of these animals; we will recognize them by these sounds, and these sounds will become their proper name: they will form the most perfect, the most energetic sonorous painting; it will be *speaking* <note>original: "parlante." Here meaning the sound is so evocative and natural that it explains itself without needing a definition.</note>.

Inanimate objects will be painted by...

Other objects will be depicted by sounds that express the relationship of these objects with animated beings: thus, in almost all **Languages**, the name of the **Ox** has become the name for bulk <note>original: "groſſeur." The author is likely referring to the phonetic root "bu-" or "bo-" found in many Indo-European languages (like the Greek *bous*), which he argues is the root of words for things that are large or swelling.</note>, and for everything that is large.

All objects were thus found to be named by **imitation** or by **comparison**.

However, almost all names appear arbitrary <term>Arbitrary: in linguistics, the idea that there is no natural or "logical" reason why a specific sound represents a specific thing.</term> in practice, and they vary constantly from one language to another: but this does not at all invalidate the truths we have just developed. Most names, imitative in their origin, have been altered imperceptibly, so that one cannot perceive—without extreme attention—their relationships with the objects they designate; and as names given by comparison have necessarily...

# CHAPTER IV.

## *Speech is of a divine origin.*

Although Language is the application of sounds to objects that have some relationship with them, it is nonetheless of a divine origin. It was not men who formed these sounds and these relationships; it is God who made Man a speaking Being.

Without doubt, Speech came from God himself: He alone could put the finishing touch on the admirable qualities of man, by endowing him with the Art of speaking—with that Art, the bond of society, which leads man from

<meta>catchword: from [de]</meta>

<summary>The author concludes that linguistic variation results from shifting cultural perspectives over time, but maintains that the capacity for speech is a divine gift from God intended to foster human society.</summary>
<keywords>divine origin, nature of language, social bond, linguistic change, God, human nature</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 15, which argued that the art of speaking leads humanity from one level of understanding to the next, illustrating the infinite nature of human inquiry and the divine design of our vocal capacity.</meta>

<page-num>16</page-num>
<header>NATURAL HISTORY OF SPEECH.</header>

## CHAPTER VI.

### *Elements of Speech.*

Since Speech was not at all the result of chance or the mere invention of men, and since it is not the arbitrary effect of Divine Power either, but is instead founded upon Elements taken from Nature—matched both to the nature of man and to that of the objects he is obliged to paint—one can hope to discover the manner in which it is formed and the causes of this energy <note>In 18th-century linguistics, "energy" refers to the expressive power and vitality of a language.</note>

# CHAPTER VII.

## On the Etymological Art.

But what is this **Etymological Art** which leads to the source of **Speech**, which accounts for every word, and which shows the relationships between **Languages**?

Its name, which comes to us from the Greeks, was not formed by chance or arbitrarily: it is a word that accurately depicts the object it designates; but for this purpose, one must know the value of the elements of which it is formed.

**ETYMOLOGY** is composed of the Greek words *Logos* <note>original Greek: λόγος</note>, speech, and *étymos* <note>original Greek: ἔτυμος</note>, true; this latter word was formed from the Oriental <term>תם</term> <note>Referring to the Hebrew/Semitic root T-M, which implies completeness or integrity</note>, *Tym* or *Tum*, which signifies, *perfection, justice, truth*

<meta>catchword: truth (In the original French, the word "vérité" is split between pages; "vé-" appears here, with "rité" as the catchword for the next page.)</meta>
<vocab>Etymological Art, Logos, étymos, Oriental, תם, Tym, Tum, perfection, justice</vocab>

<summary>The author introduces Chapter VII, defining "Etymological Art" as the study of word origins and language relationships. He breaks down the Greek roots of the word "Etymology," linking it further back to an "Oriental" (Semitic) root meaning perfection or truth.</summary>
<keywords>etymology, Greek roots, Hebrew roots, linguistic origins, definition of language, logos, etymos</keywords>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page completes the word "truth" (vérité) from the catchword on page 24.</meta>

<page-num>25</page-num>
<header>ORIGIN OF LANGUAGE.</header>

...truth, without which there is nothing perfect. Etymology therefore signifies *True Speech*, the *just* & *exact word*: it consists of the perfect knowledge of the value of words, of their relationships with their objects, of their origin, and of their revolutions <note>The author uses "revolutions" to describe the historical changes and shifts in meaning that words undergo over time.</note>. To know a word is, in effect, to know the causes that led to it being assigned the meaning with which it is clothed, the Language from which it originates, the family to which it belongs, and the alterations it has undergone.

The Etymological Art consists of the principles and rules by which one discovers all these things.

One sees by this that we use this word in a much broader sense than all those who have conducted research on etymologies. Until now, in dealing with the etymology of a word, researchers sought only to know from which Language it had been borrowed and to which Languages it was common.

But this was a field far too narrow, and for that very reason, more dangerous

This advantage is inestimable, because of the multitude of words one must learn when called upon to study **Languages**; even the firmest and most fortunate memory eventually collapses under this enormous weight, if one does not know how to relieve it through the most effective means; but there is none that can be compared to this *etymological path*; for by presenting at a single glance all the derivatives and all the compounds of the same word across all **Languages**, it allows us to grasp them all at once; so that the attention necessary to retain one causes us to retain a thousand; this careful management of our strength multiplies it, in a way, to infinity; so that we accomplish in a short time and without difficulty what previously required prodigious effort.

<page-num>29</page-num>
->ORIGIN OF LANGUAGE.<-

derives from the Etymological Art is the ability to follow the progression of ideas acquired by mankind. Words were created only to express ideas; thus, the path of ideas was followed in order to form them. One will therefore find—in the arrangement of words by families and in the grouping of primitive words <note>The author refers to "primitive words" as the original, simple roots from which more complex vocabulary is derived.</note>—the manner in which humans proceeded in their thoughts: those they had first, those born from these initial ones, those they owed to Nature, or those which were the result of their own skill and reflection.

From this, two inestimable advantages for remembering words arise: the connection of the ideas that gave birth to them, and the derivation of these words themselves. Through the former, one sees the words that *ought* to exist; through the latter, one sees that they *do* exist and how they were formed.

By then comparing **Languages** in this regard, one sees those that have made the most of these first **Elements**, those where the greatest number of ideas have been combined, and those where the **Art of reflection**, invention, or perfection has been carried the furthest.

# CHAPTER VIII.

## *Principles of the Etymological Art, in Relation to Languages in General.*

The more useful the knowledge of **Etymologies** <term>Etymologies: the study of the origins of words and how their form and meaning have changed over time.</term> is, the more important it becomes to establish it upon a solid foundation; this is all the more necessary as nothing is easier than to go astray in the search for Etymologies, and to perceive relationships between words that have none and which belong to families very distant from one another. a Language that subdivides into a great number of branches; and these branches bear the name of Dialects. Dialects are therefore the internal or national nuances of a Language.

When a Language is spoken by a great number of different Nations, among whom it has undergone significant alterations, such that it resembles its original self much less, this Language subdivides into a great number of others which are its **Daughters**, and it is their **Mother**. ## CHAPTER IX.

### *Principles of the Etymological Art, regarding the form of words.*

The form of words consists of the letters of which they are composed and the arrangement of those letters: but in this regard, the same word changes constantly when passing from one Language to another: *fervor* <note>Latin for "boiling heat"</note> becomes *ferveur* <note>Modern French for "fervor"</note>; *octo* <note>Latin for "eight"</note> becomes *huict*, then *huit* <note>French for "eight"</note>; *cœlum* <note>Latin for "sky" or "heaven"</note>, *ciel* <note>French for "sky"</note>; and what we pronounce *poivre* <note>French for "pepper"</note>, is pronounced in Latin *piper*; just as we say *golfe* <note>French for "gulf"</note>, while the Greeks say *golpos* <note>original Greek: κόλπος (kolpos), meaning a bay or fold</note>.

Thus, just as Languages change constantly, each of the words of which they are composed successively takes on the most varied forms.

These alterations, however, must follow constant rules, by means of which one can always trace back to the pri-

<header>ORIGIN OF LANGUAGE.</header>

mary origin of these words, and follow them through all their metamorphoses. In effect, as words have never been arbitrary <note>The author is challenging the idea that language is a social contract or random invention; he believes words are naturally tied to the things they describe.</note>, their alterations could never have been so: they have always had physical causes for which it is easy to provide an explanation.

The changes of form that words undergo can be reduced to three classes: changes of *vowels*, changes of *consonants*, and changes of *place* <term>metathesis: the switching of the order of letters or sounds within a word over time</term> between the letters that compose a word: hence, three principles of etymology.

### FIRST PRINCIPLE.

> *Changes or alterations in Vowels do not prevent one from recognizing the origin of words.*

NATURAL HISTORY OF SPEECH.

vowels, or to most of them. We have, for example, changed almost all the vowels of Latin words: from their *a* we have made *ai* and *e*: *pane* <note>Latin: "bread"</note> became *pain*; *fame* <note>Latin: "hunger"</note> became *faim*; *mare* <note>Latin: "sea"</note> became *mer*.

From their *e* we have made *oi* and *i*: *serus* <note>Latin: "late"</note> became *soir* <note>"evening"</note>; *cera* <note>Latin: "wax"</note> became *cire*.

From their *i* we have made *a* and *e*: *lingua* <note>Latin: "tongue" or "language"</note> became *langue*; *firmus* <note>Latin: "strong" or "firm"</note> became *ferme*.

We have changed their *o* into several other vowels: into *eu*; *hora* <note>Latin: "hour"</note> became *heure*; *honor* <note>Latin: "honor"</note> became *honneur*. Into *u*; *octo* <note>Latin: "eight"</note> became *huict* <note>An archaic French spelling</note> or *huit*. Into *ou*; *totus* <note>Latin: "all" or "whole"</note> became *tout*. Into *ui*; *corium* <note>Latin: "leather" or "hide"</note> became *cuir*, and so on.

The *u* of the Romans is often an *o* in French: *urtica* <note>Latin: "nettle"</note> became *ortie*; *numerus* <note>Latin: "number"</note> became *nombre*; and sometimes *oi*: *nux* <note>Latin: "nut"</note> became *noix*.

These same words, in passing to other peoples, were likewise associated with vowels that were neither the vowel found in the Latin word, nor the one found in French: thus *nox* <note>Latin: "night"</note> or *nuit* <note>French: "night"</note> are *night* in English, *nacht* in German, *notte* in Italian, and so on.

# ORIGIN OF LANGUAGE.

is very simple: it is drawn from the very nature of vowels. Their pronunciation is so light, so subtle, and the pronunciation of one has such a close relationship to the pronunciation of vowels of the same class <note>The author likely refers to vowel "orders" or classes such as front/back or high/low vowels</note>, that they are constantly confused with one another. If twenty people were to repeat or write down a foreign word that they were hearing for the first time, they would all differ in their rendition of its vowels.

It follows from this that a person who refuses to recognize the relationship between two words simply because those words do not share the same vowel would be acting against all reason, and could never compare two languages with one another.

Indeed, despite this change of vowels, the word does not cease to be the same or to belong to the same family, since they share a common meaning and the consonants and the resulting generic sound <note>The "generic sound" refers to the stable acoustic core of a word, which the author argues is maintained by consonants even when vowels change.</note> are similar.

## SECOND PRINCIPLE.

*The change or alteration of a portion of the consonants within a word does not prevent us from recognizing that word’s origin.*

Although the Latins say *pellis* <note>Latin: "skin"</note> and *sapor* <note>Latin: "flavor" or "taste"</note>, while we pronounce *favor* <note>French: "faveur"</note> and *skin* <note>French: "peau," which the author links phonetically to "sapor/favor" and "pellis"</note>—formerly *pel*, from which come *to peel* <note>French: "peler"</note> and *pelisse* <note>A fur-lined garment</note>—one nonetheless recognizes that these French words are the same as the Latin words to which they correspond. This is because the meaning and a portion of the consonants are the same, and because the generic sound <note>The "generic sound" refers to the core acoustic identity maintained by consonants of the same family.</note> offered by these words falls within the same class; one perceives quite clearly that they are merely nuances of the same sound.

This is further based on the nature of consonants. There exist, as we shall see hereafter, only seven orders of consonants, and each of them is composed of "weak" and "strong" consonants, whose sound differs only by a greater or lesser degree of force. Thus, if one fails even slightly to grasp this degree of force in

In *favor* <note>French: "faveur"</note>, for example, we have substituted the weak consonant **v** for the strong consonant **P** <note>The author is likely referring to the Latin root "sapor" or "p" sounds in related roots</note>. It is this ongoing substitution of a strong consonant for a weak one that had prevented the recognition of the relationships between the words used by several languages to express what we understand by the word **Speech** <note>French: "Parole"</note>, all of which belong to the same root as *parole* and *parler* <note>French: "to speak"</note>—the root **Par**, which signifies the *action of expressing one's ideas through language or Speech*.

In Celtic, **Far**: speech.
In Breton, **A-var**: speech.

### NATURAL HISTORY OF SPEECH.

In German, *Mahre* <note>original: "Mahre"; an archaic word for a story, news, or account, related to the modern German "Märchen"</note>, discourse.
In Irish, *Bearla* <note>original: "Bearla"; in modern Irish, "Béarla" refers to the English language, but historically it signified language or speech in general</note>, speech.
In Hebrew, *De-ber* or *De-ver* <note>original: "De-ber" or "De-ver"; the Hebrew root D-B-R (דבר) meaning "to speak"</note>, speech.
In Latin, *Verbum* <note>original: "Verbum"</note>, word, speech.

One easily sees the relationship between all these words, even though the first consonant almost always changes—being sometimes *P*, sometimes *B*, *V*, *M*, or *F*.

1°. The meaning of all these words is constantly the same; it is always *speech* that they designate, or ideas related to speech.
2°. Of the three essential characters <note>The author uses "characters" to refer to the individual phonetic components or letters of the root</note> of which this word is composed, the last two are the same in all these languages, or at least the final consonant *R*: it is always *ar*, or *er*.
3°. The consonants which occupy the first place here—these *P*, *B*, *V*, *F*, and *M*—are consonants of the same order, which are all pronounced with the lips, and are for that reason called *labials* <term>labials: a category of speech sounds formed using the lips, such as [p], [b], [m], [f], and [v]</term>: they are thus, in a way, a single sound, which differs only by a greater or lesser degree of force; nuances of the same sound which do not prevent one from recognizing a constant sound in this word; just

## THIRD PRINCIPLE.

*The change in the placement of a few letters in a word does not prevent us from recognizing its origin.*

Often the fundamental letters of a word change their position as they are passed from one Language to another.

2°. Often, one also adds a vowel to the beginning of **primitive Roots** <term>primitive roots: the most basic, original form of a word from which others are derived</term>, so that the letter which should have been the first is only the second. Thus our old word *state* <note>original: "Estat"</note>, which we now write as *state* <note>original: "Etat"</note>, came from *status* <note>original: "Status"</note> through the addition of the vowel *E*.

3°. Often, one also adds consonants to the end or the beginning of words: from *ear* <note>original: "aure"</note>, *ear* <note>original: "oreille"</note>; from *sun* <note>original: "sole"</note>, *sun* <note>original: "soleil"</note>. We say *gilded* <note>original: "doré"</note> instead of saying *gilded* <note>original: "oré"</note>, which in Latin is *adorned with gold* <note>original: "aurato"</note>.

4°. Nothing is more common than the suppression of some letter. From *time* <note>original: "temps"</note>, one made *time* <note>original: "tems"</note>; from *your* <note>original: "vostre"</note>, *your* <note>original: "votre"</note>.

5°. Finally, one continually unites two or more **radical words** <term>radical words: the core building blocks or base words that carry the primary meaning</term> to form only a single one. Sometimes one recognizes the joined words without difficulty: *couch grass* <note>original: "Chiendent," literally "dog's tooth"</note>, *close-fitting coat* <note>original: "justaucorps," literally "just-to-body"</note>, *coat rack* <note>original: "portemanteau," literally "carry-cloak"</note>, *master key* <note>original: "passepartout," literally "pass-everywhere"</note>.

Often one no longer recognizes the words that served in this composition. Would one suspect these words *at the* <note>original: "au"</note>, *then* <note>original: "alors"</note>, *still* <note>original: "encore"</note>, or *ribbon* <note>original: "ruban"</note> of being words composed by the union of—

## CHAPTER X.
### *Rules to follow in the search for Etymologies.*

Since the science of Etymologies <term>Etymology: the study of the origin of words and the way in which their meanings have changed throughout history</term> rests upon a small number of clear and indisputable principles, its practice can be reduced to a few simple rules. These are determined by these principles and will ensure the investigation of Etymologies, giving them the greatest possible certainty. These are the principal rules:

1°. Do not dwell on the vowels of words to recognize their relationships. <note>Vowels often shift between dialects or over time, while consonants tend to remain more stable indicators of a word's root.</note>

2°. Do not confuse accessory letters <term>accessory letters: extra letters added to a word for sound or grammar that aren't part of the original core root</term>...

<sig>P ij</sig>

<vocab>Etymologies, Language, etymology, ribbon, reu-band, vowels, accessory letters, grammar</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes the discussion on how common French words like 'au' and 'alors' are contracted from multiple words and introduces a formal methodology for etymological study. It emphasizes that researchers should focus on core roots rather than changing vowels or extra decorative letters.</summary>

<keywords>etymology, linguistic rules, word contraction, vowel shifts, root words, French language history, ribbon etymology</keywords>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page continues the numbered list of rules for etymological research from page 51, completing Rule 2 and providing Rules 3 through 7.</meta>

# NATURAL HISTORY OF SPEECH.

...of a word with its primitive and fundamental letters.

3°. Reduce words to radicals <term>radicals: the core roots or most basic forms of words</term> usually composed of two consonants separated by a strong vowel.

4°. Classify all words into families.

5°. Subordinate all moral, spiritual, or figurative meanings of a word to its physical sense. <note>This rule reflects the 18th-century view that all abstract language begins with physical descriptions—for example, the word "spirit" originally meant "breath."</note>

6°. Assume no alteration in a word that cannot be justified by usage and by analogy <term>analogy: the linguistic pattern where words change following the example of similar words</term>.

7°. Avoid any forced Etymology, or any that does not carry conviction with it.

<sig>D ij</sig>

<vocab>primitive letters, radicals, consonants, strong vowel, word families, physical sense, moral senses, analogy, Etymology</vocab>

<summary>This page outlines the final five rules for scientific etymology, emphasizing the importance of identifying core radicals and prioritizing physical meanings over figurative ones. The author argues for a disciplined approach that avoids speculative or "forced" word origins.</summary>

### CHAPTER ONE.
### *THE VOCAL INSTRUMENT.*

The knowledge of an Art always depends on the Elements that compose it: one could not, therefore, form a correct idea of the origin of Language and the relationship between Languages without knowing their primary causes—especially the nature and effects of the Vocal Instrument, from which all the elements of speech are drawn; those sounds without which there would be no speech, no painting of ideas. <note>The author uses "painting of ideas" to describe how speech makes abstract thoughts visible or perceptible to others.</note>

The Vocal Instrument is the collection of organs by means of which Man manifests his ideas through speech, and his sensations through the voice and through song.

These organs are very numerous;

<header>NATURAL HISTORY OF SPEECH.</header>

they compose a very complex instrument, which combines all the advantages of wind instruments, such as the flute; string instruments, such as the violin; and keyboard instruments <note>original: "instrumens à touches," literally instruments with keys</note>, such as the organ, with which it has the most in common; and which is, of all musical instruments invented by man, the most resonant, the most varied, and the most closely resembling the human voice.

Like the organ, the Vocal Instrument has bellows, a case, pipes, and keys. The bellows are the lungs; the pipes, the throat <note>original: "gosier"</note> and the nostrils; the mouth is the case; and its walls <note>the surfaces of the mouth like the palate and teeth</note>, the keys.

This instrument provides man with simple sounds, such as the voice and song; and representative sounds, which are modifications of the voice, such as vowels and consonants.

**THE MUSCLES** are organs intended for movement. They consist of a mixture of soft and reddish fibers, vessels, nerves, and membranes, interlaced and forming a tissue: they terminate at both ends with whiter, more solid, and more densely packed fibers that constitute the attachments by which the muscles hold onto the neighboring parts they must put into play.

**NERVES** are whitish cords of different sizes, which originate from the brain and the *medulla oblongata* <term>medulla oblongata: the base of the brainstem, responsible for autonomic functions like breathing</term>, spreading into all parts of the body, serving as the seat of sensation, and the cause of all movements of the muscles.

All the fibers that compose these nerves and these muscles are hollow and filled with cells, where a fluid floats <note>original: "nâge un fluide"; this refers to the 18th-century concept of "animal spirits" or "nerve fluid" that was thought to transmit motion</note> that has the property of becoming agitated and swelling by an effect of the will: then the vessels containing it necessarily widen: consequently, they shorten, and by shortening, they stretch and displace all the parts to which they are attached, resulting in the movement of the parts of the body.

The lungs are attached by their lower end to various muscles, the principal of which is the **DIAPHRAGM**; and by their upper end, to a canal called the **WINDPIPE** <term>trachea-artery: an archaic term for the trachea, which was historically believed to carry air like an "artery" for the breath</term> and through which they communicate with the outside air.

When the diaphragm rises or contracts, it lifts the ribs which weigh upon the chest: by this means, the bottom of the lungs moves closer to the top and widens by extending into the void left by the ribs: then air enters with ease through the **Windpipe** <term>trachée-artère: literally "trachea-artery." Following the windpipe, at its upper extremity, is another much shorter channel called the **LARYNX**; placed at the front of the neck, it forms the "knot of the throat," or the **Adam's apple**. Its upper opening is positioned behind the base of the tongue, so that it receives the air coming from the nostrils as well as that which enters through the mouth. It is composed of five cartilages, joined by muscles and membranes.

Of these cartilages, one is placed in the front; it is the largest of all; and having the shape of a shield, it bears its name <term>Thyroid cartilage: From the Greek *thyreos*, meaning "shield."</term>. The one that serves as a base for the others is shaped like a ring <term>Cricoid cartilage: From the Greek *krikos*, meaning "ring."</term>: two in the shape of a funnel <term>Arytenoid cartilages: From the Greek *arytaina*, meaning "ladle" or "funnel." All these cartilages move by means of twelve muscles, which lengthen or widen the opening, or shorten or narrow it.

The upper end of the larynx rests upon the ***hyoid*** bone placed at the base of the tongue: it owes its name to its resemblance to the letter U, which was pronounced *hy* in Greek <note>original: "hy en Grec". At the top of the larynx, between its anterior cartilage <note>The thyroid cartilage or "shield."</note> and the posterior cartilages <note>The arytenoid cartilages.</note>, within the opening they leave between them, are semi-circular ligaments that narrow this opening and leave only a very small space for the air; this space is called the **glottis**, and these ligaments form its ***lips*** <note>These "lips" are known today as the vocal folds or vocal cords.</note>.

--- Page 89 ---
<meta>This page continues the anatomical description of the larynx and glottis from the previous page, shifting focus from the structure of the cartilages to the mechanics of the vocal ligaments and the production of sound.</meta>

->### ORIGIN OF LANGUAGE.<-
61

Each of these ligaments or muscles is doubled upon itself and contains a bundle of fibers; they become longer and less curved as they are stretched, to the point that they can join together so as to leave no passage for air. Depending on whether the opening they leave is larger or smaller, different tones result. The wider these lips <note>Today called the vocal folds or vocal cords.</note> are spread, the deeper the tone; it becomes high-pitched as they draw closer together.

One can even view the fibers that compose these lips as so many ribbons or strings, which the air sets in motion by turns, depending on whether it exits with more or less force; from this, various sounds result within the **vocal instrument**.

This is how the voice is produced, which is nothing other than the air escaping from the lungs and rendered sonorous by the friction it experiences as it exits with effort through the glottis: but these are not the only effects it undergoes; for it must then become speech.

## CHAPTER II.

->*Organs that form Speech.*<-

However admirable the portion of the vocal instrument we have just described may be, it does not constitute the whole of this instrument; and the part that remains for us to explore is no less wonderful or engaging: it is formed by the entire cavity of the mouth, the **resonance box** <note>The author uses the word "caisse," comparing the oral cavity to the body of a stringed instrument like a violin or lute, which amplifies and shapes sound.</note> of the vocal instrument. It is this box which, through its construction and the various organs of which it is composed, gives rise to all those modifications of the voice that make it fit to form the various languages spread across the Earth, and to paint our ideas in the most exact manner and with the most pleasant colors.

# CHAPTER III.
## *How Man was led to Speech.*

Such is the marvelous instrument with which the Divinity <note>The author refers here to a Creator, following the 18th-century tradition of viewing natural history as a reflection of divine design.</note> presented Man when He formed him, and which was to serve him to manifest his sensations through cries, his pleasures through song, and his ideas through speech. But how did Man know that he was endowed with the art of speech? It is because he carried the sense of it within himself.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how air is modified by the vocal apparatus. It transitions from the previous page's description of air escaping through the lips to the categorization of these modifications as "sounds" or "vowels," using the metaphor of a musical instrument.</meta>

the same force, its sonorous quality undergoes various modifications, as if it were passing through organic pipes <note>The author uses "organic pipes" to describe the various parts of the vocal tract (throat, mouth, etc.) that shape sound, much like the pipes of an organ.</note> that are more or less open.

These modifications are called SOUNDS; this is, in effect, the way in which the vocal air sounds to our ears. They are also called VOWELS, as they are the product of the voice; but we reserve this latter word for *written sounds* <note>The author makes a distinction common in 18th-century linguistics: "sounds" are what we hear, while "vowels" are the characters used to represent them in writing.</note>.

Since the opening of the mouth is capable of a very great number of gradations, there will necessarily exist a very great number of sounds. One can, however, reduce them to a small number of fundamental sounds which together form an octave drawn from nature, since the vocal instrument is a true flute, and every kind of harmony is contained within the octave.

The highest of the seven sounds that compose this octave is pronounced through the largest possible opening of the mouth; and the lowest, through the smallest possible opening. The first is A, the other is OU <note>In French phonetics, "OU" represents the /u/ sound, as in the English word "soup."</note>: all other sounds are found between these two; such as...

<meta>This page continues the classification of vowels begun on page 69, where the author compared the human voice to a musical instrument. # NATURAL HISTORY OF SPEECH.

...as the *Æ* <note>original: "Æ des Latins." This refers to the Latin diphthong which was pronounced as a long, open "e" sound.</note> of the Latins, or our extremely open *E*; our ordinary *E*, pronounced from the middle of the mouth; and *I*, *O*, and *U*, which can only be pronounced by the elongation of the mouth, which closes more and more.

These seven sounds or vowels were called by the Ancients, SPIRITS, because they are the result of the breath, which was called *spiritus* <note>The author is highlighting the etymological connection between "spirit" and the Latin *spiritus*, meaning breath or blowing. In ancient grammar, "spirit" referred to the force of breath used in articulation.</note>.

Such is the property of sounds: they can last as long as the expiration from the chest that forms them continues, since they are nothing other than the air provided by this expiration and modified by the lips.

They also have the property of being pronounced in various ways:

1st. With great sweetness, from the middle of the mouth.

2nd. From the back of the throat, by breathing with force.

3rd. By ending them with a slight nasal sound.

4th. They are capable of a long pronunciation and a short pronunciation.

---

(1) For the same reason that we resort to new words to express our ideas when the Language does not provide them, we adopt a new meaning for the words it does provide when that meaning is analogous to what the words already convey. Thus, by employing the term *Modes* for the various manners of pronunciation used by each People, we are applying it in a specific way, yet one that is perfectly analogous to the meaning this term holds in the language of *Music*, which uses this word <note>In 18th-century music theory, a "mode" referred to the specific arrangement of intervals in a scale (like major or minor), which dictates the "character" of the music—just as Gébelin argues phonetic "modes" dictate the character of a language.</note>

These **Modes** arise from the extreme ease with which one can pronounce equally from the outer end of the vocal instrument, from its inner end, from the middle of the mouth, etc.; so that, depending on whether the voice places its effort on one or the other of these points, the pronunciation is different.

In regions where the cold is rigorous, where all movement is slowed, and where all fibers are tightened, the mouth opens much less; one pronounces from the front of the mouth, whistling rather than speaking.

In the mountains, where the lungs are more exercised than in the plains, pronunciation is much more rapid.

Those who live in abundance and whose manners <note>The French "mœurs" refers to the collective customs, morals, and social habits of a culture.</note> are gentle and easy, have a gentle and softened pronunciation; they shun the harsh and strongly pronounced sounds of coarser Peoples.

Among the same People, pronunciation changes along with their manners: we could not bear the speech of our Fathers of the fifteenth century; it would seem infinitely too harsh to us.

in the study of Languages, when one recognizes their relationships through this difference in pronunciation.

Such are the principles or general laws according to which one can compare these various pronunciations.

I. The vowel of a primitive word changes constantly, weakening and descending from the strongest sounds to the softest sounds.

A changing into E; E into I; I into U, etc.
*Manus* <note>Latin: hand</note>, *main* <note>French: hand</note>.
*Ebur* <note>Latin: ivory</note>, *yvoire* <note>French: ivory</note>.
*Skim* in English, *écume* in French <note>foam or scum</note>. The word for "name" <note>original French: "nom"</note> is pronounced *Name* in German, *noun* in English, and *nimi* in Finnish.

II. The vowel is placed before or after the consonant that forms the same word with it; thus, while some say *Ab* or *Am* for Father and Mother, others pronounce them *Pa* and *Ma*. <note>This refers to the concept of metathesis or the shifting position of sounds within a syllable, particularly in "nursery words" found across different cultures.</note>

III. The aspiration <note>The "h" sound or breathy quality of a letter</note> changes into a simple vowel, or is softened by a consonant. *Habere* <note>Latin: to have</note>, *Avoir* <note>French: to have</note>. *Hordeum* <note>Latin: barley</note>, *Orge* <note>French: barley</note>.
*Hardes* <note>French: clothes/rags</note>, in the *Langue d’Oc* <note>The Occitan language of Southern France</note>, *Fardes*.
*Hodiernus* <note>Latin: of today</note>, Modern.

## CHAPTER VII.

### *Values assigned to simple or primitive Sounds.*

The ORIGIN of words is not difficult to find; they are born from the sounds and intonations of the vocal Instrument <note>the author treats the human vocal apparatus—the throat, tongue, and lips—as a biological musical instrument</note>; these were the Elements of Language; one cannot look for them anywhere else. Sounds were destined by their nature to portray or designate sensations; and intonations became likewise—by their nature being absolutely different from that of sounds—the portrayal of ideas. So it is that all words are subdivided into two great Classes: words that portray sensations, and words that por- <note>The text cuts off mid-word at the end of the page; the French "peignent" translates here as "portray" or "paint"</note>

<summary>This page concludes the introductory section on phonetic laws and opens Chapter VII, which introduces the author's theory that sounds represent physical sensations while intonations represent abstract ideas.</summary>

->ORIGIN OF LANGUAGE.<-

—tray ideas <note>This completes the word "portray" (French: *peignent*) from the previous page</note>; and these Classes are subdivided into others, relative to sensations and ideas. Thus, one sees words emerge from Nature and expand along with knowledge, while they all lead back to those primary elements without which no language would exist.

The sound **A**, the highest of all <note>The author likely refers to the "openness" of the vowel sound or its perceived priority in the vocal scale</note>, designates the state by which one is affected, what is proper to us, consequently what one possesses, what one enjoys; as well as dominance and priority.

From this come these French expressions:
*He HAS a high fever* <note>original: *Il A une grosse fiévre*</note>, where **A** designates the state by which one is affected.
*He HAS great wealth* <note>original: *Il A de grands biens*</note>, where **A** designates what one possesses.
*This carriage BELONGS TO the Queen* <note>original: *Cet Equipage est A la Reine*</note>, where **A** designates property.
*He HAS written* <note>original: *Il A écrit*</note>, where **A** designates that one has reached the state to which one aspired, regarding writing.

It is the same for most other languages.

<meta>This page continues the phonetic analysis of primary sounds begun on the previous page. Having discussed the sound "A," the author now moves through the other vowels and their supposed primitive meanings.</meta>

->NATURAL HISTORY OF SPEECH.<-

The sound **HÉ**, which is extremely open and also written as **H**, **AI**, or **Æ**, and whose aspiration softens into *khé*, *ghé*, *qué*, etc., signified life and everything that serves to sustain life, such as fields, the earth, plants, and so on.

The sound **E** designates everything relative to existence and the quality of being. From this comes the verb **E**, from which the French *il est* <note>original: "il eſt" (he is)</note>, *être* <note>original: "être" (to be)</note>, etc., are derived.

The sound **I**, often pronounced **EI** or **AI**, signifies the hand or touch; it includes all the ideas carried by the concept of the hand: *power, protection, help, a handful, a wing*, etc. <note>The author links the sound "I" to the Hebrew letter "Yodh," which means "hand" and represents the /i/ or /j/ sound.</note>

The sound **O**, a cry of admiration, became the name for light—one of the most pleasing sensations. It likewise became the name for everything that causes this sensation: fire, the sun, the eyes, and the sense of sight.

The sound **U**, which depicts the action of drawing in liquids or sipping <term>humer: to suck in or inhale a liquid or scent</term>, served to designate water, humidity, humors, and the very action of sipping. This sound changed sometimes into *hy* and sometimes into *o*; from this come *hydra* and *dropsy* <note>original: "hydre & hydropiſie." Both words derive from the Greek "hydor" (water). Hydra is a water-serpent, and dropsy (edema) is an accumulation of fluid in the body.</note>, one of which designates a water serpent and the other a disease caused by water.

<summary>This page presents a theory of sound symbolism, arguing that the vowels HÉ, E, I, O, and U originally represented fundamental concepts like life, existence, power, light, and water.</summary>

Here we also see how man encompasses, under a single name, objects that are very different in themselves but linked by their usage; consequently, man was obliged to establish in speech a close union between these things, reflecting how they present themselves to him all at once, and how he perceives them with a single glance. ## CHAPTER VIII.

### *Values assigned to simple or primitive Intonations.*

Ideas, being of an absolutely different nature than sensations, could not be depicted by the same signs; and just as sounds depicted sensations, **intonations** <note>In this context, the author uses "intonations" to refer to the intellectual or abstract inflection of a sound, distinguishing it from the raw physical "sound" associated with basic feelings.</note> depicted ideas: it would not even be difficult to show that between sounds and intonations, the same differences reign as between sensations and ideas: thus Nature, which endowed animals with sensations and not ideas, gave them sounds and refused them intonations.

But not every kind of idea can be depicted by just any intonation: that would suppose that ideas have nothing to distinguish them, and that intonations unit-

<meta>catchword: unit-</meta>

<summary>The author transitions from discussing physical sensations to the expression of abstract ideas, arguing that while animals share "sounds" with humans for feelings, "intonation" is a uniquely human capacity for expressing thought.</summary>
<keywords>intonation, sensations, ideas, nature, animal vs human, linguistics, primitive language</keywords>

-ed <note>original: "sent", completing the word "unissent" (unite) from the previous page's catchword "unit-".</note> all the same properties and to the same degree: two equally absurd suppositions.

What, then, did men do regarding language? They matched it to their ideas.

Pleasant ideas were depicted by pleasant intonations; rapid ideas by rapid intonations; slow ones by slow ones; those whose qualities were contrary to these were depicted by intonations that contrasted with them. Such was the first impulse that formed Languages, from which the first words were born, which then diversified infinitely by combining with one another.

The <term>LABIAL</term> <note>From the Latin *labia* (lips); refers to sounds like P, B, or M produced primarily by the lips.</note> touch, the easiest to employ, the softest, the most graceful, served to designate the first Beings that man knows: those who surround him and to whom he owes everything, those whom he loves by preference. The author notes that these French words are dominated by labial sounds, which he believes are the most natural for a child to articulate first.</note>

the mouth & all the effects of the mouth, such as drinking, eating, speaking, breathing, & that in all Languages, because all have been drawn from Nature.

Thus this single touch <note>The author uses "touch" (French: *touche*) to refer to what modern linguists call a "point of articulation"—the specific place in the mouth where a sound is formed.</note> became the source of a prodigious quantity of words, & is their etymological cause.

The **DENTAL** touch differs entirely from that one. As the teeth are as firm as the lips are mobile & flexible, the intonations that come from them are as strong, as resonant, as loud as the labial intonations are soft & light. The tongue, which first presses against the teeth and then moves away from them abruptly & with force, obliges the mouth to open as much as possible, & to leave a free field for the explosion of air which occurs in this way with the greatest force.

The intonations resulting from this thus naturally become the depiction of everything that is resonant & loud; from there, a multitude of primitive words <note>The author refers to "primitive words" as the original, root sounds of human language before they were modified by centuries of usage.</note> and drawn from

Nature. It is by this touch that one *thunders* <note>original: *tonne*</note>, that one resounds, that one astonishes, that one gives the *tone*; through it, one designates loud instruments: *drums*, *kettledrums*, *dulcimers*, *trumpets*; from this, the words *tympanum*, *tintinnabulation* <note>original: *tintin*</note>, *tact*, *touch*, *intonation*, etc. By it, one encourages dogs at the hunt; the voice resounds in the distance, it pierces the immensity of the forests.

It is thus that Nature has provided for all the needs of man, and that he experiences her aid without study or effort; man follows his impressions without suspecting it. But if, when he comes to reflect upon the advantages he derives from them, he does not recognize that it is to her that he is indebted, or if he imagines that these observations are pure fantasies <note>original: *chimères*</note>, he is an ingrate who does not deserve the name of a sensitive and observant Being.

It is not surprising that from this distinctive property of the dental touch were born the names of vast and dominant objects: of masses piled in a *heap* <note>original: *tas*</note>, of *all* <note>original: *tout*</note>, of *so much* <note>original: *tant*</note>, of *domi-*

<meta>catchword: nant,</meta>
<vocab>Nature, touch, tone, drum, kettledrum, dulcimer, trumpet, intonation, dental touch, sensitive Being</vocab>

<summary>The author explains how dental sounds (T, D) naturally represent loud noises, musical instruments, and large quantities. He argues that these linguistic connections are a natural gift from "Nature" rather than a human invention.</summary>

The lingual intonation *l* designates gentle movements, objects whose progress is continuous and tranquil, and everything that is limpid and clear; whereas its forceful counterpart, the lingual intonation *r*, designates rough and strong movements, noisy objects, or those that move by leaps and jolts, and those that are steep; such are the words *wheel, rock, boulder, ravine, rapid, rough.* <note>original: *roue, roc, rocher, ravine, rapide, rude*</note>

One thus depicts, through these intonations, all objects in the form of canals, all hollow and excavated objects, and all objects that follow a long course like a channel; hence these words:

# CHAPTER IX.
## *On several other ways of forming words.*

---

THE relationship of sounds and intonations with
Nature was not sufficient to depict
the entirety of ideas; it was therefore necessary to resort
to other sources of words, just as simple,
just as natural, and in which man
remained always an imitator.

The first of these methods was to impose
names on animate or inanimate objects, by
imitating the cries of the former and the noises of all.
This is what we call *onomatopoeia* <term>onomatopoeia: the formation of a word from a sound associated with what is named</term> or *word for-
mation*. Such were the names of the *ox*,
the *raven*, the *rooster*, the *cicada*, etc. <note>original: *bœuf, corbeau, coq, cigale*</note> Such are
the words *tric-trac* <note>a board game similar to backgammon, named for the sound of the pieces</note>, *taffeta* <note>original: *taffetas*; a crisp silk fabric that makes a rustling sound</note>, *puff* <note>original: *bouffée*</note>.

The second method was to associate two or

--- Page 127 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of word formation techniques, focusing on the second and third methods: the combination of individual sounds into clusters and the joining of separate words into compounds.</meta>

->ORIGIN OF LANGUAGE<-

several vocal sounds <note>original: *intonations*</note> together, in order to express, through this union, ideas that these sounds were not sufficient to express alone. Thus L and R are preceded by almost all other sounds; we have words in *bl*, *cl*, *gl*, *fl*, *pl*, and in *br*, *cr*, *gr*, *fr*, *pr*, which share more or less in the nature of L and R, such as *to glide*, *fluid*, *flower*, *weeping*, *terror*, *grinding*, *cry*, and so on. <note>original: *glisser, fluide, fleur, pleur, effroi, grincement, cri*</note>

The union of s and t, or *st*, of which the first is a sibilant <term>sibilant: a hissing sound, like 's'</term>, and the second a dental <term>dental: a sound produced by the tongue against the upper teeth, like 't'</term>; so it is that an extremely mobile and rapid sound finds itself united to the most fixed of sounds. This union, I say, forms a multitude of words that all designate a permanent or *stable* Being. <note>The author is connecting the phonetic stability of the 't' sound with the concept of "stability."</note>

The third method was to unite two or more words into one, in order to express composite ideas: such are our words *today* <note>original: *aujourd'hui*; literally "to the day of this day," which the author counts as five components: *à le jour de hui*</note>, *now* <note>original: *maintenant*; literally "hand-holding" (*main tenant*)</note>, *privilege* <note>original: *passe-droit*; literally "pass-right"</note>, *overseas* <note>original: *outremer*</note>, *to reject* <note>original: *rejetter*</note>, *to undo* <note>original: *défaire*</note>, and *perfect* <note>original: *parfait*; literally "thoroughly done"</note>. One can establish as a principle that every word of two syllables is a compound word which reunites two ideas.

### 96 ###

different syllables to form only a single one.

The fourth and final method was to designate non-material Beings by the same words that already indicated material Beings. Thus, the word *spirit* <note>original: *esprit*</note>, which signified respiration—a material or sensory object—subsequently designated the faculty of thought, which is not a material or sensory being or faculty.

And one attributed to non-material beings the same qualities as physical beings. We speak of a *lively*, *fiery*, *impetuous*, or *stunted* MIND <note>original: *vif, ardent, impétueux, bouché*; the author compares these mental traits to the behavior of fire, wind, or a blocked passage</note>, as if it were a fire, the wind, or a canal. The heart was called *tender*, *hard*, or *fickle*, as if it were a plant or a butterfly.

It was not enough to have found the means to "paint" the ideas of physical and moral or intellectual objects; it was also necessary to find a way to express negative ideas—to paint objects that do not exist or are absent. In this, man did not fail himself. He even developed two methods instead of one.

Sometimes, he depicted the same object in a contrary sense; sometimes, he was satisfied with substituting a weak intonation <term>intonation: the rise and fall of the voice in speaking</term> for a strong intonation.

->**VOCABULARY**<-

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how language expresses negative ideas, following the previous page's introduction of the two methods (contrary sense and weak intonation). Here, the author provides specific examples from Greek, Latin, and French to illustrate how meanings were inverted.</meta>

->ORIGIN OF LANGUAGE.<-
### 97 ###

G

The letter "A", placed at the end of a noun, indicated the existence or possession of an object. "A", placed at the head of that same noun, indicated its non-existence or privation. This was especially the method of the Greeks <note>The author is referring to the "alpha privative," where the Greek prefix *a-* or *an-* negates the following word, such as *atheos* (without God)</note>.

"*In*", at the end of a noun, indicated existence, extent, or reality; at the head, it designated non-existence or privation; as in *useless* <note>original: *in-utile*</note>, *unjust* <note>original: *in-juste*</note>, *immaterial* <note>original: *im-matériel*</note>, and *formless* <note>original: *in-forme*</note>.

The strong intonation and vowel being dedicated to the positive object, the weak intonation and vowel were dedicated to the negative object—to privation. Thus, the Latin *icy-cold* <note>original: *gel-idus*</note>, which designates the quality of being cold or frozen, is merely the "weak" version of *hot* <note>original: *cal-idus* or *cal-dus*</note> which signifies the opposite. Our French words *frost* <note>original: *gelée*</note> and *heat* <note>original: *chaleur*</note> likewise come from the same root, and through opposition. It was necessary for there to be opposition between words, since there was opposition between ideas. This method, consequently, was very much in accordance with nature, and allowed radicals <term>radicals: the fundamental roots or base forms of words that carry the primary meaning</term> to serve all ideas without multiplying them <note>The author argues that language is efficient; instead of inventing a new word for "cold," early speakers simply modified the "strong" sound of the word for "hot"</note>.

->**VOCABULARY**<-
**Origin of language**, **etymology**, **Greeks**, **roots**, **radicals**, **Latin**

<summary>This page explains how early languages expressed negation and opposites through the use of prefixes like the Greek "a-" and Latin "in-," as well as through vowel shifts between "strong" and "weak" sounds.</summary>
<keywords>etymology, negation, alpha privative, vowel shifts, roots, radicals, Greek, Latin, linguistic history</keywords>

## CHAPTER ONE.

->Advantages of Writing.<-

Nothing is less durable than speech; it strikes the air and leaves no trace; and if it makes any impression on those who hear it, this impression is non-existent for those who are not enclosed within the small circle it travels. The fruits one gathers from it are therefore only the fruits of the moment: however, the more it was essential to human happiness, the more important it was to find the necessary means to extend its happy effects. How could one remember a multitude of useful and necessary inventions, if one could not fix one's ideas outside of oneself, and trace them in a manner which

It is therefore with good reason that the Egyptians made the invention of Agriculture, of Astronomy, and of Writing advance at an equal pace. ## CHAPTER IV.

->*Writing is nothing but an imitation.*<-

**MAN** invents nothing; but he imitates and he perfects: it is from these faculties that Writing was born. One wished to transmit an idea, but this idea depicted an object; one had only, then, to paint this object, to trace its figure, and the idea was transmitted; thus, one wrote by the same means by which one spoke. Writing, like Language, was founded on imitation: <term>Nature provided all the materials<note>The original French phrase "la Nature en fit tous les frais" suggests that Nature bore the cost or supplied everything necessary for this development.</note></term>. Just as a Traveler, having arrived in Lands where he is ignorant of the Language, is reduced to painting for the eyes what he would in vain try to make known through sounds.

<vocab>Egyptians, Agriculture, Astronomy, Writing, Mercury, Language, imitation, Nature, Traveler, Lands</vocab>

<summary>The author links the Egyptian origins of writing to agriculture and astronomy, characterizing all three as divine gifts. He argues that writing is not a "new" invention but a natural imitation of physical objects, comparing early writing to a traveler using drawings to communicate in a foreign land.</summary>
<keywords>Egypt, Mercury, Thoth, origin of writing, mimesis, imitation, communication, agriculture, nature</keywords>

Thus, a *radiant circle* depicted the Sun; a *crescent*, the Moon; a *partitioned square*, an enclosure or a garden; *wavy lines*, water; a *wing*, speed and the winds; an eye, sight; a hand, strength and power.

Writing thus formed a tapestry that could not be mistaken, and such were the first monuments that human industry raised in this genre. But these tapestries, these monuments, were raised only for instruction and for public happiness; they presented the most venerable lessons, the works of Legislators and Sages—precious repositories of everything relating to Religion, Morality, Agriculture, and Government.

From this comes the name **HIEROGLYPHS** or <term>*Sacred Mysteries*<note>original: "Mystères sacrés". The author provides a contemporary interpretation of the etymology; linguistically, "hieroglyph" comes from the Greek *hieros* (sacred) and *glyphein* (to carve).</note></term>, given to these characters; and the name *Hieroglyphic Writing* that this script bears, because of the excellence of the things it depicted.

Such was also the origin of Chinese Writing, which appears so singular and unlike any other: at first <term>hieroglyphic<note>In this context, the author uses "hieroglyphic" to mean any system of pictographic writing where symbols represent objects or ideas rather than sounds.</note></term>, or a simple imitation of objects, its strokes gradually became so tightened and distorted that they no longer seem like anything but the result of caprice, and it is difficult to convince oneself that they were imitative in their origin.

## CHAPTER V.

### *On Alphabetic Writing.*

Alphabetic writing has always been regarded as having originated in Egypt, and as having been invented to take the place of <term>hieroglyphic writing<note>In the 18th-century context of this manuscript, "hieroglyphics" refers broadly to any system of writing using pictures to represent ideas or objects, rather than just the specific script of ancient Egypt.</note></term>: but these two suppositions did not prevent the invention of alphabetic writing from remaining an inexplicable problem.

Since all writing is "painting," or hieroglyphic, it necessarily follows that alphabetic writing is itself a collection of hieroglyphic characters. One will have no trouble being convinced of this when examining the figures that the Alphabet offered at its birth, and the relationships of their objects with the <term>organ<note>The author is referring to the speech organs—the mouth, tongue, and throat—suggesting that the shape of letters originally mimicked the physical way the sound was produced.</note></term> that produces the sound noted by each of these figures, and with the value of the words they form. The perfect correspondence that reigns between all...

## CHAPTER VI.

### *Concerning the objects depicted by the primitive Alphabet.*

The Figures on this Plate II that correspond to the vowels are a man and an ox's head, the divisions of a cultivated plot of land, a human face, a hand, an eye, and an ear. These are all objects taken from Nature and employed by Alphabetic Writing.

These hieroglyphs <note>The author uses the term "hieroglyphs" here to describe the original pictorial signs that he believes eventually evolved into the simplified letters of the alphabet.</note> therefore possessed a necessary value, which never depended on chance or whim.

The first designates Man, the Master, the Owner of the Earth.

The second, the Ox, that Companion of Man in the labors by which he makes the Earth fruitful.

The third, this very Earth made fertile by the care of its Owner—the fields which are the sources of life.

The fourth, the Head of Man,

# CHAPTER VII.

->*Objects represented by the characters corresponding to consonants.*<-

If we rediscover in hieroglyphic writing those characters that depict sounds <note>In this context, the author likely refers to vowels as 'sounds' or 'voices.'</note>, we likewise find characters corresponding to intonations <note>The author uses 'intonations' to refer to the articulation of consonants, distinguishing them from the pure sounds of vowels.</note>. The writing of the most remote ages presents our consonants with the same value they possess in our spoken languages, and with the same form as in our alphabets. This is easily verified by examining the sequence in our Plate II.

There, one sees that *P* originally represented the shape of an open mouth viewed in profile; one cannot fail to recognize the two lips and the upper teeth: this shape is scarcely changed in the Hebrew alphabet <note>The Hebrew letter 'Pe' indeed means 'mouth.'</note>. It is clearly recognizable in the Greek and Etruscan alphabets, with the sole difference being that it has taken on a per-

<meta>This page continues the specific analysis of the letter P, detailing its transition from a profile view of the mouth to its modern form. It then moves into a discussion of the letter B as a symbol for containment, and introduces the labio-nasal consonants M and N.</meta>

pendicularly; and from there, our *P*, by turning this letter—as the Greeks did—from right to left, and by rounding the stroke that corresponds to the upper teeth. But this letter is a true hieroglyph <note>A symbol that represents an object directly rather than just a sound.</note>, since it depicts the mouth and signifies: 1st, the mouth itself in all these ancient languages; 2nd, *to speak*, which is the proper function of this organ, whether one pronounces this letter as *P*, or aspirates it <note>Adding a breathy "h" sound to the consonant.</note> as *Ph* or *F*. Indeed, *PHÉ* or *PE* <note>The Hebrew letter 'Pe' (פ).</note> means "the mouth" in Hebrew; *FA* <note>original: "FA"; likely referring to the Latin root *fari* (to speak) or the Greek *phánai* (to say).</note> means "to speak" in Greek and Latin, as does *E-po* <note>original: "E-po"; referring to the Greek *εἴπω* (eipō), meaning "I say."</note> in the Greek language.

*B*, also being an intonation <note>A term the author uses to describe the physical articulation of a consonant.</note> of the lips, served to designate the mouth from another perspective—as having the property of containing or enclosing. From this comes its shape, that of a box; and its value, *b* or *beth* <note>The Hebrew letter 'Beth' (ב), which means "house."</note> meaning a box, a house, an enclosure, or anything that contains.

Next come the two labio-nasals <note>Sounds produced using both the lips and the nasal cavity.</note> *M* and *N*. As intonations of the same organ, they were necessarily used to designate two corresponding ideas, whether by their meaning or by their shape.

*M* designates in all languages the idea of *Mother*, of *maternity*, of a productive and fruitful Being. *N* designates the idea of *Son*, of a Being produced or *born* <note>The author is connecting the letter N to the French word "né" (born).</note>, the idea of fruit, of everything that is tender and new.

*M* was therefore represented in hieroglyphic character <note>In this context, a "hieroglyphic character" refers to a symbolic picture that acts as a letter.</note> under the figure of a tree, of a plant, of a person who raises their arms to carry their infant, or to gather fruit: and for the same reason, *N* was represented under the figure of a fruit still attached to the Being to which it owes its birth.

Just as the Father, Head of the Family, Master, was depicted at the head of the hieroglyphic figures, so his Companion, and their Son, their heir, the pledge of their love, the fruit of their care, of their labors, the continuer of their projects, also formed part of these figures. And did they not necessarily enter into Writing, as they did into Language? What more interesting objects could one present there, from whatever point of view they were considered, as members of the same family, as Cul- <note>The text cuts off here, likely intending to say "Cultivateurs" (Cultivators) or "Culte" (Worship).</note>

# ORIGIN OF LANGUAGE.

*M* designates in all languages the idea of *Mother*, of *maternity*, of a productive and fruitful Being. *N* designates the idea of *Son*, of a Being produced or *born*, the idea of fruit, of everything that is tender and new.

Thus, *M* was represented as a hieroglyphic character <term>hieroglyphic character: a written symbol that visually depicts an object to represent an idea</term> in the form of a tree, a plant, or a person raising their arms to carry their infant or to pluck fruit; and for the same reason, *N* was represented in the form of a fruit still attached to the Being to whom it owes its birth.

Just as the Father—Head of the Family and Master—was depicted at the head of the hieroglyphic figures, so too his Companion and their *Son*—their heir, the token of their love, the fruit of their care and labor, the continuator of their projects—likewise formed part of these figures. And did they not necessarily enter into Writing, as they did into Language? What objects more interesting could be presented there, from whatever point of view one regarded them, as members of the same family, as Cul- <note>The word is cut off at the end of the page; it likely intended to say "Cultivateurs" (Cultivators) or "Culte" (Cult/Worship).</note>

<margin>Language, hieroglyphs, symbolism, etymology, Mother, Son, Family</margin>

<summary>This page explores the symbolic origins of the letters M and N, associating M with the mother and productivity, and N with the son and the result of birth. The author argues that the core family unit—father, mother, and child—formed the foundational imagery for early writing systems.</summary>
<keywords>etymology, M, N, Mother, Son, hieroglyphs, family unit, symbolic writing, linguistics</keywords>

# NATURAL HISTORY OF SPEECH.

...tivators of a land that owed everything to them, as favorites of Nature? In a figurative and allegorical sense, they designated Osiris, Isis, and Horus <note>original: "Orus"</note>—fertilizing Nature, fertilized Nature, and the Beings born from this fertility; in other words, Intelligence, Matter, and the Universe, the result of the power of Intelligence over Matter.

The Ladies of the East use the *Camel* as a mount, and this animal is distinguished by its long neck and by the advantage of making long journeys in a short time. Its head and long neck thus became the emblem of every channel, every throat, everything that has the shape of a neck, and everything that runs and passes. And from this comes the shape of the G. <note>The author refers to the Semitic letter "Gimel," which historically shares a root with the word for camel.</note>

The C, which has the same shape and value as the primitive K but is turned from right to left, depicts the *hollow* of the hand; it is thus the speaking hieroglyph <term>hieroglyph: a symbol that visually represents the object or idea it names</term> for everything that is hollow.

The guttural Q still retains its shape

# ORIGIN OF LANGUAGE.

...ancient, especially in minuscule handwriting, *q*. It represents a cleaver, a small axe, or anything used for cutting. And languages are filled with words written with a *q*, or in which *c* has taken its place, which designate any kind of division.

The whistling sound *S* is represented by a *saw*, the name of which is a true onomatopoeia <note>onomatopoeia: a word that phonetically imitates the sound it describes</note>, a name borrowed from the sound of the saw itself. This sound is also represented by the lower jaw, because it designates everything used for grinding or chewing—everything that performs the function of teeth—at least among those peoples who substitute *S* for *D*.

A *Roof* <note>original French: "Toit"</note> was the representation of the letter *T*, which designates *shelter, cover, a roof*; from this came the Latin *tego* <note>original Latin: "tego", meaning to cover or protect</note>, to cover or defend; and the words *pro-tect, pro-tection, Archi-tect*.

The *Cross*, another kind of primitive *T*, was the representation of *perfection*, of *ten*, the perfect number; of everything great and elevated, like the image of two hands crossed which equal ten, or like the image of a Man with arms outstretched to embrace all.

### NATURAL HISTORY OF SPEECH.

The *D* has the shape of a triangle with a door in the middle. It represents the entrance to a tent, the outside of a house. This is the meaning of this letter in the Hebrew Alphabet <note>The Hebrew letter 'Dalet' literally means 'door.'</note> and in Chinese Writing.

To depict angles, or objects that are sharp, pointed, steep, or protruding—the nose, rocks, etc.—one only had to draw the nose; and this became the letter *R*, the shape of all physical objects designated by words containing *R*.

Finally, the letter *L* originally had the shape of a wing, or of a folded arm used like wings to run faster: this is what the sound itself designates, as we have already seen. From this come the names for wing, flank, fluid, etc., in Latin *ala* <note>original: "ala", meaning wing</note>, *latus* <note>original: "latus", meaning side or flank</note>, *fluo* <note>original: "fluo", meaning to flow</note>, etc.

Thus was Writing born; thus were all ideas depicted. In this way, the eye perceived everything the ear could hear; and what speech could not make known was transmitted to the most distant places and the most remote generations.
