## NATURAL HISTORY OF SPEECH

The *D* has the shape of a triangle with a door in the middle. It represents the entrance to a tent, the outside of the house. This is what this letter signifies in the Hebrew Alphabet <note>The Hebrew letter Daleth (ד) literally means "door."</note> and in Chinese Writing.

To depict angles, or objects that are sharp, pointed, steep, or protruding—such as the nose, rocks, etc.—one only had to draw the nose; and this became the letter *R*, the figure for all physical objects designated by words beginning with *R*. <note>The author suggests a visual link between the profile of a nose and the shape of the letter R to explain its phonetic use.</note>

Finally, the letter *L* originally had the shape of a *wing*, or an arm folded back and used like wings to run better: this is what the sound itself denotes, as we have already seen. From this come the names for *wing*, *side*, *fluid*, etc.; in Latin: wing <note>original: *ala*</note>, side <note>original: *latus*</note>, I flow <note>original: *fluo*</note>, etc.

Thus Writing was born, and thus all ideas were depicted: in this way, the eye perceived everything that the ear could hear; and one transmitted to the most distant places and the furthest generations that which speech alone could not make known to them.

# CHAPTER VIII.

## *The Number of Simple Characters contained in Alphabetic Writing.*

In writing, the number of simple characters <term>simple characters: the fundamental, irreducible signs or "radicals" of a writing system</term> is always very limited, because the number of simple ideas is itself very limited. The Chinese, who have carried the number of simple characters the furthest, count only 214 <note>This refers to the "radicals" or "keys" used in Chinese lexicography to categorize characters</note>; and one can even reduce these to almost a third, because within that number, there are several which are composite, and several which are merely subdivisions. We have only 23 characters in our Alphabet <note>In the 18th century, it was common to count 23 letters, often treating I/J and U/V as variations of the same characters</note>: the Greeks had no more; for their Alphabet combines several that are merely composites; some even are specific to their pronunciation.

The analysis of speech thus reduces the primitive alphabetic characters to far fewer than 23; and History with the Monu-

## CHAPTER VIII.

### *Number of Simple Characters used in Alphabetic Writing.*

In Writing, the number of Simple Characters is always very limited, because the number of simple ideas is itself very limited. The Chinese <note>The author refers to the 214 "radicals" or "keys" used to categorize Chinese characters, popularized in the West via the Kangxi Dictionary (1716).</note>, who have carried the number of Simple Characters the furthest, count only 214; and one can even reduce them to almost a third, because in this number, there are several that are compound, and several that are only subdivisions. It is certain that the primitive Greek Alphabet had only 16 Letters; the Latins and the Etruscans originally had no more. The Bastulo-Turdetani <note>Referring to the "Bastule" script used by ancient peoples in the southern Iberian Peninsula (modern-day Spain).</note> alphabet in Spain, that of the Northern Peoples or Runic, the Irish, etc., do not offer a greater number.

It appears that the number of 22 among the Hebrews designated the 22 Patriarchs from whom they descended, and who end with Jacob, Father of their 12 Tribal Chiefs.

The number of 16 would therefore represent the 16 Patriarchal Generations which had just been completed at the moment of the dispersion of the peoples <note>A reference to the biblical narrative of the Tower of Babel and the subsequent scattering of humanity.</note>.

# ORIGIN OF LANGUAGE.

[alpha]betic <note>original: "tique", completing "alphabétique" from the preceding page.</note> was to give to each of its Elements the name of the sound or intonation that it represented; to call *A*, the figure that depicted an *A*; *B*, the figure that depicted a *B*, etc. From then on, Writing marched in step with speech, and was perfectly corresponding to it. One could pronounce Writing and paint all the portions of each word; tracing Writing upon speech with the greatest precision.

This advantage is lacking for the Chinese; it would however be easy for them to acquire it: thus our spoken and written Languages prevail greatly over theirs in brevity and simplicity; and they are, by that very fact, infinitely easier to learn.

# CHAPTER IX.

### *On the places where Alphabetic Writing was born.*

It has been believed that Alphabetic Writing had been invented by the Egyptians, weary of their hieroglyphs: but what has been said on this matter is nothing but a fiction <note>original: "Roman," literally "Novel," here used to mean a fabricated or mythic history</note>, contrary to all historical tradition & to all experience; no People having renounced their customs to such an extent, & abandoned one Alphabet for another: the Chinese have not been able to resolve to sacrifice their writing to the alphabetic one in use among their Conquerors: can we ourselves resolve to sacrifice even a single one of our letters, much less change or reform our Alphabet?

Hieroglyphic Writing was in use in the Orient, in China, & in Egypt, when the Chaldeans <note>An ancient Semitic-speaking people of southern Mesopotamia</note>, associating speech with Writing, developed an Alphabet that could be pronounced: such was the origin of Alphabetic Writing, born from the hieroglyphic, as soon

<meta>catchword: as soon</meta>

<vocab>Alphabetic Writing, Egyptians, hieroglyphs, Chinese, Conquerors, Chaldeans, hieroglyphic, Orient, Egypt</vocab>

<summary>In this chapter, the author disputes the theory that the Egyptians invented the alphabet to replace hieroglyphs, arguing instead that writing systems are culturally ingrained and rarely abandoned. He proposes that alphabetic writing originated with the Chaldeans, who adapted earlier hieroglyphic forms into a pronounceable phonetic system.</summary>

<keywords>origin of the alphabet, hieroglyphs, Egyptology, Chaldeans, linguistic history, Chinese writing, cultural preservation, phonetic evolution</keywords>

This page traces the transmission of the alphabet from the Chaldeans to the Phoenicians and eventually to Europe, citing classical Roman authorities like Lucan and Pliny the Elder.</meta>

<sig>I</sig>

as soon as they sought to pronounce it. It was soon communicated to the Phoenicians, who carried it throughout all of Europe along with their Arts, so that they came to be regarded as its Inventors. Thus, **LUCAN** <note>Marcus Annaeus Lucanus (39–65 AD), a Roman poet</note> gives them all the credit in his *Pharsalia* <note>An epic poem detailing the civil war between Julius Caesar and Pompey</note>:

> "The Phoenicians, he says, if fame is to be believed, were the first who dared to fix speech by material figures. Memphis <note>The ancient capital of Egypt</note> did not yet know how to compose books with the plants that grow on the banks of its river <note>A reference to papyrus, which grows along the Nile</note>; its magical Languages <note>original: "Langues magiques," referring to the then-undeciphered and "mysterious" Egyptian hieroglyphs</note> were preserved on marble only by figures of birds or animals."

Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman naturalist and historian</note> had seen more clearly when he assured us that writing had been in use among the Assyrians from time immemorial.

The Egyptians also made use of Alphabetic Writing; but they did so as imitators, and not as inventors.

Let us observe that when the Orientals <note>Used here to refer to the peoples of the Near East, specifically Semitic-speaking groups</note> expanded the Alphabet from 16 letters to 22, they likewise had recourse to hieroglyphic characters.

<summary>The author argues that while the Phoenicians are often credited with inventing the alphabet and spreading it to Europe, they actually adopted it from earlier sources. He cites classical authors to suggest that writing existed among the Assyrians long before the Egyptians adapted it for their own use.</summary>

## CHAPTER X.

->*The fate of the primitive Alphabet in a part*<-
->*of Europe.*<-

The Alphabet of 16 letters passed into Europe at an early date; it was known in Greece under the name of *Pelasgian* letters <note>Referring to the Pelasgians, the name used by ancient Greek writers to describe the indigenous populations that preceded the Hellenes in Greece</note>, after the name of the first inhabitants of Greece. It later gave way to the Alphabet of 22 letters, which was carried to Europe, so it is said, by Cadmus <note>In Greek mythology, Cadmus was a Phoenician prince credited with introducing the alphabet to the Greeks</note> who was searching for his sister Europa; this is merely an allegorical tale upon which no historical certainty can be built. -ing was also known very early by the Etruscans, and long before the Romans cultivated letters.

From the Greeks and the Romans, letters passed to the other Peoples of Europe, most of whom increased the number of letters in the Alphabet. The Peoples of the North <note>Likely referring to Germanic and Scandinavian tribes who used Runic alphabets</note> however stuck to the Alphabet of 16 letters, and the Irish still have only 17.

The letters of the Alphabet initially went from right to left, because they began writing in that direction. The Greeks soon found that it was more pleasant, instead of always writing from right to left, to write alternately from right to left and from left to right, precisely as oxen plow; this manner of writing was called *boustrophedon* <term>from the Greek roots *bous* (ox) and *strephein* (to turn), describing text that runs back and forth like a plow in a field</term>; there still exist Greek inscriptions engraved in this style: it is even believed that it was not unknown to the Hebrews: it is found at least on one of their medals, which had been inexplicable until it was read in this manner.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "boustrophedon" writing style introduced on the previous page, transitioning into how most nations eventually shifted to writing from left to right and how this affected the physical shape of the letters.</meta>

<header>NATURAL HISTORY OF SPEECH.</header>

...as can be seen in our Volume on the *Origin of Language and Writing*, where several of these *boustrophedon* <term>from the Greek for "ox-turning," referring to text that alternates direction like a plow in a field</term> inscriptions will also be found.

Most Nations subsequently found that it was easier and more advantageous to write from left to right than from right to left, especially once they became accustomed to cursive writing <note>original: *écriture courante*; referring to a flowing script where the pen remains on the page, rather than individual carved letters</note>: such were the peoples of Europe, and several peoples of Asia and Africa; the Hebrews and the Arabs are almost the only ones who have preserved the ancient manner <note>writing from right to left</note>.

But during this change, several ancient characters changed their shape; almost all were reversed from right to left; their strokes necessarily had to follow the movement of the hand: this is what primarily distinguishes the Latin, French, and ordinary Greek Alphabets from the primitive Greek Alphabet and the Hebrew Alphabet, which are otherwise similar in almost every respect, as we shall see.

**A** is the first letter of the Alphabet in Latin, in Greek, in Hebrew, etc., and its form has changed very little.

Let us add that the weak intonations designated large objects: *b*, the house <note>The Hebrew letter *Beth* literally means "house."</note>; *g* or *c*, the camel <note>The Hebrew letter *Gimel* is traditionally associated with the word for camel, *gamal*.</note>; *d*, the door of the house <note>The Hebrew letter *Daleth* means "door."</note>, etc.: so that it was necessary to place them together.

One likewise had to place the strong intonations together, because they designated parts of the human body: *o*, the eye <note>The letter *Ayin*, which means "eye."</note>; *p*, the mouth <note>The letter *Pe*, which means "mouth."</note>; *r*, the nose <note>The letter *Resh*, often associated with the "head."</note>; *s*, the teeth <note>The letter *Shin*, which means "tooth."</note>, etc.

It is not surprising, either, that some letters have often exchanged pronunciation and value with one another; that *T* and *Th* <note>The letters *Teth* and *Tav* in Hebrew, or *Tau* and *Theta* in Greek.</note> have swapped places; that *D* and *S* have exchanged values; that the same is true for *F* and *P*; that the French *X* has taken the place of the Greek *X* <note>The Greek letter Chi (χ), which in ancient times held a different phonetic value than the Latin/French X.</note>, even though it is pronounced differently, as this Greek letter is an aspirated *K*; because these letters have never differed from one another except by slight nuances in pronunciation.

This is because man is imitative, and he always aligns himself as closely as possible with his model.

## CHAPTER III.
### *On the qualities that the painting of Ideas must have,*
### *and which become the basis of Grammar.*

SO THAT the painting <note>original: *peinture*. This is a central metaphor in the text: language is seen as a visual "painting" or "depiction" of internal thoughts.</note> of our ideas produces the effects for which it is intended, it must resemble the idea itself as closely as possible, and take on its essential qualities.

An idea in itself is always clear and lively; it has the brilliance and speed of a lightning flash. The painting one makes of it must therefore possess the same qualities; it must be luminous, energetic, and prompt. Grammar will thus possess these same qualities, since this Art <note>Here, "Art" refers to a systematic field of knowledge or a technical skill governed by rules.</note> must depict ideas in the most exact manner.

<note>Key terms: Universal Grammar, Intellectual world, Mind, painting of Ideas, clarity, rapidity, energetic.</note>

Thus our phrases—the painting <note>original: *peinture*. As noted previously, the author views language as a visual representation or "portrait" of internal thoughts.</note> of our ideas—must offer the greatest clarity and contain nothing ambiguous; every part must be well-defined, sharp, and distinct.

This is not all. The idea of an object is painted in our mind all at once, in the blink of an eye; it would therefore be desirable if it could be rendered with the same speed. This is all the more necessary because humans, united in society and bound to one another, have a multitude of ideas to communicate; we are just as impatient to quickly understand what someone is trying to tell us as we are to express ourselves.

We will therefore make words follow one another with speed; but since that is still not enough, we will economize on the number of words. We will suppress all those that are not absolutely necessary for the clarity of Discourse <note>original: *Diſcours*. In this context, it refers to any formal or structured communication of thought.</note>—all those that can be supplied by the context of the whole. Often, we will even combine two or three words into one to move more quickly.

From this will arise singular ways of speaking—

### CHAPTER ONE.

->*That the Tableaus of ideas through Speech are composed of various Parts.*<-

It is the same with our ideas: they revolve around relations <term>rapports: the connections or logical links between different concepts or objects</term>; relations of objects to one another; relations of objects with the qualities they combine; relations with ourselves, etc. One might even say that all our knowledge <note>original: *connoissances*</note> consists of nothing but relations; in all things, we do nothing but compare objects with one another; through this, we learn to distinguish them from each other; and by constantly rising from comparison to comparison, from relation to relation, nothing escapes our research. From this comes that spirit of curiosity, without which we would know nothing, and which is merely a spirit of comparison; yet it is harmful or advantageous, depending on the objects to which we apply it.

Every relation presupposes various objects that contribute to forming it, so that the painting <note>original: *peinture*. These various parts will, however, be few in number, since ideas can be reduced to relations that are almost always of the same nature.

## CHAPTER II.

### *Distinctive characteristics of the Parts of Speech.*

It is no more difficult to distinguish the various Parts of an idea than to distinguish those of a Body <note>In this context, the author refers to a physical object or organism to illustrate how parts relate to a whole.</note>. One knows that one Part of a Body is not the same as another because one cannot affirm of one what is affirmed of the other; because they have different functions and places; because they produce diverse effects; and because without them, this body would not exist, or it would be defective. It is the same with the various species of words that enter into the painting of ideas <term>painting of ideas: the author's recurring metaphor for how language represents mental concepts</term>.

1º. Since they relate to different Parts of the idea and are each intended to fulfill a function proper to themselves, one cannot say of one what is said of another.

2º. They will have different functions.

3º. They will produce diverse effects.

4º. They will be indispensable.

<vocab>Parts of Speech, idea, Body, functions, painting of ideas, words</vocab>

<summary>The author introduces a systematic way to identify parts of speech by comparing them to the organs of a physical body. He argues that just as a body requires distinct parts with unique functions to be complete, a sentence requires specific categories of words to successfully "paint" a thought.</summary>
<keywords>parts of speech, linguistic functions, analogy, grammar, painting of ideas, logic, structure of thought</keywords>

--- Page 199 ---
<meta>This page applies the four criteria for distinguishing parts of speech established on the previous page to a specific example: "Cicero was eloquent."</meta>

-> UNIVERSAL GRAMMAR <-

In this phrase, **CICERO WAS ELOQUENT**, we see three words, each of which belongs to a different Part of Speech, because they each, in their own way, possess the distinctive characteristics of the Parts of Speech.

1º. One cannot say of one what is affirmed of the other: one is a Noun; the other two constitute entirely different Parts.

2º. They fulfill different functions; for one designates the subject of the Picture <note>The author uses the term "Picture" (French: *Tableau*) to describe a sentence, consistent with his theory that language "paints" mental concepts.</note>; another, a quality of this subject; the third, links them.

3º. They produce different effects, since one calls to mind the idea of such a man; the other, that of a man depicted with such a character.

4º. They are indispensable; for if one is suppressed, the Picture will no longer exist.

One has only to give a name to each of these Parts of Speech. This name will even always be derived from what essentially constitutes them, from the functions they fulfill.

<vocab>Universal Grammar, Cicero, Part of Speech, Noun, Picture, functions</vocab>

<summary>The author uses the sentence "Cicero was eloquent" to demonstrate how different words perform unique and vital functions—identifying the subject, describing its qualities, and linking them—to form a complete mental "picture." This logical breakdown serves as the basis for naming and classifying the parts of speech based on their utility.</summary>
<keywords>grammar, Cicero, parts of speech, noun, subject, quality, linguistic link, sentence structure, painting of ideas</keywords>

Any word that combines these four properties, and which does not fall into any of the Parts of Speech already recognized and determined, will form a new Part of Speech; or in other words, it will be necessary to admit as many different parts as there are types of words distinguished by these four Characteristics.

## FIRST CLASS.

->*Parts of Speech that change form,*<-
->*in order to help present the same relationship:*<-
->*and 1. of the first three.*<-

In order for the Picture <note>French: *Tableau*. In 18th-century linguistics, a sentence was often described as a "picture" or a "painting" that represents the structure of a mental thought.</note> of an idea to be complete, three words are necessarily required: there may be many more, but there cannot be fewer.

These three words will serve to designate:
One, the object, or the <term>SUBJECT</term> <gloss>the person or thing being discussed</gloss> of the idea.
The other, the <term>QUALITY</term> <gloss>the attribute or characteristic</gloss> that one notices there, and by which this subject becomes interesting.
The third, the <term>LINK</term> <note>French: *liaison*. This refers to the "copula" (typically the verb "to be"), which connects the subject to its quality.</note> that one perceives between these two words.

These three species of words are found in the Picture that we presented at the end of the preceding Chapter: *Cicero was eloquent*.

1. *Cicero* indicates the subject of the Picture.
2. *Eloquent* presents a quality, a manner of being of any man who excels in the art of speech.

NATURAL HISTORY OF SPEECH.

3rd. *Was*, shows the relationship that we perceive between Cicero and this quality: it completes the Picture <note>French: *Tableau*. The author uses this term to describe a sentence as a visual representation of a mental thought.</note>, by linking the various Parts that compose it.

Of these three Parts, the first is called a Noun, because it serves to name, to designate objects, the various Beings that exist in Nature.

The one placed third is called **Adjective**: a word formed from the Latin *adjectus* <note>original: "adjectus", meaning "added"</note>, which means *added*, because words of this species are added following the Noun, to state the quality that one perceives in the object that this Noun designates; or to put it better, because it adds to the Noun of the object the knowledge of its qualities.

The one placed between these two, and which is here the word *Was*, is called **Verb**, from the Latin word *Verbum* <note>original: "Verb-um"</note>, which means *Speech par excellence* <note>Literally translated from Latin as "The Word." The author argues the verb is the most vital element of a sentence.</note>, the word on which all the force and energy of the Picture turns, its entire harmony, its life in a sense, since it alone creates its soul, unites all its parts, and makes it so that they form a Whole.

It is to these three Parts, truly constitutive of Language, that all discourse & all knowledge must be reduced. ### *Fourth Part of Speech.*

There exist names that apply to all objects of the same species: such are the words *Man, Woman, King, Queen, Assembly, City*, etc. These words apply to all Beings who are *Men, Women, Kings*, etc.

Every time, therefore, that one wishes to apply them to a single object, to a single man, to a single woman, to a King, etc. & thus take them in an individual sense, it will be necessarily required to accompany them with a word that draws them out of this generality, which makes known among all the objects that this name

<note>The text here begins to describe the function of the Article, which serves to individualize or determine a general noun.</note>

->The Assembly was brilliant.<-
->THE King is generous.<-
->THIS Woman is beautiful.<-

which are as precise as these same paintings would be indefinite without them: these words, in effect, *Assembly was brilliant*, *King is generous*, *Woman is beautiful*, present no specific object and offer no fixed meaning.

These words THE, THIS, & every similar word, therefore constitute a new Part of Speech; for they have nothing in common with those we have just discussed.

They are called ARTICLES, from the Latin word for *joint* <note>original: *Articulus*</note>, a word that designates those *articulations* or *joints* by means of which one moves the various limbs of the body. These words are in

How great, for example, is the multitude of relationships offered by the idea of a **young Person**? It is these words that constitute this second Class of the Parts of Speech, whose distinguishing characteristic is to never change their form <note>These are "invariable" words, such as prepositions and conjunctions, which do not change based on gender, number, or tense.</note>, because, being destined to link several objects, they cannot belong to any one in particular.

### *First Part of Speech, of the second Class.*

The same action, the same state, the same quality are susceptible to an infinite number of nuances: for two People will not possess the same quality to the same degree: they will not perform the same action equally; some will display more skill, others more liveliness, or more intelligence, etc.

The words necessary to express these various nuances exist; such as, *well, superbly, much, little, poorly, etc.*

These are called ADVERBS <note>From the Latin *ad-verbum*, meaning "to the verb." The author explains that these words exist specifically to add detail or "shading" to the actions described by verbs.</note>, because they are designed to modify the qualities expressed by the Verbs they accompany.

SECOND PART. Objects are linked to one another by various relationships: hence, the necessity of having words that link these objects with their relationships: as in this phrase, *Caesar lost his life,* BY <note>original: *DE*</note> *the very hand of his friends*; where, "BY" shows the relationship that existed between the death of Caesar and the hand of his friends <note>In modern grammar, these linking words are known as prepositions. They act as the "connective tissue" that shows how one noun relates to another in terms of cause, place, or manner.</note>.

These are the words called *PREPOSITIONS*.

# CHAPTER IV.

## *TABLEAUS resulting from the various Parts of Speech.*

From these different Parts of Speech arise various Tableaus <note>The author uses "Tableaus" (Tableaux) to describe the mental "pictures" or structures formed when words are combined to express a complete thought or proposition.</note>, regarding their composition, the nature of the qualities they display, and the arrangement of the words.

### I. *Tableaus of ideas, considered relative to their composition.*

In this regard, the Tableaus of our ideas are simple, compound, and complex.

1°. They are SIMPLE, when they contain only a single object and a single quality: *The Sun is scorching; the weather is stormy.*

2°. They are COMPOUND, when they offer several objects or several qualities: *Alexander, Caesar, Attila, and Genghis Khan were the scourges of the Human Race.*

3°. They are COMPLEX, when some of their members are expressed by

### II. *Tableaus of ideas relative to the nature*
->*of the qualities they display.*<-

The qualities of a Being, especially of that
which forms the primary subject of the Tableau, can
denote either its manner of existing, or its
actions, or what it undergoes from
other beings. From this, there are three kinds of Tableaus:
the ENONCIATIVE, the ACTIVE, and the PASSIVE. <note>In modern linguistics, these categories correspond roughly to descriptive sentences using a copula (is), active voice sentences, and passive voice sentences.</note>

The first kind states a simple exis-

*round: man is rational.*

The second kind presents beings as
acting: *Columbus discovered the New World.*

The third kind presents beings as
being the object of an action, as undergo-
ing its effect: *The New World was discov-*
*ered by Columbus.*

The first of these Tableaus are formed
by Adjectives; the second by
Active Participles, and the third by
Passive Participles. <note>The author views verbs as essentially participles combined with the idea of existence, a common theory in 18th-century "universal grammar."</note>

NATURAL HISTORY OF SPEECH.

### III. *Tableaus of ideas relative to the assembly of words.*

With the aim of matching the speed of thought as closely as possible, we are not satisfied with using very short words or those of a single syllable, such as *I*, *he*, *eye*, *nose*, etc.; we also combine several words into a single syllable. We say, for example, *it is my book*, instead of saying, *this book is the book of me*. <note>The author is describing the linguistic efficiency of possessive pronouns, which compress a relationship of ownership into a single word.</note>

Secondly, we omit every word that is not necessary for the understanding of the Tableau, and which can be supplied by the context as a whole: thus, we say *the rich*, instead of saying *rich people*, etc. <note>In linguistics, this is known as "substantivation," where an adjective functions as a noun.</note>

From this result words and phrases that are called *elliptical*, <term>ellipsis: the omission from speech or writing of a word or words that are superfluous or able to be understood from contextual clues.</term> that is to say, which contain ellipses or omissions. *My* is an elliptical word, taking the place of the three words *the . . . of me*. *The rich*, etc., form an elliptical phrase, since it designates *rich people*.

<vocab>grammar, ellipses, monosyllables, syntax, brevity, possessive pronouns, substantivized adjectives</vocab>

<summary>This page explores how language uses brevity and "ellipses" to keep pace with the speed of thought, specifically highlighting how single words like "my" or phrases like "the rich" stand in for longer, more complex constructions.</summary>
<keywords>Universal Grammar, ellipsis, linguistic economy, possessive pronouns, syntax, brevity, Tableaus of ideas</keywords>

## CHAPTER ONE.
### *OF THE NOUN.*

All our Discourse revolves around some object, around one of those objects contained within the vast expanse of the Universe. The NOUN, that Part of Speech <term>Part of Speech: a category of words that have similar grammatical properties, such as nouns, verbs, or adjectives.</term> which designates existing beings, or those supposed to exist, will therefore take its place at the head of the Parts of Speech: for it is not caprice that decides their rank and their preeminence; they hold these advantages from Nature herself, when she fixed their number. <note>In the 18th-century view, grammar was seen as a reflection of the natural laws of human thought rather than an arbitrary set of rules.</note>

#### §. 1. *Utility of Nouns.*

It is through nouns <note>original: "noms"</note> that we designate all beings that exist: we make them known to 

<summary>This page introduces the second part of the treatise, focusing on the "Noun" as the most essential part of speech because it names the objects and beings that make up the universe.</summary>

->NATURAL HISTORY OF SPEECH.<-

at that moment by this means, as if they were placed before one's eyes. Thus, in the most isolated retreat, in the deepest night, we can pass in review the universality of beings; represent to ourselves our parents, our friends, everything we hold most dear, everything that has struck us, everything that can instruct or amuse us; and by pronouncing their name, we can reason about them with our peers. <note>The author argues that language serves as a mental filing system, allowing us to think and talk about things that are not physically present.</note>

We thus keep a register through Nouns <note>original: "Noms." In French, the word "nom" serves for both the grammatical "noun" and the general "name."</note> of everything that is, and of everything we know; even of that which we have never seen, but which has been named for us, and which has been made known to us through their relationships with objects already known to us.

Let us not then be surprised that man, who speaks of everything, who studies everything, who keeps note of everything, has given Names to everything that exists: to his body and to its various parts, to his soul, to his faculties, to that prodigious multitude of beings that cover the earth, or which are hidden in its womb; <note>The "womb" of the earth refers to its interior or depths, specifically minerals and geological features.</note>

### §. 2. *Of the different species of Nouns.*

Just as we say, *Sun*, *Moon*, *Sky*, *Earth*, words by which we designate existing objects; we likewise say *Man*, *Plant*, *River*, words which are not the name of any being in particular, but which present to us all those who are of the same nature <note>The author is distinguishing between proper nouns, which name unique entities, and common nouns, which categorize groups of things sharing the same characteristics.</note>. We say in the same way *whiteness*, *height*, *roundness*, *goodness*, *friendship*, designating thereby, not beings, but the qualities of the body or of the soul, considered as the object of our ideas, as the being or the thing with which we occupy ourselves. We likewise consider *actions* as objects of our ideas: hence these words *action*, *offer*, *walk* <note>original: "marche." This refers to the act of walking or a gait, transformed into a noun.</note>: an admirable invention which gives an extreme facility to make speech faster, more energetic, and more useful.

In this sentence, *France is a Kingdom of a vast extent*, we see the first three types of nouns.

**FRANCE**, is the name of an individual object, of a Country.

<vocab>Nouns, Sun, Moon, Man, Plant, whiteness, friendship, actions, France, Kingdom</vocab>

<summary>The author classifies nouns into distinct categories: those naming unique individual objects, those naming general classes of beings, and those naming abstract qualities or actions.</summary>
<keywords>grammar, nouns, classification of speech, abstract concepts, proper names, common nouns, linguistics</keywords>

--- Page 219 ---
<meta>This page concludes the definitions of the three types of nouns introduced on the previous page—Proper, Appellative, and Abstract—and introduces a fourth category related to actions.</meta>

<page-num>173</page-num>
<header>UNIVERSAL GRAMMAR</header>

**KINGDOM** is the name of all Countries which are governed, like France, by a single Leader.

**EXTENT**, is the name of a quality considered as if it had an existence of its own, separate from that of the beings in which it is found. <note>In 18th-century linguistics, "extent" or "extension" refers to the physical space an object occupies, treated here as a noun in its own right.</note>

Of these three species of Nouns, the first is called **PROPER OR INDIVIDUAL NAME**, because it belongs specifically to the one who bears it.

The second, **APPELLATIVE**, because it serves to give a common *name* <note>original: "appellation." This is what modern grammar refers to as a "common noun," like "tree" or "river."</note> to all beings of the same species.

The third, **ABSTRACT**, because it is given to one of the states under which any object can be viewed, as if this state were a real being, by setting aside the object itself and its other qualities, from which one makes an *abstraction*, or which one puts aside, to occupy oneself only with this single quality (1).

---

(1) One may call those of the fourth species, *Active* nouns, because they relate to actions; or *Verbal* nouns, because they designate actions just as Verbs do. <note>These are nouns like "the walk," "the song," or "the refusal," which describe an act rather than a person or thing.</note>

<summary>This page defines and classifies nouns into four categories: proper names for individuals, appellative names for species, abstract names for qualities, and verbal nouns for actions.</summary>
<keywords>grammar, noun classification, abstract nouns, proper nouns, linguistics, terminology, 18th-century education</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the four classes of nouns defined on page 173—Proper, Appellative, Abstract, and Active/Verbal—and explores the etymological roots of the word "Noun" itself across various language families, linking it to the concept of knowledge.</meta>

<page-num>174</page-num>
<header>NATURAL HISTORY OF SPEECH.</header>

The first of these Nouns depicts an individual, in that which constitutes them and which is found only in them. <note>This refers to Proper Nouns, like "Socrates" or "Paris."</note>

The second of these Nouns depicts it, on the contrary, according to the qualities it shares with all beings of its species. <note>This refers to Appellative or Common Nouns, like "man" or "city."</note>

The third depicts it as if it were composed of but a single trait, a single quality. <note>This refers to Abstract Nouns, such as "wisdom" or "whiteness."</note>

The fourth depicts its active state. <note>This refers to Verbal Nouns, which describe an action as if it were a thing, such as "the race" or "the song."</note>

### §. 3. *Etymology of the word* NOUN.

This word is common to a great number of Languages: to Latin, which pronounces it *Name* <note>original: "Nom-en"</note>; to Greek, which made from it the word *Name* <note>original: "o-NOM-a"</note>; to the Northern Languages and to Persian, which pronounce it *Nam* and *Name*; to the Indians <note>Likely referring to Sanskrit or Hindustani.</note> who make it *Naom*. It comes from the primitive root *NO*, which signifies *knowledge* or *science*, and which has provided words to all Languages.

## ARTICLE II.

### 1°. *On Genders.*

Genders are the different endings that the same word takes, according to whether it relates to Man or to Woman. That which relates to Man is called MASCULINE, and we call FEMININE that which relates to persons of the feminine sex. Thus the word *le* <note>original: "le" (the masculine definite article in French)</note> is Masculine, and the word *la* <note>original: "la" (the feminine definite article in French)</note> is of the Feminine Gender. Some Languages even have a third Gender, which one...

### 2. On Numbers.

Nouns receive yet another kind of ending, according to whether they designate a single object of the same kind, or whether they designate several. Thus one says *House* <note>original: "Maison"</note> when it is a question of only one, and *Houses* <note>original: "Maisons"</note> when it concerns several. It is this property of Nouns that is called NUMBERS. Two kinds of Numbers are distinguished in most Languages.

The SINGULAR, which designates only one...

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument that all words—even abstract adjectives—originate from concrete primitive nouns. Having previously discussed trade and movement, the text now focuses on the concept of size and the specific root "Ram."</meta>

<header>NATURAL HISTORY OF SPEECH.</header>

*Great* <note>original French: "Grand"</note>, in Latin *grandis*, is an adjective formed from the primitive root *Ram*, which signifies height or elevation, and which was preceded by the letter *g*, following the pattern of words beginning with *r* and *l*. It is from this same root that *Ramus* <note>Latin: "branch"</note> emerged—meaning a branch or a bough—and, by synecdoche <term>synecdoche: a figure of speech where a part is used to represent the whole, or vice versa</term>, an *oar* <note>original: "rame"</note> for rowing, because it consists of a tree branch. *Gram* was gradually pronounced as *grand*, and by adding the Latin adjectival ending, we obtained *grandis*. From this, we further derive *grandeur*, *to grow* <note>original: "grandir"</note>, *greatly* <note>original: "grandement"</note>, and *aggrandizement* <note>original: "aggrandissement"</note>; in such a way that, through successive derivations, *ram* has formed words fifteen letters long.

Thus, there is no word of any kind that does not relate to a primitive noun, which always depicts a physical object.

In this way, Nouns become the base and the key to Languages: they serve as so many compartments among which all words must be distributed. Indeed, since speech depicts our ideas, and ideas depict the objects represented by Nouns, these nouns are the only words that necessarily exist and are drawn...

It is this simple and noble harmony which constitutes the beauty of language, and which alone can facilitate its study.

### 4°. *On Derived and Compound Words.*

Since Primitive Nouns serve only to designate objects, and cannot suffice to express all our ideas, they are supplemented by means of Derived and Compound Words.
By means of derivation, a Noun changes slightly in form and becomes suitable for depicting all the ideas related to the same object: thus, from the Latin word *salt* <note>original: "sal"</note>, from which we have made the word *salt* <note>original: "sel"</note>, derive all of these: *salty* <note>original: "salé"</note>, *to salt* <note>original: "saler"</note>, *salt-cellar* <note>original: "saliere"</note>, *saltiness* <note>original: "salure"</note>, etc.
A compound word is a word formed by

### 5°. *On Figurative Words.*

Finally, without multiplying the number of words, we double and triple them by successively giving them several figurative meanings, in addition to the physical meaning they originally offer. These figurative words are formed in a great number of ways that can be reduced to these four.

1°. *Extension*, a figure of speech called by a Greek word CATACHRESIS <note>Greek: *katakhrēsis*, meaning "misuse" or the application of a term to a thing which lacks a proper name of its own</note>. Here, words are diverted from their primary meaning to take on another that has some relationship with it. It is by catachresis that we say a REAM <note>original: "RAME"</note> of paper, from the word *ramus* <note>Latin: "branch"</note> which signifies branch, a *leaf* <note>original: "feuille"</note> of paper, or a *hand* <note>original: "main"; a traditional unit of paper quantity</note> of paper.

Examples include the etymological components of "transport" and "midday," as well as the botanical metaphors used to describe quantities and units of paper.</summary>

<header>UNIVERSAL GRAMMAR</header>

2°. The change of *Name*, or METONYMY <term>Metonymy is a figure of speech where a thing or concept is referred to by the name of something closely associated with it, such as "the crown" for a monarch</term>. This figure occurs on various occasions, such as when one substitutes the name of the cause for that of the effect, or the name of the effect for the name of the cause. *To live off one’s labor*, to mean *to live off what one earns through their labor*. *A shore without shade*, to mean *without trees*.

The name of the container for the thing contained. A *nest* <note>original: "nid"</note> instead of the *birds that are in the nest*.

The name of the place where a thing is made, substituted for the name of the thing itself. A *Persian* <note>original: "Perse"</note>, instead of a *fabric manufactured in Persia*. A *Damascus* <note>original: "Damas"</note>, for a *saber made in Damascus*.

The sign for the thing signified. The *Robe* <note>original: "Robe"</note> for the *Judiciary* <note>original: "Magistrature"; referring to the traditional garments worn by judges</note>.

3°. *Comprehension* or SYNECDOQUE <term>Synecdoche is a figure of speech where a part of something is used to represent the whole, or the whole for a part</term>: by this figure, one makes the mind conceive more or less than what the word used signifies in its proper sense. Thus one says *one hundred thousand souls*, *one hundred hearths* <note>original: "feux", literally "fires"</note>, for *one hundred thousand people*, *one hundred families*.

UNIVERSAL GRAMMAR

# CHAPTER II.

## ON ARTICLES.

Proper Names, as we have seen, always present a determined sense, whereas <term>Appellative Names</term> <note>Now commonly known as "common nouns."</note>, being suitable for several objects of the same nature, designate none in particular. Whenever, therefore, we have occasion to designate one of these, we shall be obliged to accompany their names with some words that draw them out of this indeterminate sense they offer, & which make them the name of the very Object we wish to depict, so that it may be recognized instantly, as surely as if we were pointing to it with our hand. Such is the use of Articles. These words determine, as if by gesture, among several objects to which the same name applies, the one we have in view.

Like Adjectives, they accompany Nouns & they change form with them;

**Proper names, Appellative names, Articles, Adjectives, Grammar**

<summary>This page introduces Chapter II on Articles, explaining that they are necessary to clarify which specific object a common noun refers to, acting much like a physical gesture of pointing.</summary>
<keywords>grammar, articles, proper names, appellative names, common nouns, linguistics, determination</keywords>

--- Page 234 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Articles from page 187, moving from their general definition to their specific distinction from adjectives and their classification in the French language.</meta>

NATURAL HISTORY OF SPEECH.

but they differ essentially from Adjectives, in that the latter make known the qualities of the objects being spoken of; whereas the Article does nothing but show the object itself: it only adds to it an idea of presence, more or less distant.

There exist in French three kinds of Articles: **THIS** [CE], **THE** [LE] & **A** [UN]: they all three differ by the manner in which they determine the noun they accompany.

Indeed, an <term>appellative word</term> <note>A common noun, such as "man," "dog," or "house," which refers to a category rather than a specific individual.</note> can be applied to a single one of the objects to which it belongs, in one or the other of these three ways:

Either by pointing to this object, because it is already before one's eyes.

Or by determining it in a manner that prevents it from being confused with any other object designated by the same name.

Or by stating it purely and simply as an existing object.

Thus, in saying **THIS PALACE** *is very beautiful*, one points to a Palace upon which one only has to cast one's eyes.

<meta>This page immediately precedes the previously translated Page 189. It establishes the theoretical distinction between adjectives and articles before the author provides the detailed examples of the "Palace" seen on the following page.</meta>

# NATURAL HISTORY OF SPEECH.

...but they differ essentially from Adjectives, in that the latter make known the qualities of the objects of which one speaks; whereas the Article does nothing more than point out the object itself: it adds to it only an idea of presence, whether more or less distant.

There exist in French three kinds <note>The original OCR reads "fortes," a common misreading of the historical "long s" in the word "sortes" (kinds).</note> of Articles: *CE* [this/that], *LE* [the] & *UN* [a/an]: they all three differ by the manner in which they determine the noun they accompany.

Indeed, an <term>appellative word</term> <note>A common noun that describes a class of things (like "palace" or "dog") rather than a specific proper name (like "Versailles" or "Fido").</note> can be applied to a single one of the objects to which it belongs in one of these three ways:

Either by pointing out that object, because it is already before one's eyes.

Or by determining it in a manner that prevents it from being confused with any other object designated by the same name.

Or by stating it purely and simply as an existing object.

Thus, by saying *THIS PALACE is very beautiful*, one points out a Palace upon which one has only to cast one's eyes.

<vocab>Adjectives, Article, appellative word, French, Palace</vocab>

The last <note>Referring to the example using "A" (Un).</note> is neither before one's eyes, nor determined in any particular way: it is simply stated.

*Ce* [This/That] is therefore a **demonstrative article**.

*Le* [The] is an **indicative article**.

*Un* [A/An] is an **enunciative article**.

### *Characteristics of Articles.*

Articles are extremely short words, simple monosyllables: they consist of only a single sound, a single burst of the voice; and it was necessary for them to be so; had they been longer, they would not have been more useful; they would have fatigued the attention, and they would have strayed too far from gesture <note>The author refers to the Enlightenment-era theory that language evolved from physical gestures. Short articles were thought to mimic the quick action of pointing.</note>.

1°. They never travel without a <term>Noun</term> <note>original: *Nom*. In 18th-century grammar, the term "Nom" (Name) was used for what we now call a Noun.</note>, having no meaning without them.

### *Plural Articles.*

Let us not omit that *le* and *la* [the] become **THE** <note>original: *LES*</note> in the plural; *ce* and *cette* [this/that], **THESE** <note>original: *CES*</note>. The Men, these Women. As for the plural of *un* [a/an], it is the elliptical <note>An "ellipsis" is the omission of words that are understood in context; the author argues that "des" is a shortened form of a longer phrase.</note> word **SOME** <note>original: *DES*</note>: indeed, just as one says in the singular, *a scholar has written that*, etc., one ought to say in the plural *some among the scholars*, or *some of the scholars have written that*. But since *some* can be suppressed, one says simply *some scholars* <note>original: *des savans*</note>.

### *Articles Joined to other Parts of Speech.*

We have seen that to make Speech more lively and concise, one often joined two or more Parts of Speech into a single word, from which were formed elliptical <note>An "ellipse" or elliptical word is a linguistic shortcut where certain elements are omitted or combined because they are understood by the listener.</note> words that cannot be assigned to any single Part of Speech. This occurs especially with regard to Articles.

Thus, **MY** <note>original: *MON*</note> stands in place of these words, *the mine* <note>original: *le mien*</note>. **YOUR** <note>original: *TON*</note> takes the place of these, *the thine* <note>original: *le tien*</note>.

**WHO** <note>original: *QUI*</note>, stands for these two words, *and this [person], or that person*.

**ALL** <note>original: *TOUT*</note> replaces these words, *the entirety of beings*.

It is therefore not surprising that they are used at the beginning of nouns, just like articles, even though they are not truly articles themselves, since the article is hidden within them.

Let us add that Articles often unite with Nouns to the point of becoming an essential part of them.

### ON ADJECTIVES.

#### §. I. *Necessity of having words that designate the qualities of objects.*

<meta>This page completes the sentence from page 196, continuing the discussion on the necessity of adjectives. It specifically defines the difference between an abstract noun and an adjective based on how they relate to objects.</meta>

-> # UNIVERSAL GRAMMAR # <-

...gnificence <note>The text completes the word "magnificence," the first part of which appeared as the catchword "ma-" at the end of the previous page.</note> of a beautiful sunset, charm the sight, etc., while the qualities of a father, a friend, a relative, a protector, etc., have unalterable rights over our heart. Remove these qualities from an object, and it will no longer be anything to us. Man himself can only become perfect and lovable by constantly increasing his good qualities.

It was therefore necessary to have words that expressed not only qualities—which is the purpose of abstract nouns—but words that made these qualities known as they exist within the objects of which one speaks; and it is these words that we call *Adjectives*.

Thus, *brightness* <note>original: "éclat"</note> is an abstract noun, because it depicts a quality considered in itself; but *bright* <note>original: "éclatant"</note> is an adjective, because it depicts this quality as being found in an object.

It is for this reason that Adjectives are constantly placed alongside the Noun that depicts the object in which the qualities expressed by these words are found: thus language approaches Nature as closely as pos- <note>The text breaks mid-word, likely concluding as "possible" on the next page.</note>

<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 197, completing the argument that language mirrors nature by pairing nouns (objects) with adjectives (qualities).</meta>

-> # NATURAL HISTORY OF SPEECH # <-

-sible <note>Completing the word "possible" from the end of the previous page.</note>: for the noun is accompanied by qualifying words, just as the object is accompanied by its qualities.

These words are called <term>ADJECTIVES</term> <note>From the Latin *adjectivum*, meaning "that which is added."</note>, that is to say, *added*, because they are added to Nouns; and they add to the idea of the Nouns those qualities possessed by the objects designated by these Nouns.

In this phrase, *this Temple is vast, superb & magnificent*, the words *vast, superb, magnificent*, are Adjectives, since they express the qualities one perceives in the object in question.

*Great, pleasant, wise, pretty, honest, virtuous*, etc., are Adjectives.

Nothing is so easy to distinguish as the Noun and the Adjective: the one always designates an object, the other never designates anything but qualities: the one stands alone, the other always needs a support, a Noun to which it relates.

One further sees this difference between them: that the Noun applies only to objects of the same species <note>The author refers to the "species" or category of an object; for example, the noun "dog" only refers to that specific type of animal.</note>, whereas the adjective can

<summary>This page defines the adjective as a word "added" to a noun to express the qualities of an object. The author highlights the fundamental distinction between nouns, which represent independent objects, and adjectives, which require a noun for support.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 198 regarding the versatility of the adjective, specifically how a single adjective can be applied to diverse categories of nouns.</meta>

# UNIVERSAL GRAMMAR #

-sociate <note>Completing the word "associate" from the previous page.</note> with a multitude of different nouns or objects: thus we say,
An *elevated* man, an *elevated* place, an *elevated* cloud, an *elevated* voice, etc. <note>The French word *élevé* translates to both physical height ("high") and social or moral status ("elevated").</note>
One could regard Adjectives as <term>ellipses</term> <note>An ellipsis is a linguistic device where words are omitted because they are understood from the context.</note>, for they depict less the quality itself than the state of an object accompanied by such or such quality. Thus, an *elevated* man, an *elevated* place, signify a man or a place that possesses the quality we call *elevation*. One gains, through this method, brevity—which is a great point—and very varied turns of phrase without monotony, which is another very important matter.
From this result the <term>Enunciative Pictures</term> <note>The author uses this term to describe sentences that "paint" a description or state of being.</note> that we have mentioned:

->This Tower is prodigious.<-
->This Dome is magnificent.<-
->The Weather is unsettled.<- <note>original: "Le Tems eſt dérangé." In the 18th century, this referred to weather that had turned stormy or foul.</note>

Such is also the difference that reigns between articles and adjectives: the latter can be expressed, as we have just seen, by different (though longer) formulas, whereas nothing can take the place of the article.

### NATURAL HISTORY OF SPEECH ###

Adjectives were therefore born from Nouns; originally, they were those very Nouns, placed after other Nouns to qualify them. When Language was in its infancy <note>The author refers to the "cradle" of language, a common 18th-century concept suggesting that early human speech was primitive and based on sensory experiences.</note>, a *Mountain-Man* signified a man of great stature, a man as tall as a Mountain; a *bear-man* was a crude or boorish man. The name of the Sun, or *Bel* <note>original: "Bel". This refers to the ancient Semitic deity Bel or Baal, whom Enlightenment scholars often associated with the sun. The author suggests a link between this name and the French word for beauty, "belle".</note>, was borrowed to designate beauty, and it has remained attached to that quality, even while it was lost as a name for the Sun itself. Thus, one combined the simplicity of an emerging Language unburdened by many words; the richness of Poetic Language filled with figures and comparisons; and the precision of Philosophical Language, which must always align with the nature of things and should not proceed by comparison.

Soon, however, it was noticed that it was inconvenient to have two Nouns follow one another, and that it was problematic for the same word to designate an object at one time and a quality at another. To remedy these inconveniences, a means of the greatest simplicity was employed; it was

<vocab>Adjectives, Nouns, Language, Mountain-Man, Bel, Beauty, Poetic Language, Philosophical Language</vocab>

<summary>The author argues that adjectives originated as nouns used metaphorically to describe other nouns, blending poetic imagery with the practical needs of early language. Over time, these nouns evolved into distinct qualifiers to avoid the confusion of using the same word for both objects and their attributes.</summary>
<keywords>etymology of adjectives, metaphorical language, Bel, sun symbolism, poetic language, philosophical language, linguistic evolution</keywords>

to add to the end of Nouns a letter or a syllable that would indicate that these Nouns designated only a quality: thus, from *ice* <note>original: "glace"</note>, one made *iced* <note>original: "glacé"</note>: from *mountain* <note>original: "mont"</note>, *mountainous* <note>original: "montueux"</note>: from *King* <note>original: "Roi"</note>, *royal* <note>original: "royal"</note>: from *son* <note>original: "fils"</note>, *filial* <note>original: "filial"</note>.

There exists, therefore, no Adjective that is not immediately linked to a Noun, that does not derive from it, and that does not draw all its energy <note>In 18th-century linguistics, "energy" refers to the descriptive power or the semantic essence of a word.</note> from it: this singularly facilitates and simplifies the study of Languages, since with the knowledge of Nouns, one has that of all the Adjectives that were formed from them.

### §. 2. *Nouns derived from Adjectives.*

Such is the flexibility of Language that, after having formed Adjectives from Nouns, it further forms Nouns with Adjectives: 1st. by simply suppressing the Noun; thus one says *the Great*, *the Rich*, *the Learned* <note>original: "les Savans". In the 18th century, "Savant" referred to a person of great learning or a scientist.</note>, *goods* <note>original: "les biens". This refers to possessions or property, derived from the adjective for "good."</note>, as if they were Nouns, whereas they are actually Adjectives, but whose Nouns have been suppressed; by saying *the Great*, instead of saying *great men*; &c. *the Rich*, instead of saying *the...*

<meta>This page completes the sentence from the previous page, detailing the specific categories of comparison in grammar, specifically identifying four types used in the French language.</meta>

<header>UNIVERSAL GRAMMAR.</header>

at least in all Languages, and four in French.

1st. The POSITIVE. It expresses the quality in itself, purely and simply: *great*, *wise*, *sweet* <note>original: *doux*; this term can also mean soft, mild, or gentle depending on the context of the quality being described</note>, etc.

2nd. The COMPARATIVE. It states that of two objects, one possesses a quality to a greater degree than the other. *Greater*, *wiser*, *sweeter*, etc., are Comparatives.

3rd. The RELATIVE SUPERLATIVE; it raises an object above all others, relative to the qualities they have in common. The *greatest*, the *wisest*, the *sweetest*, etc.

4th. The ABSOLUTE SUPERLATIVE; it raises a quality to the highest degree it can reach, and it makes no comparison between two objects, examining the object in question only in itself. *Very great*, *very wise*, *very sweet*, etc., are absolute superlatives.

### §. 5. *Comparative Links.*

When one uses Comparatives, it is to express the relationship that exists between two Objects or two Nouns and the same quality: therefore, so that a clear and precise picture results from it, these various Nouns must be linked in such a way that one immediately perceives they are in contrast. This is what we do in our language using the conjunction THAN <note>original French: *QUE*</note>, when dealing with a Comparative; and by the preposition OF <note>original French: *DE*</note>, when dealing with a Superlative.

This harvest is more abundant THAN the others.

always of external objects that one speaks. One constantly has the opportunity to speak both of oneself and of those whom one addresses. *I am wise*, *you are wise*, *he is wise*. <note>original: *Je suis sage*, *tu es sage*, *il est sage*.</note>

These are what we call PRONOUNS; that is to say, *words that designate persons without the aid of Nouns, and in instances where it would be impossible to use them*.

### Use of Pronouns in Enunciative & Passive Tableaus.

We have just said that there were active Pronouns, *I*, *thou*, *he*; and passive Pronouns, *me*, *thee*, *him*. And this is constantly true in active Tableaus <note>Tableau: The author uses this term (meaning "picture" or "scene") to describe the mental representation of a sentence's structure.</note>; but there also exist enunciative Tableaus and passive Tableaus in which one finds *I*: *I am skillful*, an enunciative Tableau <note>Enunciative: A sentence that simply states a fact or condition of being.</note>: *I am esteemed*, a passive Tableau; but in these instances, *I* is considered the subject of the phrase, while *me* is considered its object. This in no way contradicts what we have put forward regarding *I* considered as an active pronoun: only the circumstances have changed.

In some Languages, *thou* <note>original: "tu"</note> was the only pronoun used in the singular when speaking to a single person; gradually *you* <note>original: "vous"</note>, which was used out of respect toward Princes, dispossessed *thou*, which could only be preserved at opposite extremes: in familiar usage, and in the sublime of Poetry.

UNIVERSAL GRAMMAR.

Regarding the origin of these words: **I** <note>original: "je"</note> comes from the verb **E** <note>The author likely refers to a primitive root for existence or being.</note>, and thus energetically designates the person who announces themselves; **THOU** <note>original: "tu"</note> comes from the primitive **T**, which signifies *greatness*, and very well designates the respect one has for the person being addressed. **THE** and **HE** <note>original: "le" and "il"</note> come from the primitive **L**, which signifies *wing* or *side*, and thereby very well designate that it concerns a third person who is not before us, but further away, to the side, in the vicinity.

We have seen above that the article *the* <note>original: "le"</note> was conflated with the pronoun: from this come the words *my*, *thy*, and *his* <note>original: "mon", "ton", "son"</note>, whose plurals are *our*, *your*, and *their*, which have long been regarded as pronouns, but which are nothing other than <term>elliptical words</term> <note>Elliptical words: Terms that condense a longer phrase into a single word for the sake of brevity.</note>.

*My*, instead of *the*.... *of me*: *my book* for *the book of me*.

*Thy*, instead of *the*.... *of thee*: *thy book* for *the book of thee*, etc.

Since elliptical words are not found in nature, but are only the result of the imagination of each Nation, it

## CHAPTER V.
### ON THE VERB.

Since Nouns and Adjectives are intended to be linked together, just as qualities are linked within objects, a word was required to facilitate this union. This word brings warmth and life to those other parts of speech; it is what presents them in groups, in descriptive scenes, and in great masses. This word above all others is that which designates existence: the word **IS**. Without being a noun, an article, an adjective, or a pronoun, it unites all these elements and gives them a sense of being and a strength that they could not achieve on their own. Consequently, it is the most frequently used word. This is demonstrated by these examples drawn from the beautiful scene between *Joas* and *Athaliah* <note>Characters from Jean Racine’s 1691 play *Athalie*, which was frequently used in the 18th century as a model of perfect French grammar and rhetoric.</note>.

<vocab>Verb, Nouns, Adjectives, existence, Joas, Athaliah, grammar, linguistics</vocab>

One would likewise have the idea of all these names, of all these qualities; but without *is*, they would have no relationship, they would not form any <term>Picture <note>original: "Tableau"; the author uses this metaphor to describe a complete, structured thought or a logical proposition that "paints" a scene in the mind.</note></term>.

### *Origin of the Name it bears.*

This word, serving to form all the Pictures of Speech, and to instill within them a life of which they would be deprived without it—thereby allowing Speech to finally fulfill its goal, which is to paint ideas—deserved a name distinguished from all others, and one that would convey an interesting concept: it is the word *VERB*; borrowed from the <term>Latin <note>original: "Latins"; referring to the word *verbum*, which means "word."</note></term>, it signifies WORD in general; and here, WORD par excellence. It could not have been better named, since it gives to Speech all the force of which it is capable.

This word itself comes from the primitive root *var*, *bar*, or *par*, which signifies *word*, *emanation*, or *passage* <note>The author is following 18th-century linguistic theories, like those of Court de Gébelin, which sought to find "primitive roots" shared across all human languages to explain the origin of ideas.</note>. And such is Speech: an emanation, a vehicle that makes ideas travel, making them pass from one mind into another.

### UNIVERSAL GRAMMAR

The Verb is therefore the word that unites qualities to their objects, which shows that the objects being spoken of exist with such or such quality attributed to them.

This is why, in logical terms, the quality is called the ATTRIBUTE, and the object the SUBJECT; for it is the subject to which the attribute is related, the being to which the quality is attributed: when one says *the sun is bright*, *bright* is the attribute; *sun*, the subject; and *is*, the verb or the *link* <note>original: "lien"; the author emphasizes the verb as the connector in a logical equation.</note> that joins the one to the other.

The whole together forms a picture that is called a SENTENCE <note>original: "PHRASE"; in 18th-century linguistics, this refers to a complete grammatical structure.</note> in grammatical terms, and a PROPOSITION in logical terms, or a STATED JUDGMENT; *stated*, to distinguish it from an internal judgment of which it is the depiction; and *judgment*, because one has judged, decided that such a quality is indeed within such a subject; without this, one would speak without judgment; for one would attribute to subjects qualities that they do not possess, everything would be in confusion, and one would paint only a chaos of ideas; whereas healthy and exquisite judgment consists of seeing

in Beings the qualities they possess; and not attributing others to them; which is not a merit easily acquired.

This word IS <note>original: "EST"; the author is discussing the third-person singular of the verb "to be."</note> could not have been better chosen for the use for which it was intended; it was taken from Nature itself; it is the imitation of breathing itself—of that respiration which is the effect and sign of life. To use it to unite qualities and objects was to animate them, to paint them in the most energetic and effective manner.

Thus, this word *is* is known in almost every language, and is the head of a prodigious multitude of words relating to existence.

Let us not omit that, through its simplicity and energetic concision, it fulfilled the desire of speech—which is to approach the speed of a gesture, and to hasten along with the rapidity of time—and that it in no way hindered the progress of discourse or the mental pictures of our ideas. It would have surely caused such a hindrance had it been even slightly longer, since it recurs constantly in speech.

# CHAPTER VI.

## *Of Participles.*

Participles express the various states in which beings are found as a result of their actions upon themselves or upon others.

When they depict a being in the act of doing something, they are called ACTIVE Participles. And when they depict a being who experiences the effects of the action of another, they are called PASSIVE Participles.

->*Loving*, *doing*, *praising*, are<-
->active Participles.<-
->*Loved*, *done*, *praised*, are passive<-
->Participles.<-

Participles play a very great role

<meta>This page continues the discussion of participles from page 221, moving from their basic definition to their philosophical importance in describing human action and their grammatical construction from Latin roots.</meta>

NATURAL HISTORY OF SPEECH.

in Speech, because they depict the actions of men—those actions which themselves play such a great role on the stage of the World. It is through his active qualities that Man distinguishes himself among all Beings: it is through them that he manifests his most excellent faculties, that he provides for everything, that he constantly perfects himself, that he is virtuous or vicious, worthy of praise or of blame. It is through their actions that Families and Nations themselves rise above their current state, improve their lot, and put the Earth in a condition to receive and make happy a greater number of inhabitants.

These words are formed by the union of two others, a noun and an ending: the ending *en* or *an* for active participles, which designates a Being—the *Ens* <note>original Latin: "Ens," a philosophical term for "a being" or "that which exists."</note> of the Latins—and the ending *é* for passive participles, which designates existence.

*Loving* <note>original: "Aimant"</note> is therefore for *aim-ant*, literally *the Being who is in the active state called love*.

<summary>This page argues that participles are vital to language because they reflect the transformative power of human action, and explains that active participles are etymologically derived from the Latin word for "being."</summary>

*Lov-ed* <note>original French: "Aim-é"</note> is, word for word, *the Being who experiences* from another the effects of the action called *love*.

These endings were already employed by the ancient Greeks & by the ancient Latins. Indeed, it must have been felt very early on how much they shortened speech, and how much force and clarity they gave to it at the same time.

Since Participles designate qualities, they shall therefore be subject to the same laws as Adjectives: they will take on forms analogous to those of the objects to which they refer; they will have genders and numbers. Thus, in the singular, one says *praised* <note>original: "loué" (masculine) and "louée" (feminine)</note>; in the plural, *praised* <note>original: "loués" and "louées." In English, unlike French or Latin, the participle does not change its spelling to match the gender or number of the subject.</note>

It is the same for the active participle in Latin, in Greek, & in the Old French of our Fathers; they used to say *praising* <note>original: "louant," "louante," "louans," "louantes." In modern French, the active participle—the "-ing" form—no longer changes to match the noun.</note>

The reason for the difference observed in this regard between them and us—where the active participle never changes form or is <term>indecinable</term> <note>A word that does not change its form or ending regardless of how it is used in a sentence.</note>—comes from the fact that we do not em-

> But he, **seeing** <note>original: "voyant"</note> in me his brother’s daughter,
> Held for me, dear Elise, the place of both father and mother....
> Who could, however, express the intrigues
> Formed in this place by this crowd of Rivals
> Who all, **discussing** <note>original: "discutant"</note> such a great interest,
> From Ahasuerus’s <note>The Persian King in the Book of Esther</note> eyes awaited their decree?

This *seeing* and this *discussing* act as circumstances; it is as if one had said, *because he saw*, and *while they were disputing*.

### *Passive Participle employed in Active Verbs.*

In saying that *loved* <note>original: "aimé"</note>, *praised* <note>original: "loué"</note>, etc., are passive participles, we have a difficulty to resolve; it is that these participles associate with the verb *to be* <note>original: "être"</note> or with the verb *to have* <note>original: "avoir"</note>, to form active verbs; when one says, for example, *I have written* <note>original: "j'ai écrit"</note>, *I have praised* <note>original: "j'ai loué"</note>: which seems contradictory <note>The author is pointing out the grammatical paradox where a "passive" form—the past participle—is used to construct "active" past tenses in French and English.</note>.

Universal Grammar

But this is one of the common ellipses <note>A grammatical term for the omission of words that are understood by the reader.</note> in speech: thus *I have written* <note>original: "j’ai écrit"</note> signifies "I have just caused such a thing to exist as *written* by me." *I have loved* <note>original: "J’ai aimé"</note> will signify "I have been in the state called *to love*."

If one adds the object of these actions—be it a letter that one has *written* <note>original: "écrit"</note>, or Scholars <note>original: "Savans," an archaic spelling</note> whom one has *praised* <note>original: "loué"</note>—these words *written* and *praised* will nevertheless change in neither gender nor number; one will say *I have written a letter* <note>original: "j’ai écrit une lettre"</note>, *I have praised some Scholars* <note>original: "j’ai loué des Savans"</note>; and not, *I have written-fem a letter* <note>original: "j’ai écrite une lettre"</note>, *I have praised-plural some Scholars* <note>original: "j’ai loués des Savans"</note>; for it would then seem that one was saying *I have*, that is to say, *I possess a written letter*, etc., and that is not, however, what one intended to say. <note>The author is explaining the logic of French past participle agreement. He argues that when the participle doesn't agree, it's because the focus is on the action performed rather than the current state of possession of the object.</note>

This same passive participle also designates circumstances, but past ones; thus when one says, *this considered* <note>original: "ce consideré"</note>, *all maturely weighed* <note>original: "tout mûrement pesé"</note>, it is as if one said *after having considered these things*, *after having weighed everything with care*, etc.

## CHAPTER VII.

### *On Elliptical Participles, or Active Verbs.*

The Verb IS <note>original: "EST"</note> was found in all the Tableaus of speech <note>The author uses the term "Tableau" (picture/scene) to describe the structure of a sentence as a visual representation of a thought.</note>, and this made speech too monotonous; a remedy was sought for this monotony; it was soon found: it was to make the Verb TO BE <note>original: "ÊTRE"</note> disappear in active Tableaus, and to simply express the pronoun and the action, as when we say *he lodges, he walks, he offers* <note>original: "il loge, il marche, il offre"</note>, where there are only two words that mean the same thing as these: *he is lodging, he is walking, he is offering* <note>original: "il est logeant, il est marchant, il est offrant"</note>.

This ellipsis <note>A grammatical term for the omission of words that are understood by the reader.</note> was very beautiful and was not at all difficult to grasp; one saw without effort that the person designated was not the action expressed after them; but that one simply wished to represent them in the state designated by that action.

Such was the origin of active Verbs, of those Verbs that exist in all Lan-

## ARTICLE II.

->*On Tenses.*<- <note>In French, the word *tems* (modern *temps*) refers to both chronological time and grammatical tense. The author is exploring the philosophical link between the two.</note>

UNTIL NOW we have considered existence only at a single point, in the current moment. This is the only time that would exist for us if we were limited to simple sensations; experiencing only the current sensation, we would have no knowledge other than that of the moment; but such is the perfection of man, that not only does he have a sense of the present, but by recalling his past actions, he still preserves the memory of time that is no more; and by extending his view beyond the present, he discovers times that are not yet: thus our existence expands through both the past existence we recall and the future existence we foresee.

<summary>The author concludes a list of verbs derived from nouns and begins a new section on the nature of time and grammatical tense, arguing that human consciousness uniquely connects past, present, and future.</summary>
