This diversity of Tenses <note>The original French *tems* (modern *temps*) refers to both chronological time and the grammatical tense of a verb.</note> is continually depicted in our ideas; they all bear its imprint, since we cannot represent to ourselves any being or any action without seeing them in the present, the past, or the future.

From this comes the division of verbs into three Tenses: **PRESENT**, **PAST**, and **FUTURE**. *He is, he was, he will be. He does, he did, he will do.* <note>Original: *Il est, il fut, il sera. Il fait, il fit, il sera.* The second "sera" is likely a printer's error for "fera" (will do), to match the verb "faire" (to do/make) used in that sequence.</note>

But although, absolutely speaking, only these three Tenses can exist—so that one might call them **ABSOLUTE TENSES**—there can nevertheless exist a great number of relative ones, for both the past and the future; for all portions of the past are not equally distant, and of these portions, some precede and others follow. Thus, yesterday is less distant than the day before yesterday; and of these two days, one was prior to the other. It is the same for the future.

The necessity of distinguishing these nuances <note>The author uses "nuance" to describe the subtle differences in timing, such as the "recent past" versus the "remote past."</note> was felt; hence a great number of <term>relative Tenses</term> <note>Relative tenses are those that describe an action in relation to another point in time, rather than just the moment of speaking (e.g., "I had eaten before he arrived").</note>, a number of pasts, and a number of fu-

<summary>The author explains that because humans perceive time in three stages, verbs are divided into Present, Past, and Future. He then introduces the concept of "Relative Tenses" to account for different degrees of distance in time, such as the difference between yesterday and the day before.</summary>

tures, both anterior and posterior; but many more past tenses than future tenses, because there is much more to say about the past than about the future.

These various tenses are expressed in different ways: by a single word, like the absolute tenses, *I was doing* <note>Original: *je faisois* (archaic spelling of *faisais*). This is the imperfect tense, indicating ongoing or repeated action in the past.</note>. By a participle joined to the absolute tenses of the verb *to have*, such as *I have done*, *I had done*, *I shall have done* <note>Original: *j’ai fait, j’eus fait, j’avois fait, j’aurai fait.* The author is describing the formation of compound tenses.</note>. By the verbs *to come*, *to go*, and *to owe/must*, joined to an infinitive, such as *I must do*, *I am going to do*, etc. <note>In French, the verb *venir* (to come) is used for the recent past (*je viens de faire* - I have just done), and *aller* (to go) for the near future.</note>

Finally, others relate, according to the circumstances, to several different epochs; this is why they are called **INDEFINITE**. When we say, for example, *I* **DO** *this work tomorrow*, this present tense is taken for a future because of its <term>indefinite quality</term> <note>An "indefinite" tense is one that lacks a fixed temporal boundary, allowing the present tense to be used to describe a certain future.</note>, which makes it suitable for designating a future event as if it were present.

### UNIVERSAL GRAMMAR

**The PRESENT**; it designates what takes place in the instant one speaks.

**The PAST or PRETERITE**; it designates what took place in an instant that no longer exists.

**The FUTURE**; it designates what will take place in an instant that is not yet here.

And these three **<term>Tenses</term> <note>Original: *Tems*, an archaic spelling of *temps*.</note> are ABSOLUTE**: they are of the greatest simplicity, referring only to a single object.

Often, on the contrary, one is obliged to indicate the relationship of what is said with other events: for this purpose, new forms and new tenses were necessary, and from this come the **RELATIVE TENSES**.

1st. One can represent an event as present at the time when an event that no longer exists occurred; the form one will use for this will therefore be a relative present. Such is this one: **I WAS DOING** <note>Original: *JE FAISOIS*. The author is describing what we now call the "imperfect" tense, which portrays a past action as if it were currently "in progress" at a specific past moment.</note>. *I was doing an interesting Work when you arrived.*

Here, the event is present, not for the moment in which one speaks, but for the moment of which one speaks, and which is past. Thus, the event—

But as we are only providing Elements here, we will not speak of indefinite Tenses, nor of those formed by an infinitive. We will limit ourselves simply to giving an idea of the absolute and relative Tenses, each of which has a particular form, and which are common to most languages. Those who desire more metaphysics on these objects need only consult the works we have just cited, or what we have said about them and the extract we have given in this regard from the *Universal Grammar* of Mr. Harris <note>James Harris (1709–1780), whose 1751 work *Hermes, or a Philosophical Inquiry Concerning Universal Grammar* was highly influential.</note>, written in English.

There exist, then, essentially and necessarily three Tenses <note>Original: *Tems*, an archaic French spelling of *temps*.</note>.

->UNIVERSAL GRAMMAR<-

The *PRESENT*; it designates what takes place in the instant in which one speaks.

The *PAST* or *PRETERIT*; it designates what took place in an instant that is no more.

The *FUTURE*; it designates what will take place in the instant which is not yet.

And these three Tenses are *ABSOLUTE*: they are of the greatest simplicity, referring only to a single object.

Here the event is present, not for the moment in which one speaks, but for the moment of which one speaks & which is past. Also the event—

those in which one considers the event only in itself, independently of its beginning or its end.

One can also consider an action relative to the *disposition* <note>In this context, "disposition" refers to the speaker's intent or readiness to act.</note> one has to perform it, and relative to the *obligation* one has to perform it. From this, new formulas result.

Regarding disposition, one says: *I am going to do*, *I was going to do*, *I will go to do in a moment*, etc.

Regarding obligation, one says: *I must do*, *I was to do* <note>Original: *je devois faire*. In 18th-century French, this imperfect form often carried the weight of "I was supposed to do" or "I owed it to do."</note>, *I shall have to do*, etc.

We see, therefore, that it is easy to form various divisions of Tenses <note>The author uses the 18th-century spelling *Tems*.</note> and to give them various names, depending on the point of view from which they are considered; but what must never be forgotten is that every event can be considered relative to the time when it took place, and relative to other events; and it is to this double property that every division of Tenses and the names given to them must be reduced.

These words are called *Prepositions*, from two Latin words <note>original: *prae* (before) and *ponere* (to place)</note> which mean *placed before* or *placed to govern*. <note>In grammar, "governing" refers to how one word controls the form or meaning of another.</note>

The **PREPOSITION** is thus a word that serves to mark a relationship between two objects.

Prepositions are quite numerous in every Language; it is therefore appropriate to divide them into classes, so that one might more easily form a clear and exact idea of them.

They can first be related to two large Classes: the *Enunciative* <term>Enunciative: Used here to describe prepositions that state a simple relationship or existence within a static scene.</term> employed in *Enunciative tableaus*, and the *prepositions of action* or *circumstantial* employed in *Active and passive tableaus*. <note>The author uses the word "tableaus" (tableaux) to describe the mental "pictures" or sentence structures that language creates in the mind of the listener.</note>

<header>UNIVERSAL GRAMMAR.</header>

Each of these Classes will be subdivided into others.

## FIRST CLASS,

### Enunciative Prepositions.

Enunciative Prepositions <term>Enunciative: Terms used to describe words that state a fact or a simple state of being, rather than an action.</term> designate simple relationships of existence, resulting from the very nature of Beings. Two objects can be compared relative to their manner of being, under the five following relationships: relationships of *situation*, of *time* <note>original: *tems*</note>, of *place*, of *union*, and of *dependence*.

### I. *Prepositions that indicate a relationship of* SITUATION.

The situation of an object is always relative to that of another; for it is only by comparing objects with one another that one forms an idea of their situation. But this situation can be considered from different points of view, such as those of *surface*, *capacity*, *distance*, and *order*.

<sig>Q</sig>

<meta>This page continues the detailed classification of "Enunciative" prepositions, specifically those relating to "situation." The previous page introduced the five categories of these relationships (situation, time, place, union, and dependence).</meta>

-># NATURAL HISTORY OF SPEECH.<-

### 1. *Prepositions of situation, relative to the surface.*

We distinguish between two kinds of surfaces: one horizontal, the other perpendicular. The surface of a table is of the first kind, and that of a building <note>The author uses a building to represent a vertical plane.</note> is of the second.

### *Prepositions of situation, relative to the horizontal surface.*

Horizontal surfaces, having a top and a bottom, give rise to two different situational relationships that are necessarily expressed by two different prepositions: for the same object can be placed above or below such a surface. From this, two Prepositions arise:

**ON** <note>original: *SUR*</note>, a Preposition that expresses a situational relationship of an object being in a superior position relative to the horizontal surface of another object.

**UNDER** <note>original: *SOUS*</note>, a Preposition that expresses a situational relationship of an object being in an inferior position relative to the horizontal surface of another object.

This Book is **ON** the table, under the table.

<vocab>prepositions, situation, horizontal surface, perpendicular surface, on, under</vocab>

<summary>The author defines prepositions of situation by categorizing surfaces into horizontal and perpendicular planes. He specifically introduces "on" and "under" as the primary descriptors for horizontal relationships.</summary>
<keywords>grammar, prepositions, horizontal surface, perpendicular surface, situational relationships, spatial linguistics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the systematic classification of prepositions of situation. Having previously addressed horizontal surfaces (above/below), the author now moves to perpendicular surfaces and begins the discussion of spatial capacity.</meta>

<header>UNIVERSAL GRAMMAR 243</header>

### *Prepositions of situation, relative to the perpendicular surface.*

Perpendicular surfaces, such as those of a wall or a door, offer two situational relationships; for relative to such a surface, an object can be placed in front of or behind it: hence,

**IN FRONT OF** <note>original: *DEVANT*</note>, a Preposition that expresses a situational relationship of an object being in an anterior position relative to the perpendicular surface of another object.

**BEHIND** <note>original: *DERRIERE*</note>, a Preposition that expresses a situational relationship of an object being in a posterior position relative to a perpendicular surface.

This tree is **IN FRONT OF** the wall, **BEHIND** the house.

### *2°. Prepositions of situation, relative to the capacity of an object.*

If one considers an object such as a house or a case, relative to its capacity or to the property it has of containing other objects within its interior, two new relationships and two new Prepositions result from it.

4. *Prepositions relative to the order in which*
*objects are found.*

The order in which objects are found relative to other objects can be considered under three different relationships: an object precedes, follows, or is placed between others: from this, various Prepositions arise.

**BEFORE** <note>original: *Avant*</note>, a Preposition that marks that one object precedes another.
-> His Guards marched **BEFORE** <note>original: *avant*</note> him. <-

**AFTER** <note>original: *Après*</note>, a Preposition that marks that one object follows another.
-> **AFTER** <note>original: *Après*</note> the lightning, the thunder. <-

**BETWEEN** <note>original: *Entre*</note>, a Preposition that marks that an object is found in the middle of two others.
-> Switzerland is **BETWEEN** <note>original: *entre*</note> France & Germany. <-

**AMONG** <note>original: *Parmi*</note>, a Preposition that marks that an object is in the midst of a great number of others.
-> He was found **AMONG** <note>original: *parmi*</note> those whom the Festival had attracted. <-

## IV. DECLARATIVE PREPOSITIONS *relative to* UNION.

Objects can exist alone or together: which gives rise to other relationships, and thereby to other **Prepositions**.

**WITH** <note>original: *Avec*</note>, **Preposition** that indicates a relationship of union and cooperation <note>The original term *concours* suggests a meeting or "running together" of things or actions.</note>.

**WITHOUT** <note>original: *Sans*</note>, **Preposition** that excludes all relationship of union and cooperation.
->He is **WITHOUT** friends, **WITHOUT** help.<-

### V. **DECLARATIVE PREPOSITIONS** *relative to* **OWNERSHIP** *and dependency.*

The prepositions relative to ownership and dependency are two in number: **FROM/OF** <note>original: *De*</note> and **TO** <note>original: *À*</note>. The first has a closer relationship to dependency, marking whom one depends upon, or where one comes from.

->It is a letter **FROM** <note>original: *De*</note> my Sister.<-

**TO** <note>original: *À*</note> presents a closer relationship to the idea of possession and where one is going.

->I am sending this **TO** <note>original: *À*</note> my Sister.<-
->This Book belongs **TO** <note>original: *À*</note> the Queen.<-
->It is for the Leader **TO** <note>original: *À*</note> command.<-

## SECOND CLASS.

### *Circumstantial prepositions, or those relative to actions.*

Every action can be considered in the following ways, each of which creates a specific circumstance:

->Its origin & its Author.<-
->Its cause & its motive.<-
->The object to which it relates.<-
->The means by which it is carried out.<-
->The model according to which it is executed.<-

1°. **FROM** <note>original: *De*</note> & **BY** <note>original: *Par*</note> indicate the Authors and the origin of an action.

> His army was defeated **BY** <note>original: *par*</note> the Romans.
> This action can only come **FROM** <note>original: *de*</note> him.

2°. **GIVEN** <note>original: *Attendu*</note> & **IN VIEW OF** <note>original: *Vu*</note> indicate the motives that determine an action.

> **GIVEN** <note>original: *Attendu*</note> his wisdom, he was rewarded.

**SAVE FOR** <note>original: *Sauf*</note> indicates that a decision is made only as long as there is no more powerful motive to change it.

> **SAVE FOR** <note>original: *Sauf*</note> better advice, one follows his own.

3°. **TO** <note>original: *À*</note> & **FOR** <note>original: *Pour*</note> indicate the objects toward which an action is directed.

## SECOND CLASS.

### *Circumstantial prepositions or those relative to actions.*

Every action can be considered in the following respects, which constitute so many specific circumstances:

->Its origin & its Author.<-
->Its cause & its motive.<-
->The object to which it relates.<-
->The means by which it is carried out.<-
->The model after which it is executed.<-

**1. FROM & BY** <note>original: *DE & PAR*</note>, indicate the Authors and the origin of an action.

> His army was defeated **BY** <note>original: *PAR*</note> the Romans.
> This action can only come **FROM** <note>original: *DE*</note> him.

**2. GIVEN & CONSIDERING** <note>original: *ATTENDU & VU*</note>, indicate the motives that determine an outcome.

> **GIVEN** <note>original: *ATTENDU*</note> his wisdom, he was rewarded.

**SAVE FOR** <note>original: *Sauf*</note>, indicates that one only makes a decision insofar as there is no more powerful motive to the contrary.

> **SAVE FOR** <note>original: *Sauf*</note> a better opinion, one follows his own. <note>In this context, the author uses "sauf" to mean "barring" or "subject to," showing how a motive can be conditional.</note>

**3. TO & FOR** <note>original: *À & POUR*</note>, indicate the objects toward which an action aims.

<page-num>253</page-num>
<header>Universal Grammar</header>

**AGAINST** <note>original: *CONTRE*</note>, marks a violation of the rule, opposition to an object.

->He acts **AGAINST** the law.<-
->He goes **AGAINST** common sense.<-

Prepositions thus become part of continual use; they constitute a great part of the beauties and subtleties <note>The French *finesses* refers to the delicate nuances of meaning that native speakers use to express complex thoughts.</note> of a language, so that it is very important to have precise ideas of them.

It is true that sometimes they seem to be employed in all sorts of senses, often very little linked to each other, sometimes even opposite; but when one traces them back to a proper and physical sense, one easily sees the birth of all these varieties that were previously thought inexplicable. <note>The author argues that even the most abstract uses of language have their roots in physical, tangible descriptions of the world.</note>

### *Origin of the words used as Prepositions.*

None of these words was ever the effect of chance: they were always formed from Nouns that designated objects relative to the physical sense offered by these prepositions. **UPON** <note>original: *SUR*</note>, formed from the Latin **ABOVE** <note>original: *SUPER*</note>, comes from the primitive root **UP** <note>original: *HUP*, likely referring to the Greek root *hupsi* (on high) or *huper* (over)</note>, which designates *elevation*.

<summary>This page concludes the study of specific prepositions with "against" and argues that all prepositions, however abstract their use, originate from physical descriptions and nouns related to tangible reality.</summary>
<keywords>prepositions, AGAINST, etymology, Universal Grammar, language philosophy, SUR, SUPER, HUP</keywords>

--- Page 322 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 253 regarding the etymological origins of prepositions, moving from the specific example of "upon" to "before," "outside," and "to," concluding with a general theory of how prepositions function as elliptical nouns.</meta>

<page-num>254</page-num>
<header>NATURAL HISTORY OF SPEECH</header>

*In front of* <note>original: *devant*</note> & *before* <note>original: *avant*</note> are formed from the Latin *ANTE* <note>Latin for "before" or "in front of."</note>, born from a primitive root which signifies the eye and that which is in front of it.

OUTSIDE <note>original: *HORS*</note> comes from a primitive root signifying a door, an entrance, or the exterior of a house.

TO <note>original: *A*</note>, designating a relationship of property or belonging, comes from the primitive root *A*, which designates possession.

The same is true for all other prepositions, in whatever language they may be.

They derive all their energy <note>In 18th-century linguistics, "energy" refers to the expressive power or the inherent meaning a word carries from its origin.</note> from the noun from which they were formed, and whose value they represent by ellipsis <note>An "ellipsis" here refers to the shortening of a longer phrase into a single functional word.</note>; not as designating an object itself, but as showing that there exists between two other objects a relationship corresponding to the nature of the object designated by the noun from which they are formed. Thus, UPON <note>original: *SUR*</note>, signifying ELEVATION, and being placed between the names of two objects, indicates that there is a relationship of elevation between them—that one is elevated relative to the other.

Thus, prepositions are not new words added to languages: they are not

<summary>The author explains that prepositions like "before," "outside," and "to" originate from nouns representing physical objects or concepts, acting as a shorthand to describe the spatial or logical relationship between two things.</summary>
<keywords>etymology, prepositions, grammar, ANTE, SUR, elevation, linguistics, word origins</keywords>

--- Page 323 ---
<meta>This page completes the sentence from page 254, which argued that prepositions are not entirely new inventions but rather specialized uses of existing nouns. It transitions into a discussion on "inseparable prepositions," which we today refer to as prefixes.</meta>

<page-num>255</page-num>
<header>UNIVERSAL GRAMMAR</header>

are nothing but a particular use of already existing words.

### INSEPARABLE PREPOSITIONS.

From this practice of employing a word in an elliptical sense <note>As established on the previous page, "elliptical" refers to a word standing in for a longer phrase or a more complex spatial relationship.</note> to designate the relationships between objects, another use of Prepositions was born, which became the source of a prodigious quantity of words.

This consisted of placing Prepositions at the head of words to diversify their meaning and indicate their relationships; from this comes inexhaustible riches for Languages, through the abundance of words that are born from them, and through the finesse and precision they spread throughout the expression of ideas.

Thus, from the single Verb TO PUT <note>original French: *METTRE*, derived from the Latin *MITTERE*, "to send" or "to place."</note>, we create all of the following by means of inseparable or initial prepositions <note>In modern linguistics, these are called prefixes.</note>:

*AD-mit* <note>original: *AD-mettre*</note>, or to put near oneself <note>The Latin prefix *AD-* signifies "to" or "toward."</note>.
*COM-mit* <note>original: *COM-mettre*</note>, or to put with <note>The Latin prefix *COM-* signifies "with" or "together."</note>.
*DE-mit* <note>original: *DE-mettre*; in this context, it refers to dismissing or removing, such as "demitting" an office.</note>, or to put out.
*O-mit* <note>original: *O-mettre*</note>, or not to put before <note>Derived from the Latin *OB-*, often meaning "before" or "against."</note>.
*PER-mit* <note>original: *PER-mettre*</note>, or to put by means of another <note>The Latin prefix *PER-* signifies "through" or "by."</note>.

<summary>The author explains how prepositions, when used as prefixes (inseparable prepositions), greatly expand a language's vocabulary and precision. He illustrates this by showing how various prefixes modify the root verb "to put" to create distinct meanings like admit, commit, and permit.</summary>

->OF ADVERBS.<-

gradations of one and the same quality, its nuances.
These words exist. Thus, when one says *to write well*, *to write badly*, *to write quickly*, *to write slowly*, *to write superbly*, all these words—*well*, *badly*, *quickly*, *slowly*, *superbly*—offer as many different nuances of the state or the action called *writing*.

The Latins call these words **AD-VERBS** <note>original: *AD-VERBES*; from the Latin *ad* (to/beside) and *verbum* (word/verb)</note>, that is to say, words destined to modify **VERBS**, because they more usually accompany verbs; there are even some that only ever go with Verbs.

There are some that serve more particularly to mark the nuances of adjectives; these are the ones used to mark degrees of comparison:
*More rich*, *very rich*, *excessively rich*, *less rich*, *little rich*.

One can distinguish two kinds of Adverbs: those that express gradations like the latter, and those that designate the manner in which one does a thing, and which can consequently be rendered through other phrasings by means of prepositions. Such

These latter Adverbs can therefore be called **ELLIPTICAL** <note>In linguistics, an "ellipse" or "elliptical" form is one where words are omitted but understood; here, the author suggests a single adverb replaces a longer phrase.</note>. One recognizes them especially when, reading Works in foreign Languages, one sees adverbs there that one is obliged to render by a preposition and an adjective in one's own Language, and *vice-versa* <note>original: *vice-versa*; Latin for "the other way around"</note> formulas expressed by a preposition and an adjective that one renders in one's own Language by adverbs.

We shall therefore call these formulas, such as *in a wise manner*, *with wisdom*, and any other similar ones, *adverbial formulas*, since they fulfill the function of adverbs.

Such is the essential difference between the preposition and the adverb: the preposition marks the relationship reigning between two objects, *the sun is above the horizon*; whereas the adverb indicates only the relationship perceived between a name and a quality; *he rises with rapidity* to the highest honors.

In the second case, on the contrary, since the word accompanying it designates only a quality, this word can be combined with the preposition into a single term without the speech losing its clarity, because no essential part disappears: the word that replaces the other two—the adverb—is a qualifying term, just as it was when the idea it presents was expressed by two words.

One even had to resort to this turn of phrase to make the thought more vivid, and to reduce the monotony that would reign through a too frequent use of prepositions.

->*The Adverb is an ellipsis.*<-

The Adverb is therefore nothing but an ellipsis <note>In linguistics, an "ellipsis" occurs when words are omitted from a sentence but their meaning remains understood. In the first case, the noun loses everything that normally accompanies it as a noun and stands alone. In the second, the adjective appears alone with an ending that takes the place of the omitted noun. In the third, the noun and the adjective unite to form a single word.

From this arise these expressions: *to write poorly*, *to write obligingly*, *to write for a long time*.

*POORLY* <note>Original: *mal*. ### *Their origin.*

Every adverb comes from a noun taken in one of these three elliptical senses <note>Referring to the "shortened" versions of phrases discussed on the previous page.</note>.

NOTHING <note>Original: *Rien*.</note> is the Latin *rem*, meaning *thing*: *there is nothing*, is a way of saying *there is not any thing*: just as we use the word PERSON <note>Original: *personne*.</note>, to designate the absence of any human being.

TOO MUCH <note>Original: *Trop*.</note> comes from the word *troppo* <note>Italian for "too much."</note> or *troupe* <note>French for "a troop" or "group."</note>; designating a *multitude*.

ENOUGH <note>Original: *Assez*.</note> comes from *fat* <note>Referring to the Latin root *satis*.</note>, pronounced *faz*; which signifies *satiety* or *sufficiency*, joined to the preposition *a* [to/at]. *Assez* is therefore, word for word, *to sufficiency*.

Those ending in MENT <note>The standard adverbial suffix in French, equivalent to the English *-ly*.</note> come from the Latin *mente* <note>Ablative form of *mens*, meaning "with the mind" or "in the spirit of."</note>; meaning mind or manner; *prudemment* <note>prudently</note> or *prudentimente*, or in a prudent manner; *sage-ment* <note>wisely</note>, in a wise manner; *obligeamment* <note>obligingly</note>, in an obliging manner.

## CHAPTER III.

->*Of Conjunctions.*<-

If the Pictures of Speech <note>The author uses the term "Tableaux de la Parole" to describe language as a series of mental images or scenes painted for the listener.</note> were only composed of two related objects, or if it were never necessary to determine the meaning of the words that paint each of these objects by using other words, the Parts of Speech we have just discussed would be sufficient to link all the portions that enter into the Pictures of our ideas; but the presentation of our ideas is rarely limited to such simplicity. It is often necessary to add phrase to phrase, picture to picture: we therefore need words that link these various portions; and these words must be of the greatest simplicity; they must have the speed of a gesture; they must take nothing away from the conciseness of language; so that, in order to unite two Pictures, they do not form a third one between the two.

These words exist; they are called CONJUNCTIONS, a word formed from the preposition <note>The text cuts off here, but the author is preparing to explain that "conjunction" comes from the Latin *cum* (with) and *jungere* (to join).</note>

<sig>R r <note>A signature mark used by early printers to help binders assemble the pages of a book in the correct order.</note></sig>

<summary>This page introduces conjunctions as essential linguistic tools for linking complex ideas and "pictures" of thought without adding unnecessary clutter to speech. The author emphasizes that conjunctions must be simple and rapid, functioning almost like gestures to connect distinct phrases.</summary>
<keywords>conjunctions, grammar, linguistics, Pictures of Speech, Parts of Speech, sentence structure</keywords>

--- Page 331 ---
<meta>This page continues the etymological breakdown of the word "conjunction" from the previous page and defines its specific role in grammar, distinguishing it from verbs and prepositions.</meta>

<page-num>263</page-num>
<header>UNIVERSAL GRAMMAR</header>

*from* *cum*, <note>Latin: "with"</note> which means *with*, and from the word *junctio*, <note>Latin: "joining"</note> as if one were to say, *words with which union is formed.*

A <term>CONJUNCTION</term> is therefore a word which, from several <term>Pictures of speech</term>, <note>The author uses "Tableaux de la parole" to describe language as a sequence of mental images or scenes.</note> creates a single whole.

It differs from verbs and prepositions, which also serve to link, in that the verb links the noun and the adjective, or the object and its quality; the preposition links related objects, and the conjunction links the very Pictures of speech themselves.

->*Copulative Conjunctions.*<-

One can reduce <term>Copulative Conjunctions</term> <note>Conjunctions used to connect words or phrases in a list or sequence.</note> to three, which in French are AND, NOR, and OR. <note>original: "ET, NI, OU."</note>

The first unites phrases.
The second separates them, excluding them from a single group.
The third leaves a choice; it is a partial result.

Thus we would say:
> Take this flower AND this one.
> Take NEITHER this flower NOR this one. <note>The original French *ni* functions as "neither/nor" to exclude both options.</note>

<summary>This page defines the conjunction as a grammatical tool that joins multiple "pictures of speech" into a unified whole. It distinguishes the conjunction's role from that of verbs and prepositions, and categorizes the three primary copulative conjunctions: and, nor, and or.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on copulative conjunctions (and, nor, or) from page 263, explaining the etymological and physiological origins of these specific words to show that their meanings are not arbitrary but rooted in the nature of sound and human gesture.</meta>

<page-num>164</page-num>
<header>NATURAL HISTORY OF SPEECH.</header>

->Take this flower or this one.<-

These words were not chosen at random to serve as a link between ideas; it was not by mere whim that the first of these conjunctions was intended to unite, the second to exclude, and the third to provide a choice: they owe this power to the very nature of the elements from which they are composed.

**And** <note>original: "Et"</note> was a derivative of the verb **E**, <note>The author posits "E" as a primitive root signifying existence or the act of being (similar to the Latin *est*), which inherently links a subject to a quality.</note> which already indicated the link of a quality with its object.

**Nor** <note>original: "Ni"</note> was formed from the nasal **N**, which has always signaled negation or refusal; this is a result of the repelling gesture that one naturally forms through the nasal sound. <note>The author suggests that the physical act of making an "N" sound mimics a physical gesture of rejection.</note>

**Or** <note>original: "Ou"</note> comes from the primitive root *ou*, which designates: 1st, a place different from the one in which one is currently located; and 2nd, a third party. By that very fact, it designates objects opposed to those of which one speaks; and consequently, the choice between incompatible objects.

It is incontestable that these instances of **THAT** link the words which precede them with those that follow: it is as if one said, *the book sent to me by you; the Author cited by you*, phrasings that our **Language** <note>French</note> cannot admit, as it forbids passive structures <note>The author is likely arguing that French prefers a direct "natural" order—Subject-Verb-Object—over the passive voice transformations common in Latin</note>.

This Conjunction was borrowed from the primitive *qhe* or *quhé*, which signified *link, cord, uniting power*. One could not better designate its value.

### UNIVERSAL GRAMMAR

1a. The Conjunction **but** <note>original: *mais*</note>, is a word designed to create a contrast with what has already been stated.

When we say, for example, *he spoke very well*, BUT *it was only to deceive*; it is as if we were saying: he spoke very well, we agree; but let us agree MOREOVER <note>original: *de PLUS*</note> that his only purpose in doing so is to deceive.

And this word was very well chosen, because it means *more*, and it is merely a variation of the Latin word *magis* <note>meaning "more" or "to a greater extent"</note>. In some Provinces, people still say, "I love him BUT <note>original: *MAIS*</note> than you," to mean *more than you*.

3rd. **FOR** <note>original: *CAR*</note>, is employed to provide a reason for what has been stated: and it was well chosen; this word originally meant *reason*, just as it meant *voice*, *word*, or *discourse*. From this root came these Latin words: *garrulus* <note>a talkative person or discourser</note>; *garrire* <note>to chatter or babble</note>; and *gerrae* <note>trifles or nonsense</note>.

The same is true for all the other Conjunctions, such as **now** <note>original: *or*</note> and **therefore** <note>original: *donc*</note>, the Latin **as/that** <note>original: *ut*</note>, etc. As one can— <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence, likely continuing with "perceive" or "notice" on the following page.</note>

## CHAPTER IV.

### Of Interjections.

Under the name of **INTERJECTIONS**, we include those exclamatory sounds wrung from us by the feelings that affect us, and through which they manifest themselves outside of us; those cries of pleasure or pain, of joy or sadness, of approval or contempt—in a word, of sensitivity—which we utter as a result of the sensations we experience, whatever their cause.

Though they vary little among themselves in terms of sound, Interjections are infinitely varied by the degree of force with which they are pronounced, by the speed with which they follow one another, by the changes they cause in facial expression <note>original: *phyſionomie*</note>, and above all, by the tone given to them. Under the various forms they take, they bur- <note>The word is cut off; it likely completes as "burst forth" or "shine through" (*éclatent*).</note>

<vocab>Universal Grammar, Comparative Grammar, Interjections, exclamatory sounds, feelings, sensations, facial expression, tone</vocab>

<keywords>interjections, universal grammar, emotions, sensations, phonetics, vocal expression, non-verbal communication</keywords>

—st forth the cry of pain, sounds of admiration, the various types of laughter, etc.

They were very aptly named *Interjections*, from two Latin words <note>original: *inter* (between) and *jacere* (to throw)</note> which signify *uttered at intervals*, because they are indeed uttered at intervals, as if by jolts, and because they are scattered among the other parts of speech without being linked to any of them.

Such is the essential difference between the other Parts of Speech and Interjections: the former create a portrait of ideas, the expression of which communicates them and makes them the property of those who hear them; **whereas the Interjection is merely a sign of what is happening within the person who lets it escape.** If, by means of it, a person makes others understand that they are experiencing a vivid and tumultuous agitation <note>The author uses "agitation" here to mean a state of emotional arousal or disturbance.</note> at that moment, they cannot make this agitation pass into the others' souls: the listeners are warned that one of their fellow beings is vividly agitated, but this agitation does not become their own.

An admirable effect of Nature, which through these

Through the Interjection, our sensations are communicated to our fellow beings to the degree necessary to move them to take part in them; yet not so much that they are affected to the same degree. If sensations were followed by such an effect, they would cease to be an advantage; they would become the most fatal gift that could be given to mankind. A cry of alarm or of pain startles, but it does not shatter us: it does not strip away the strength required to fly to the aid of the unfortunate soul imploring our assistance.

The spirit of society directs and moderates the use of Interjections. Among Savage Peoples <note>In the 18th century, "Savage Peoples" was a common term used by philosophers to describe indigenous or tribal societies, often idealizing them as stoic or "close to nature."</note>, whose lives are very harsh, greatness of soul consists in being the master of one's pain: anyone who would utter a cry in the most cruel torments would be dishonored as a coward. Among civilized Peoples, laughter is only for the lighthearted and flighty youth; and frequent admiration <note>original: *admiration*; in this period, the term specifically referred to the state of wonder or surprise at the unknown.</note> is for those who have seen nothing.

The Parts of Speech that form the first Class are Prepositions, Adverbs, Conjunctions, and Interjections; all that used to be encompassed in a single word under the general name of *Particles*. <note>In traditional grammar, "particles" referred to any short, invariable words that did not fit into the primary categories of noun or verb.</note>

The Parts of Speech that form the second class are the six others: the Noun, the Article, the Pronoun, the Adjective, the Participle, and the Verb.

The words of this second Class are called for this reason *declinable*, <term>declinable: from the Latin "declinare" (to bend or lean), referring to words that change their endings or forms</term> that is to say, words that pass successively through various states; while the others are called for the opposite reason *indeclinable*. We are therefore concerned here with the Parts of Speech called *Declinable*.

### *Division of the Parts of Speech that are Declinable.*

The Parts of Speech that undergo various modifications are subdivided into two other Classes.

1st. Words that undergo various modifications according to the number of individuals they designate. <note>In 18th-century grammar, "individuals" refers to the specific people or things a word represents, which determines if a word is singular or plural.</note>

2nd. Words that undergo various modifications, not only according to the number of individuals they designate, but also according to their relationships with actions and with the time in which these actions take place.

The first Class contains the first five Parts of Speech, or words that are simply *declinable*. <note>Based on the previous page, these five parts are the Noun, Article, Pronoun, Adjective, and Participle.</note>

The second Class contains *Verbs*, or words that are conjugated.

This Book will therefore deal with Declension and Conjugation—<term>Declension and Conjugation: "Declension" refers to the changing forms of nouns or adjectives, while "Conjugation" refers to the changing forms of verbs.</term> words almost as intimidating as they are common.

### *General Cause of these Modifications.*

If words had only a single function to fulfill in the tableaus of speech <note>The author uses the metaphor of a "tableau" (a painting or structured scene) to describe how sentences are composed of different elements working together.</note>, they would never need any modification; they would all be indeclinable. <note>In grammar, "indeclinable" refers to words that never change their spelling or ending, regardless of how they are used.</note> But if any among them is charged with various functions, it will necessarily be required, in order for it to fulfill them, to take on the qualities without which these various functions would not take place.

We need only, therefore, cast a glance over the definitions of the Parts of Speech to perceive at once those whose functions are many, and those which have but one: those which are declinable and those which are not.

The *Adverb*, which limits itself to designating a modification of Verbs; the *Preposition*, which indicates a simple relationship between two nouns; the *Conjunction*, which is used only to unite sentences; and the *Interjection*, which indicates a sentiment of the soul <note>In 18th-century linguistic theory, the "soul" often refers to the seat of immediate emotions or internal states, which interjections like "Ouch!" or "Alas!" express directly.</note>, will never be in a position to be diversely modified—

<keywords>functional grammar, indeclinable words, parts of speech, adverbs, prepositions, conjunctions, interjections, linguistic theory</keywords>

-fied, since they have only one function to fulfill.

It is not so for the other Parts of Speech. Obliged to face a great number of different objects, they can only achieve this by taking a new form each time.

The **Noun** indicates all objects of the same species <note>In 18th-century grammar, "species" refers to a general category or class of things.</note>; but these objects can be taken one by one, or several together; it will therefore be necessary for the noun to vary, according to whether it indicates one or several individuals.

The **Pronoun**, being in the same situation, will undergo the same modifications.

The **Article**, the **Adjective**, and the **Participle**, forced to follow the lead <note>The original French uses "l'impulsion," suggesting these words are "driven" or "pulled" by the nouns they modify.</note> of **Nouns** and **Pronouns**, and to conform to them, will be obliged to imitate them in the changes they undergo.

The **Verb**, designating the time of our actions—a time which varies constantly, and which is subdivided into a multitude of portions—will be obliged, in order to depict these varieties, to clothe itself in a multitude of different forms.

## CHAPTER ONE.
### Of Genders & Numbers.

What we have already said regarding these two subjects in the Chapter on the Noun means that we shall be very brief here concerning them.

Genders are designated in French in three ways; either by a different ending for qualifying words, such as *son* and *daughter* <note>original: "fils & fille"</note>, etc.; or by specific terminations; such as *-eau* for masculine nouns, like *hamlet*, *boat*, *muzzle* <note>original: "hameau, bateau, museau"</note>; and *-eille* and *-aille* for feminine nouns, like *bee*, *wall*, *rock-work* <note>original: "abeille, muraille, rocaille"</note>; etc. And thirdly, by the articles, which always possess two genders.

Various languages also have a third gender to designate objects with no relation to these two genders: and this is what is called the NEUTER GENDER.

When we employ an adjective without a determined relationship to any gender, it can be said that we are using a phras- <note>The word is cut off by the page break; it likely completes as "tournure" (phrasing or expression) on the following page.</note>

<vocab>grammar, nouns, genders, numbers, masculine, feminine, neutre, adjective, French</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces the classification of genders and numbers in French, explaining how gender is identified through word endings and articles, and mentions the existence of the neuter gender in other languages.</summary>
<keywords>French grammar, gender, number, masculine and feminine endings, neuter gender, linguistic structure</keywords>

--- Page 352 ---
<meta>This page completes the word "tournure" (phrasing/expression) from the previous page and continues the discussion on grammatical gender before moving into the concept of "Number," specifically the Dual.</meta>

<page-num>282</page-num>
<header>NATURAL HISTORY OF SPEECH.</header>

neutral phrasing, such as this sentence: *everything you do is good*; for one cannot say that this word *everything* <note>original: "tout"</note> relates to any specific gender; and among the Romans <note>original: "Latins"</note>, every such word is constantly rendered in the neuter.

In French, the third-person Pronouns <term>Pronouns|Words used to replace a noun, such as he, she, it, or they.</term> are the only ones capable of a different gender, depending on whether one speaks of a man or a woman.

We sometimes have words with two genders, depending on whether they designate a different object. The word *Orgue* <note>Organ</note>, masculine in the singular because it is viewed as a musical instrument, is feminine in the plural because it then presents the idea of a collection of flutes.

As for Numbers; several Languages, such as Hebrew, Greek, ancient Teutonic, Slavonic, etc., have, besides the singular and the plural, a third Number called the Duel <term>Dual|A grammatical number used in some languages to refer specifically to two of something, distinct from the singular (one) or plural (three or more).</term>, which designated only two objects, and which was used above all for parts of the body that are in pairs, such as the eyes, the hands, etc.

<header>UNIVERSAL GRAMMAR</header>

As for our plural ending in *S*, we hold it from the Romans <note>original: "Latins"</note> and the Greeks, who substituted it for the oriental ending in *ei*; this latter was an abbreviation of their great and primitive ending in *IM* <note>The author is likely referring to the Hebrew masculine plural ending *-im*.</note>, a very energetic ending, since it designates multitude and immensity. This ending formed the *IM-us* of the Romans, a word-ending that offers these various significations, and which became no less energetically the mark of the superlative <term>Superlative|The form of an adjective or adverb expressing the highest degree of a quality, such as "most" or "very."</term>, as in our words *Excellentiſſime* <note>Most Excellent</note> and *Révérendiſſime* <note>Most Reverend</note>.

## CHAPTER II.

### OF CASES.

CASES <term>Cases|The various forms a word takes—often by changing its ending—to show its grammatical function, such as the subject, object, or owner in a sentence.</term> consist of the changes experienced by the last syllable of a noun, independently of its gender and number, so that this noun may fulfill the various roles it must occupy in the patterns of speech.

Indeed, every Noun and every Pronoun marks—

<summary>The author describes the evolution of plural endings from Semitic 'IM' to the Latin 'S' and relates these endings to the superlative form. He then introduces the concept of grammatical 'Cases' as functional changes to the ends of words.</summary>

--- Page 354 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of how nouns and pronouns function within a sentence. The previous page ended mid-sentence, noting that every Noun and Pronoun marks the way it—</meta>

<page-num>284</page-num>
<header>NATURAL HISTORY OF SPEECH</header>

—proceeds alone, or follows another; is active, or passive; designates an agent, a goal, or a means; it fulfills, in a word, several different functions in the patterns of speech. It must therefore be characterized in these various cases by features that leave no obscurity regarding its use. In this sentence, for example:

->I inform you that the General has<-
->ordered me to hold myself ready.<-

The first-person pronoun fulfills three different functions, each designated by a particular case.

It is first active, which is what *I* <note>original: "je"</note> designates.

It is passive, or the object of the action, in:
->to hold *MYSELF* <note>original: "me"</note> ready.<-

It designates a terminative <term>Terminative|A grammatical term used here to describe the recipient of an action, similar to what we now call the dative case (e.g., "to me").</term> in, *ordered me* <note>original: "m'a ordonné"</note>, meaning "ordered to me."

These variations in Pronouns are common to us as well as to the Romans <note>original: "Latins"</note> and the Greeks.

Thus, we have arrived at the origin of Cases—those Cases which produce such a brilliant effect in the languages of these two peoples, and which we only make use of for Pronouns.

<meta>This page continues the logical progression from the previous discussion regarding the functional necessity of grammatical cases. Having established that pronouns change form based on their role (agent vs. recipient), the author now explains how this principle was extended from pronouns to nouns in certain languages.</meta>

<page-num>285</page-num>
<header>UNIVERSAL GRAMMAR</header>

<margin>30</margin>
It was Nature that led to the invention of Cases: they existed because it was impossible for them not to exist; and once they were established, Mankind did nothing more than extend or restrict their use.

It was impossible, as we have seen, for that same pronoun which designated an active person to also designate them as passive: it was necessarily required to vary the pronoun, depending on whether it fulfilled one or the other of these functions: hence ***I*** and ***ME*** <note>original: "JE & ME"</note>, ***YOU*** and ***YOU*** <note>original: "TU & TE"; in older English, this distinction was mirrored by "Thou" and "Thee".</note>, etc. And these varieties were called ***CASES***; because they depict the various cases, the various circumstances, in which these persons find themselves.

But, since Cases were thus given to Pronouns, according to whether they designated persons in an active or passive state, there was only one step left to take to extend this distinction to Nouns: it only remained to pronounce their endings differently, according to whether they were active or passive, agents or objects of actions, subjects or objects in the **Tableaus of speech** <note>original: "Tableaux de la parole"; a metaphorical term used by 18th-century grammarians to describe how a sentence "paints a picture" of an action or thought.</note>.

<vocab>Universal Grammar, invention of Cases, pronouns, nouns, active state, passive state, agents, subjects, objects, Tableaus of speech</vocab>

<summary>The author argues that grammatical cases arise naturally from the need to distinguish between the performer and the receiver of an action. He explains that while this began with pronouns, it logically extended to the varying endings of nouns in many languages.</summary>
<keywords>grammar, linguistic evolution, grammatical cases, pronouns, nouns, sentence structure, universal grammar, linguistic history</keywords>

--- Page 356 ---
<meta>This page follows the discussion of how grammatical cases (word endings indicating function) originated in pronouns and were subsequently applied to nouns to allow for greater linguistic flexibility.</meta>

<page-num>286</page-num>
<header>NATURAL HISTORY OF SPEECH.</header>

This invention of **Cases**, or rather this transfer of them from **Pronouns** to **Nouns**, was a stroke of genius to which **Greek** and **Latin** owed a great part of the beauties admired in their **Languages**. From that moment on, words no longer being bound to a fixed position, one could choose the place where they would produce the greatest effect; from this, there necessarily resulted **Tableaus** <note>The author uses the term "Tableaus" to describe the visual or mental "picture" a sentence paints through its arrangement of words.</note> that were more perfect, more harmonious, more varied, and more certain in their effects. One could bring a single word to the foreground of the **Tableau** in its turn, or cause it to recede, depending on whether one wished to focus more or less attention upon it. These were so many resources provided to the **imagination** and the **taste** of **Writers**, who were thus able to lead their Reader from surprise to surprise, and excite his curiosity until the very end by keeping it always suspended.

Without this advantage, one of our **Poets** would not have been able to say:

> ->Sad remnant of our Kings,<-
> ->Dear and last flower of such a beautiful stem,<-
<note>original: "Triste reste de nos Rois, / Chere & derniere fleur d’une tige si belle." These lines are from Jean Racine's 1691 tragedy, *Athalie*. The author argues that even in French—which has fewer cases than Latin—the poetic placement of words creates a specific emotional "Tableau."</note>

### On the number of Cases and their Names.

The number of **Cases** varies singularly from one **Language** to another. Those that count the fewest have three, such as **Arabic**. The **Peruvian** <note>likely referring to Quechua or Aymara, the indigenous languages of the Andes</note> counts, on the contrary, as many as there are prepositions. Between these two extremes are a great number of intermediaries: **German**, which admits four cases; **Greek**, five; **Latin**, six; the **Languages of Malabar** <note>referring to the Dravidian languages of southwest India, such as Malayalam or Tamil</note>, eight; **Armenian**,

As for the **French Language**, our ancient **Grammarians** admitted six cases into it, even for **Nouns**, just as in **Latin**; our modern **Grammarians** admit none, and with good reason—at least for **Nouns**; but they have wrongly abolished them in regards to **Pronouns** <note>The author likely refers to how French pronouns still change form based on their role—e.g., "je" (subject) vs. "me" (object)—even though nouns like "rose" do not.</note>.

The number of **Cases** can be determined in two ways: by form and by meaning; and this number is almost always more considerable relative to meaning than it is relative to form.

The first **Declension** <term>Declension: A group of nouns that share the same pattern of endings to show their role in a sentence.</term> of the **Latins**, *Rosa*, the **Rose**, which has six cases in terms of meaning, has only three cases by form; three of these cases end in *a*, and two in *æ*. But as the *a* of the **Ablative** <term>Ablative: A case typically indicating movement away from or the means by which an action is performed.</term> is long <note>In Latin, the Nominative "a" was a short sound, while the Ablative "a" was held longer, a distinction that changed the word's grammatical function.</note>, one can admit four cases relative to the ending. It is the same for all **Latin Declensions**: the **Dative** <term>Dative: The case indicating the indirect object (the "to" or "for" whom).</term> and the **Ablative** are almost always identical, just as are the **Vocative** <term>Vocative: The case used for directly addressing or calling someone.</term> and the **Nominative** <term>Nominative: The case used for the subject of a sentence.</term>.

Whether one says, *the father loves the son* <note>original Latin: "pater amat filium"</note>, or *the son, the father loves* <note>original Latin: "filium amat pater"—noting that the case endings preserve the meaning regardless of order</note>, it is all the same; one always sees that *the son* <note>original: "filium"</note> is the object loved, and that *the father* <note>original: "pater"</note> is the object who loves. But in French <note>The original text uses "François."</note> we could never give these words—*the father loves his son*—any other place than the one they occupy through this arrangement.

### On the Ablative, or Circumstantial Case.

These three kinds of Pictures <term>Tableaux: In Enlightenment linguistics, this refers to the mental "scene" or structure created by a sentence that represents a complete thought.</term>—the Enunciative, the Active, and the Passive—thus have this in common: they are all composed of a Verb and its subject. However, they differ in that the Active Picture is not only composed of a subject, but additionally offers an object upon which this subject acts. It thus unites an Active Case and a Passive Case, a nominative and an accusative; while the other two are deprived of the latter.

A Passive Picture being only the inverse of an Active Picture, everything found in the latter must be found in the former, but under a different form. What was the object in the Active Picture becomes the subject

--- Page 362 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 291 regarding the transformation of sentence structures. It explains how the object of an active sentence becomes the subject of a passive one, while the original subject shifts into a "circumstantial" role, specifically identifying this as the function of the Latin ablative case.</meta>

<page-num>292</page-num>
<header>NATURAL HISTORY OF SPEECH.</header>

in the passive Picture <term>Tableau: As noted previously, this refers to the mental "scene" or structure of a sentence.</term>; and what was the subject in the active Picture becomes a circumstance in the passive Picture. This circumstance is recognized in French by means of the preposition *by* <note>original French: "par"</note>; and in Latin, by means of the case called the **ablative**.

Thus, one can say equally:
*Caesar subjugated Rome, or Rome was subjugated by Caesar*.

*Caesar*, who was the active subject in the first sentence, is a circumstantial element in the second: in Latin, this would be an ABLATIVE; and this case was very well named, from the two words *ab* (meaning "by" or "from") and *latus* (meaning "carried" or "transported"); since it designates the circumstances by which we are transported from one state to another. With *Caesar* being in the ablative, one sees that it is by him that Rome was transported into a state of dependence, whereas she was free before.

The ablative is always accompanied by the preposition *a* <note>The Latin preposition "a" or "ab" marks the "agent" (the doer) in a passive sentence.</note> when it designates a proper noun; but when it designates a simple circumstance, the preposition is omitted.

## Of the DATIVE or Terminative.

Our actions are usually related to some object which is their goal or their end point: this is therefore a fourth case. For example, when we say, *he has a piece of good news announced to me by his brother*; this is a Scene <term>Tableau: The author views a sentence as a visual scene or "picture" where each word plays a role.</term> where the subject is *he*; the object is *news*; the circumstantial element is *his brother*; and the terminative <note>The author uses "terminative" for the dative because it represents the "terminus" or end point of the action.</note> is *me*; for it designates the person to whom this news is announced.

### Vocative, or Interjective Case.

The Vocative is the Case by which one addresses a person, designating them by their name or by some epithet. In saying, *take a seat, CINNA*; *Cinna* is a name in the Vocative <note>This is a likely reference to a famous line from Pierre Corneille's play *Cinna*.</note>. In saying, *what have you done, unnatural* FATHER, *ambitious* SLAVE? These words, *unnatural father*, *ambitious slave*, are epithets in the Vocative.

We call it the Interjective Case because it is the result of an exclamation, like Interjections; and because, similar to those, it does not link with any portion of the Pic-

->*Genitive, or Completive Case.*<-

A noun is not always sufficient to determine the object it must depict: one must then have recourse to another noun, which, coming to the aid of the former, completes the sense that had been started. In these examples: *the violence of the storm*, *the breath of the zephyrs* <note>Zephyrs are gentle west winds, often personified in classical literature and mythology.</note>, *the weight of years*, *the brightness of the sun*, *the son of Philip* <note>A likely reference to Philip II of Macedon, the father of Alexander the Great.</note>; the words *violence*, *breath*, *weight*, *brightness*, and *son* begin a meaning that remains suspended until one has pronounced the words *storm*, *zephyrs*, *years*, *sun*, and *Philip*, which are linked to the first words by the preposition *of*. They therefore form a Completive Case, since they complete the sense already begun. In Latin, they are recognized by a particular ending, which forms the Case called the *Genitive*, because it always designates origin or genealogy <note>original: "Genitif." The term derives from the Latin *gignere* (to beget), reflecting the idea of the "source" or "parent" of the first noun.</note>. In the preceding examples, the objects designated by these words—*the violence*, *the breath*, *the weight*, *the brightness*, *the son*—are the effect of *the storm*,

Sometimes *of* <note>original: "de"</note> appears to be completive <term>completive: a term the author uses for words that complete the meaning of a previous noun, similar to the genitive case</term>, and it is only so by ellipsis <term>ellipsis: the omission of words that are understood by the reader without being explicitly stated</term>, when one says, for example, *the City of Paris*; likewise in Latin *City* and *Paris* are in the same case. This is because in French we have suppressed a phrase between these two names that is unnecessary to express; it is as if one said, *the City which bears the name of Paris*. The Latins, who put *City* and *Paris* in the same case, form the ellipsis in another way: it is as if they said, *the City which is called Paris*.

### *Cases of French Pronouns for the First Person Singular.*

**I** <note>original: "JE"</note>, Active Case; **I** do what you are going to tell me.

**I** <note>original: "JE"</note>, Interrogative Case; did **I** hear it correctly?

**ME**, Passive Case; nothing can turn **ME** away from this project.

**ME**, Terminative Case <note>The author uses "terminative" to describe the case indicating the end-point or recipient of an action, similar to the dative case</note>; he deigned to **GIVE ME** <note>original: "ME donner"</note> this token of his faith.

**ME** <note>original: "MOI"</note>, Interjective Case; who, **ME**!

**ME** <note>original: "Moi"</note>, Passive Case; follow **me**.

**ME** <note>original: "Moi"</note>, Terminative Case <note>indicating the recipient or end-point of an action</note>; give **me** help.

**ME** <note>original: "Moi"</note>, Circumstantial Case <note>used after a preposition to indicate the circumstances of an action</note>; it is by **me** that he was saved.

**ME** <note>original: "Moi"</note>, Completive Case <note>similar to the genitive, used to complete a phrase</note>; they came in crowds around **me**.

The same applies to other Pronouns; they give rise to similar observations; and both provide tangible proof of the variety that can be introduced into the Tableaus of Speech <note>the author's term for the systematic arrangement of language</note> by means of Cases. One might say that our Language, by multiplying the forms of Pronouns, sought to compensate for the loss it suffers from the lack of Cases in regards to Nouns <note>unlike Latin or Greek, French nouns do not change their endings to show their grammatical role; the author argues that pronouns "make up" for this lack</note>.

The true reason, however, which caused pronouns to multiply to this extent in French, is that it was necessary for these Cases not to look like Cases, and for them to be subject to a fixed position like all French words. Thus, the pronouns that can precede the Verb cannot follow it; and those that can follow it cannot precede it: there has thus...

# PART II.
## ON CONJUGATION.

### FIRST SECTION.
#### *ON MOODS*

---

### CHAPTER ONE.
#### *Definition & Division.*

The <term>Tenses</term> <note>original: "Tems," an archaic spelling of "Temps." In this context, it refers to the grammatical timing of an action.</note> that we have already examined all represent existence in an absolute and positive manner. However, one can also consider existence as being dependent upon some other existence; one can also consider it in relation to people in general, without relating it to any person in particular: hence the various Forms, which are given the name of <term>MOODS</term> <note>In grammar, "moods" (or modes) express the speaker's attitude toward what is being said (e.g., a statement of fact, a wish, or a command).</note>.

The **Verb** therefore has an absolute and positive Mode; it is called the **INDICATIVE**. It is this mode whose Tenses we have already seen.

<term>conjugation</term>, <term>moods</term>, <term>indicative</term>, <term>existence</term>, <term>verb</term>, <term>grammar</term>, <term>Tenses</term>

<summary>This page introduces the second part of the treatise, defining "Moods" as the various forms a verb takes to express whether an action is absolute or dependent on another condition.</summary>
<keywords>conjugation, grammatical moods, indicative mode, verb theory, linguistic structure</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter One regarding the various "Moods" of the verb. Having established the Indicative as the absolute mode on the previous page, the author now categorizes the remaining moods into Relative and Abstract groups.</meta>

<page-num>299</page-num>
<header>UNIVERSAL GRAMMAR</header>

It indicates existence considered in itself. <note>This refers to the Indicative mood defined on the previous page, which states facts directly.</note>

Next come four RELATIVE Moods and three ABSTRACT or INDEFINITE Moods, at least in the Latin language.

#### *Second kind of Moods: the Relative Moods.*

These Moods are four in number, as we have just mentioned.

1. THE <term>IMPERATIVE</term> <note>Used for commands or requests; from the Latin *imperare*, "to command."</note>; *be wise*. It marks an existence that will take place as a consequence of the order given for it.

2. THE <term>OPTATIVE</term> <note>Used to express a wish or desire; from the Latin *optare*, "to wish."</note>; *would that I were wise!* It marks an existence that one would wish to be.

3. THE <term>CONDITIONAL</term>; *I would be wise, if I knew the means*. It marks an existence that depends on a condition.

4. THE <term>SUBJUNCTIVE</term> <note>From the Latin *subjungere*, "to subjoin" or "attach." This mood is used in clauses that are dependent on another verb.</note>; *he said* THAT I MIGHT BE <note>original: "QUE JE FUSSE," the imperfect subjunctive of the verb "to be."</note> *wise*. It marks an existence that is subordinate to another.

# CHAPTER II.
## ON THE RELATIVE MOODS.

---

### ARTICLE ONE.

->*On the Imperative Mood.*<-

THE <term>IMPERATIVE</term> <note>From the Latin *imperare*, meaning "to command."</note> expresses existence as taking place in consequence of an order given.
*Do, come, leave, are Imperatives.*
This Mood has, in the French Language, only one Person in the singular, which is the second: *do, come.* <note>original: "fais, viens."</note>
It has two in the plural, the first & the second: *let us do, do: let us come, come.* <note>original: "faisons, faites : venons, venez."</note>
It cannot have a first person in the singular. <note>The author implies that one does not issue a direct command to oneself in the same way one addresses another.</note>
As for the third Person in the singular & in the plural, they are expressed by the same formula as the <term>subjunctive</term> <note>A mood used to express various states of unreality such as wish, emotion, or possibility.</note>, *let him do, let them do.* <note>original: "qu'il fasse, qu'ils fassent."</note> One then adopts an imperative tone; whereas in the subjunctive, the tone is weaker & more narrative.

<vocab>Imperative, Mood, French Language, Person, singular, plural, subjunctive</vocab>

<summary>This page defines the Imperative mood as the language of commands and explains its specific grammatical structure in French, including its lack of a first-person singular form.</summary>
<keywords>Imperative mood, French grammar, verb conjugation, commands, relative moods, linguistics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the detailed analysis of the Imperative mood, moving from its basic definition in French to its specific forms in Latin and Greek, as well as its application to reflexive verbs.</meta>

<page-num>301</page-num>
<header>UNIVERSAL GRAMMAR 301</header>

The Latins have two ways of expressing this third person; one, as in French, by the subjunctive; the other, by the termination *-eto*. <note>The Latin future imperative ending *-eto* (e.g., *esto*, "thou shalt be") was used primarily in formal legal codes and edicts to indicate a standing command.</note> This latter form thus carries all the force of the imperative, while the other is weaker, being more narrative than imperative.

This Mood is composed of two Tenses <note>The author uses the word "Tems" (times) to refer to grammatical tenses.</note> in our Language, both of which are future; but one of which may be regarded as present relative to the other; & the latter, as past relative to a specific future point in time.

PRESENT, *do: do this now.* <note>original: "fais : fais cela à présent."</note>
PRETERIT, *have [it] done: have it done when I arrive.* <note>original: "aie fait : aie fait quand j’arriverai."</note>

<term>Reflexive Verbs</term> <note>Verbs where the action is performed by the subject upon themselves, such as "to wash oneself."</note>, such as *to dress oneself*; *to rejoice*, form these two tenses in this manner. *Dress yourself*, *dress yourselves*. *Be dressed*, *be dressed* [plural]. <note>original: "Habille-toi, habillez-vous. Sois habillé, soyez habillés."</note>

The Greeks have four tenses in the Imperative; which are almost all rendered in translation by the preterit: it disfigures a Language to thus confound such very distinct tenses. These Imperative tenses refer to various epochs; in deter- <note>The text cuts off mid-word ("déterminant") as it moves to the next page.</note>

It is the Imperative that Roman Legislators employed in the promulgation of their Laws. <note>The author refers to the use of the "future imperative" in Latin legal texts, which suggests a command intended to remain in force perpetually.</note>

The Hebrews, by a formula even more pressing, employed the second person of the future. <note>This refers to the Ten Commandments, which in Hebrew use the future tense (e.g., "Thou shalt not kill") to express a definitive prohibition.</note>

This is because the Imperative is only in the future; so that the future tense can serve for the Imperative: thus the Greeks sometimes used the Imperative instead of the future.

As for their Laws, they were stated by the <term>Infinitive</term> <note>The basic form of a verb, such as "to do" or "to be."</note>; but these words were implied: *it is ordered to*.

--- Page 373 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the nature of the Imperative mood, moving from its functional use in laws (discussed on the previous page) to its historical and linguistic origins as the most "primitive" part of the verb.</meta>

<page-num>303</page-num>
<header>UNIVERSAL GRAMMAR</header>

Of all the portions of the Verb, the **Imperative** is the first that would have presented itself to mankind: indeed, one was in the position of commanding, praying, and requesting, before one was in the position of narrating what one had done. Therefore, this tense is the simplest of all, and the root of all the others: it is always a radical and primitive noun, employed as a verb.

It is therefore not surprising that in all Languages the Imperative is the simplest tense, even in **Hebrew**, where it consists of a single syllable like primitive words, even when the Hebrew Verbs to which it belongs are composed of two syllables. It is because they neglected this observation that scholars have misunderstood the relationship between many Oriental and Western words; they did not see that these Hebrew Verbs, *to fix a day* <note>Hebrew: יעד; original transliteration: *I'hid*</note>, *to grow* <note>Hebrew: חיל; original transliteration: *I'hyl*</note>, and *to grow old* <note>Hebrew: ישן; original transliteration: *Isen*</note>, which in their imperative form become *'yd*, *hyl*, and *sen*, represent the primitive words *Id*, time; *Hyl* or *hul*, plant; and *sen*, old age; from which came the La- <note>Likely "Latin," continued on the following page.</note>

<summary>The author argues that the imperative is the oldest and most fundamental verb form because the need to command or request preceded the need to narrate. He uses Hebrew as evidence, showing how imperative forms are simple, single-syllable roots derived from primitive nouns.</summary>

## ARTICLE II.

### Of the OPTATIVE.

Our life is nothing but wishes; a prayer granted is often the fertile source of new wishes that follow in its wake, and the end of even the longest life often arrives in the midst of desires. But a wish is impatient: it was therefore necessary to give Verbs a particular form that would support this impatience; hence the optative mood <term>optative: from the Latin *optare*, "to wish"; a grammatical category used to express hopes or desires</term>. The Greeks, whose Language was so beautiful and so well-suited to the affections of the soul, dedicated a specific termination <note>a unique word ending</note> to it. Our own <note>French</note>, which is frightened by anything that feels too much like artifice or constraint, has contented itself with a small number of specific formulas.

These consist of accompanying the Verb with an optative exclamation: *Would to Heaven that!* <note>original: *plût au Ciel que !*</note> or a simple *that* <note>original: *que*</note>.

->Would to Heaven that I could doubt it!<-
->If only I could repay this important service!<- <note>original: *Que ne puis-je payer ce service important !*</note>

<meta>catchword: ARTICLE</meta>

<vocab>optative, verbs, grammar, Greeks, wishes, mood</vocab>

<summary>The author explains the origin of the optative mood as a linguistic tool born from human impatience and the constant state of desiring. He contrasts the Greek language's dedicated verb endings for wishes with the French language's use of simple idiomatic formulas.</summary>
<keywords>Optative mood, Ancient Greek, French grammar, etymology, human desire, linguistic evolution</keywords>

## ARTICLE III.

### *Of the Conditional or Suppositive.*

Often we want something; but this wanting depends on circumstances and conditions without which this will is ineffective; it was therefore necessary to find formulas by which Verbs could align themselves with this state of the soul, which is halted in its desires by external circumstances: from this comes the *Conditional* or <term>Suppositive: an older grammatical term for the conditional mood, used when an action depends on a "supposition" or "if" clause</term> Mode; thus, one says:

->I **WOULD READ**, if I had instructive books.<- <note>original: *Je LIROIS, ſi j’avois des livres inſtructifs.*</note>
->I **WOULD TAKE A WALK**, if I had the leisure.<- <note>original: *Je me PROMENEROIS, ſi j’en avois le loiſir.*</note>

We owe this tense to the Latins, although they did not make the Conditional a specific mood, instead confusing it with the Subjunctive: in this, as in several other respects, our <note>French</note> Conjugation is more numerous and more perfect than theirs.

This Mode has two tenses in French;
one, which corresponds to the Present of our Indicative, I **WOULD DO** <note>original: *je ferois*</note>.
The other, which corresponds to the Preterite <term>Preterite: a tense expressing a past action</term>, I **WOULD HAVE DONE** <note>original: *j’auroit fait*</note>.

<summary>The author defines the Conditional (or Suppositive) mood as the linguistic expression of a will restricted by external conditions. He argues that French grammar improved upon Latin by establishing the conditional as its own distinct mood with two specific tenses.</summary>

One can add several other tenses to it; especially by admitting as specific Tenses, the infinitive preceded by the verbs *to come* <note>original: *venir*</note> & *to owe/ought* <note>original: *devoir*</note>; thus one would say in the conditional future, *I* **OUGHT** *to do* <note>original: *je* **DEVROIS** *faire*</note>.

## ARTICLE IV.

### Of the SUBJUNCTIVE.

One event often depends on another; it is therefore necessary, when the verb that designates it forms a sentence itself, that it take a form that allows its dependence to be recognized: it is this form that we call the **SUBJUNCTIVE** <term>Subjunctive: a mood of the verb used to express various states of unreality such as wish, emotion, possibility, judgment, opinion, necessity, or action that has not yet occurred</term> Mode.

It is recognized, whether in Latin or in French, by a particular ending, & by the conjunctions by which it is preceded; **THAT** <note>original Latin: **UT**</note>, in Latin, whether expressed or implied; & **THAT** <note>original French: **QUE**</note>, in French. We say thus:

-> It is necessary **THAT** a young man **OBEY** his <-
-> father & mother. <-
-> He was waiting **UNTIL I HAD DESERVED** pub- <-
-> lic approval. <-

Of these tenses, the first is a Present
