The second is a comparative Preterit <note>a past tense used to compare one point in time to another</note>. To these tenses are joined these:

->**DEFINED PRESENT**, that I *might do* <note>original: *fisse*, the imperfect subjunctive</note>; it was<-
->necessary that I *did* or *should do*.<-

->**INDEFINITE PRETERIT**, that I *have done* <note>original: *aie fait*</note>; it<-
->is necessary that I *have done*.<-

These are the four Tenses that the Latins called **Present**, **Imperfect**, **Preterit** or **Perfect**, & **Pluperfect**.

It is from them also that the name **Subjunctive** comes; composed of two words *sub* <note>meaning "under" or "below"</note> & *junctus* <note>meaning "joined"</note>, which designate the property of being joined following an object.

As these tenses are subordinate to those of the Indicative <term>Indicative: the mood used for factual statements</term>, one can view them all as futures; & this is what occurs in the Italian Language, where the present subjunctive is used instead of employing the future.

*Pensa ove s'accampi* <note>Italian: "He thinks where he may encamp"</note>, he thinks wherever he may place his camp, to mean where he *will place* his camp; or, in what place he *must* place his camp.

## CHAPTER III.
### *Of Abstract or Indefinite Moods.*

---

## FIRST ARTICLE.
### *Of the Infinitive.*

This Mood <term>Mood: the form of a verb that shows the speaker's attitude or the relation of the action to reality</term> is of a different nature from the others: it does not link itself, as they do, in a determined manner with one or another of the Persons <note>Grammatical persons: first (I/we), second (you), or third (he/she/it/they)</note>; but simply with the indeterminate and general idea of Personality.

*To love*, for example, offers the indeterminate idea of a Person in general who exists in the state of love.

It is this state depicted as *existing* in a Being.

Always presupposing the idea of *Person*, but never being accompanied by any of the three Persons, it becomes an abstract word, which one can consider as a Noun <note>original: *Nom*</note>, so that it will be accompanied after the example of the

<meta>catchword: the</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the Infinitive as an "abstract" mood that expresses the general existence of a state or action without being tied to a specific grammatical person. Because it functions as a general concept rather than a specific action, the author argues it can be treated as a noun.</summary>
<keywords>Infinitive mood, abstract moods, grammatical person, personality in grammar, verbs as nouns, linguistics</keywords>

--- Page 379 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous page, detailing how the Infinitive mood bridges the gap between Nouns and Verbs. It explores the grammatical functions of the Infinitive, such as its ability to take cases and its relationship to time.</meta>

<header>UNIVERSAL GRAMMAR.</header>

Nouns, articles, and prepositions, and that it will serve like them as a subject, an object, a terminative <term>terminative: a grammatical function or case that indicates the direction or end-point of an action</term>, etc., so that it will have Cases in those Languages where Nouns are susceptible to them. Thus, one has been tempted to regard it as a Noun.

On the other hand, instead of depicting objects like Nouns, it depicts: 1. only actions or events, like Verbs; and it serves as a complement to Verbs.

2. Like Verbs, it is associated with the idea of Time <note>original: *Tems*; in this context, it refers to "tense," or the ability to express when an action occurs</note>, which is incompatible with Nouns.

These are the properties that one must not lose sight of, in order to form a correct idea of this Mood.

As we said above: it depicts the state or the action in an indeterminate relationship with Persons, or with the general and abstract idea of Persons.

*To be wise*, *to be loving*, *to be loved*, are Tableaux <note>A "tableau" (plural: tableaux) is a vivid mental picture or a staged scene</note> where one abstracts the particular idea of a person; where one does not

<meta>This page continues the analysis of the Infinitive mood, specifically following the quote from Horace on the previous page regarding how virtue consists in fleeing vice. It explains the grammatical logic of using verbs as subjects of sentences.</meta>

<page-num>311</page-num>
<header>UNIVERSAL GRAMMAR 311</header>

...fleeing; as if one were to say, *virtue is that state which we call fleeing vice.*

Thus, when the Infinitive is placed at the head of a sentence, as when we say, for example, *to sleep is a necessity*, or *sleeping restores one's strength*, "to sleep" and "sleeping" are not truly nouns, since they do not designate a specific object; they merely complete an elliptical <term>elliptical: a sentence structure where certain words are omitted because they are understood from context</term> phrase which begins with a noun, together with which they form the subject of the sentence; it is as if one said, *the state of being a sleeping person is a necessity.*

In this Mood, we observe two indefinite Tenses applicable to the three Epochs <term>Epochs: the three divisions of time—past, present, and future</term>: present, past, and future.

A Present, **TO DO**; an ellipsis for *the state of being a person who is doing.*

A Preterite <term>preterite: a tense expressing a past action</term>, **TO HAVE DONE**; an ellipsis for *the state of having been a person who was doing.*

### ARTICLE II.

#### On Gerunds.

Since the Infinitive is used like a Noun, it may take on all the cases of the

regard: they expressed themselves as if we said in English: *it is the time of the book which must be read*; *he came for the book which had to be read*: they thus said, *tempus est libri legendi* <note>original: "tempus est libri legendi"; literally "it is the time of the book to-be-read"</note>; *venit ad librum legendum* <note>original: "venit ad librum legendum"; "he came for the book to-be-read"</note>.

However, they almost always used an ellipsis <term>ellipsis: the omission of a word that is understood from the context</term> of the noun; they simply said *tempus est legendi* <note>original: "tempus est legendi"; "it is the time of reading"</note>, *venit ad legendum* <note>original: "venit ad legendum"; "he came to read"</note>: just as we say *I have done* <note>original: "j'ai fait"</note>, and not *I have an object done by me* <note>original: "j'ai un objet par moi fait"</note>.

Consequently, they were authorized to view these cases—*legendi*, *legendum*—as active formulas, in the same way that we view *I have done* as active, even though *done* <note>original: "fait"; the past participle which is inherently passive</note> is passive.

And once these cases were regarded as active, they were employed as actives and were followed, like them, by accusatives <term>accusative: the grammatical case marking the direct object of a verb</term>; such that it became a matter of indifference whether one said *tempus est libri legendi* <note>original: "tempus est libri legendi"; using the passive gerundive agreement</note> or *tempus est legendi librum* <note>original: "tempus est legendi librum"; using the gerund as an active verb taking an object</note>; this latter formula was even more elegant, because it moved further away from the common spirit <note>original: "génie vulgaire"; the literal or basic structural logic</note> of the language.

# ARTICLE III.

## *Of Supines.*

The Supines <term>Supine: a type of Latin verbal noun used only in the accusative and ablative cases, often translated as "to [verb]" or "in [verb]ing"</term>, which are no less confusing in appearance, form the accusative and the ablative <term>ablative: a grammatical case used to indicate movement away from, or the means by which something is done</term> of the past participles; and they serve as cases for the preterite <term>preterite: the past tense</term> of the infinitive: thus, everything that was said regarding Gerunds must also be said regarding Supines.

The Latins, being unable to say *this book is worthy of having been read*, made use of another turn of phrase; they said, *this book is worthy of being a read object* <note>original: "ce livre est digne d'être un objet lu"; referring to the Latin construction "dignus lectu"</note>.

They likewise said *eo lectum* <note>original: "eo lectum"; literally "I go to read" or "I go for the purpose of reading"</note>, *I go read*, and not *I go to read*, just as we say *I have*

The reason for these turns of phrase is very simple: it was their perfect conformity with the spirit <term>spirit: the French text uses "génie" (genius), referring to the natural inclination or inherent character of a language</term> of the Latin Language, which could only express through the passive—and consequently only through passive participles—neutral or impersonal phrases where the subject is not determined. These are the phrases that we express using *one* and *it* with the active voice: thus, instead of saying, as we do, *it is necessary to do*, they said *must be done* <note>original: "faciendum est"</note>; and instead of saying, *one has come*, they said *it has been come* <note>original: "ventum est"</note>; for *one has heard*, [they said] *it has been heard* <note>original: "auditum est"</note>.

If they used these forms with verbs of motion, if they said *I go to destroy* <note>original: "eo perditum"</note>, it is because verbs of motion necessarily carry with them a destination <term>terminative: a grammatical concept indicating the end-point or goal of an action</term>, always designated by an accusative <term>accusative: the grammatical case used for the direct object of a verb or the object of certain prepositions</term> with the preposition *to* <note>original: "ad"</note>. It would therefore have been a grammatical error <note>original: "barbarisme"</note> to say *I go to destroy* using the infinitive <note>original: "eo perdere"</note>; they therefore said *I go to* <note>original: "eo ad"</note>, followed by an accusative. But with this *to* <note>original: "ad"</note>, one could not use the participle ending in *-dus* <note>referring to the gerundive, like "faciendus"</note>, which does not mark...

# SECTION II.

## ON FORMS.

---

### CHAPTER ONE.

->*Their Origin.*<-

From the Tables of Discourse <note>These "Tables" refer to the author's structural classification of how language is organized and presented</note> divided into enunciative, active & passive, arise three sorts of Verbs. The *enunciative* Verbs <term>enunciative: verbs that describe a state of being or existence rather than a specific action</term>, which depict the state of a Being endowed with any quality whatsoever, such as *to be*. The ACTIVE Verbs, which depict the state of an acting Being, *to do*, *to act*. The PASSIVE Verbs, which depict the state of a Being upon which one acts, *to be done*, *to be struck*.

The same roots <term>roots: the fundamental part of a word that remains after all prefixes and suffixes are removed</term> that form Active Verbs, form Passive Verbs, *to do* & *to be done*; *to write*, *to be written*, etc.
Often the same root also serves to form enunciative Verbs <term>enunciative: verbs that describe a state or quality rather than a direct action upon an object</term>: such is the word TO MELT.
It is active in this phrase, *to melt a gold ingot*.
It is passive in this one, *this ingot has been melted*.
It is enunciative in saying, *this gold melts in the fire* <note>In this example, the author explains how "melts" describes the state of the gold without a specified external agent acting upon it in that specific phrase structure</note>.
From there, three different Forms in Verbs; *active*, *passive* & *enunciative* Forms, to which correspond the active, passive & neuter Verbs <note>The author equates his "enunciative" category with what traditional grammarians of his time called "neuter" verbs</note> of the Grammarians.
To these three kinds of Verbs, one can join two others.
The *reflexive* Form, which designates the state of an Agent who is himself the object of his action; when one says, for example, *to melt oneself*, *to whiten oneself*, *to redden oneself*, *to love oneself*.

## CHAPTER II.

### *Forms of the French Language.*

THE French language possesses the five types of Forms of which we have just spoken.

The Enunciative <term>enunciative: verbs that describe a state or quality rather than a direct action</term> is conjugated in compound tenses <term>compound tenses: verb forms consisting of an auxiliary verb (to be/to have) and a past participle</term>, sometimes by means of the verb *TO BE* (*I was arrived*, *I had arrived* <note>original: "j’étois arrivé," "je fus arrivé"</note>); sometimes by means of the verb *TO HAVE* (*I have slept*, *I had slept* <note>original: "j’ai dormi," "j’avois dormi"</note>).

The active is conjugated with the verb *TO HAVE* (*I have done*, *I had done* <note>original: "j’ai fait," "j’avois fait"</note>).

The passive, with the verb *TO BE*, in simple tenses <term>simple tenses: verb forms consisting of a single word without an auxiliary</term> (*I am sought after* <note>original: "je suis recherché"</note>); and with the verb *to have* and the verb *to be* together in compound tenses (*I have been sought after* <note>original: "j’ai été recherché"</note>): whereas in Italian, it is always with the verb *to be* (*I am been sought after* <note>original: "je suis été recherché"</note>).

## CHAPTER III.

### *Forms of the Latin Language.*

The Latin Language has only two ways of conjugating Verbs, and this is what is called the *Active form* and the *Passive form*.

The first serves equally for enunciative or neuter Verbs <term>neuter: verbs that express a state or an action that does not pass over to an object, such as "to sleep"</term>, and for reflexive Verbs.

They have a kind of Verbs called DEPONENTS <term>deponent: verbs in Latin that have a passive form but an active meaning</term>, because while having the passive form, they "lay down" <note>from the Latin 'deponere', meaning to lay aside</note> the passive meaning they ought to have, in order to offer an active one.

But this is only an apparent irregularity: every deponent Verb is passive in its origin; it became active only through ellipsis <term>ellipsis: the omission of words that are understood by the listener</term>. POLLICEOR <note>original: "POLLICEOR" (Latin for "I promise")</note>, for example, is truly passive; it means *I am bound by an engagement, by a word given*; thus by...

## C H A P T E R  IV.
### *On the Middle Form of the Greeks.*

Beyond the two Forms of the Latins, the Greeks have a third which they call MIDDLE <note>original: "MOYENNE"</note>, or the middle verb.

This Form is conjugated in some tenses like active verbs, and in some others like passive verbs. One might say that it corresponds to our enunciative or neuter verbs <term>neuter: verbs that express a state or an action that does not pass over to an object, like "to exist"</term> and to our reflexive verbs <term>reflexive: a verb where the subject and object are the same, like "I dress myself"</term>. It was worthy of the Greeks to dedicate a Form specifically to them.

Some Languages have many more; but as these forms are merely nuances of those we have just discussed, we refer those who would be pleased to have some knowledge of them to our Universal Grammar.

# BOOK III.
## ON SYNTAX.

---

### DIVISION.

When one wishes to depict an idea through speech, there are two objects to consider: 1st, the form that each word requires to link with its neighbors; 2nd, the place it must occupy.

Of these two objects, one relating to *form* and the other to *place*, the first is properly called SYNTAX, that is to say, *reciprocal arrangement*; from two Greek words, *with* <note>original: "syn"</note>, and *arrangement* <note>original: "taxis"</note>.

The second is called CONSTRUCTION, because it is through this that speech is raised or constructed.

Syntax gives the words that must enter into a phrase the form they must have for the functions they are to fulfill.

<keywords>Syntax, Construction, etymology, grammar, sentence structure, linguistic form, Greek roots</keywords>

## ARTICLE ONE.

### *ON SYNTAX PROPERLY DEFINED.*

---

## CHAPTER ONE

->Its Objectives.<-

ALL the rules of Syntax relate to two general classes: **AGREEMENT** and **DEPENDENCE**.

Agreement <note>original: "Concordance"</note> brings together all the words that relate to one and the same object.

Dependence joins to the main object those words which indicate the relationships of another object with the first.

In effect, the words of a sentence express either the qualities of the object in question in that sentence—the one being depicted, which is the

<vocab>Syntax, Agreement, Dependence, Construction, sentence, object, rules</vocab>

<summary>This page defines the two core pillars of Syntax: Agreement, which groups words describing the same subject, and Dependence, which links different subjects together through their grammatical relationships.</summary>
<keywords>syntax, agreement, dependence, sentence structure, grammatical rules, linguistic objects</keywords>

--- Page 393 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 322, which introduced the two general classes of syntax: Agreement and Dependence. This page further elaborates on their roles in structuring a sentence before beginning a specific chapter on Agreement.</meta>

<page-num>323</page-num>
<header>UNIVERSAL GRAMMAR.</header>

subject; or its relationships with other objects.
In the first case, all the words of a sentence agree with the principal word; this is *Agreement* <note>original: "Concordance"</note>. In the second case, they receive the necessary modifications so that one perceives the relationship existing between them and the subject, ensuring that it is clear they are only secondary; this is *Dependence* <note>original: "Dépendance"</note>.
*Dependence* governs only the secondary parts of the picture: *Agreement* governs the primary parts—those which constitute its essence and which must harmonize with one another.

## C H A P T E R II.
### On Agreement.

AGREEMENT is that portion of Syntax which teaches the proper means for making those words that depict the various parts of an idea agree with one another, in the same way that these ideas agree among themselves.
It governs, above all, the words without which

<meta>Continuing from page 323, this page continues the discussion of Agreement (Concordance) in syntax, focusing on how different parts of speech—nouns, articles, adjectives, verbs, and pronouns—align through grammatical number and gender to create a unified sentence structure.</meta>

there would be no picture <note>The author compares a well-constructed sentence to a painted "picture" where all elements must harmonize.</note>, and which number three or four at most.

The Noun and its article, the adjective which depicts the quality attributed to the Noun, the verb that unites them, and the pronoun.

These words, though so different from one another, nevertheless possess a common property: that of being susceptible to **number**, of having both singular and plural. One can therefore unite them by this means, bringing them into unison by assigning the same **number** to all of them. Thus, one might say:

> ->Already the horrible thunders were rumbling<-
> ->By which the ramparts are shattered.<-

By putting the article *the* <note>original: *les*</note>, the noun *thunders* <note>original: *tonnerres*</note>, the adjective *horrible* <note>original: *horribles*</note>, and the verb *were rumbling* <note>original: *grondoient*</note>, all in the plural.

The Adjective has an even closer relationship with the noun, due to its property of being susceptible to **gender**: such that these two words are placed not only in the same number, like the others, but also in the same gender. This constitutes two kinds of Agreement <note>original: *Concordance*</note>, that of **Number** and that of **Gender**.

There exists a third type <note>A third type of agreement, or "concordance," following Number and Gender discussed on the previous page.</note> in Languages that have **Cases** <term>Case: A grammatical category (like nominative or accusative) that shows the function of a noun or adjective in a sentence, common in Latin but largely absent in modern French or English</term>: then, the Noun and the adjective must be in the same number, the same gender, and the same case.

Is it necessary to observe that when several nouns in the singular agree with a verb, the verb must be put in the plural?

That it is the same for an adjective likewise accompanied by several Nouns.

And that the adjective also agrees with the Noun, although there may be the Verb *is* between the two. This city *is* very beautiful <note>original: *Cette ville est fort belle*. ## CHAPTER III.

### *On Dependency.*

To the essential words of a sentence, a great number of others are usually joined, whose union offers new ideas; these must not cause any harm to the whole of the picture <note>The author continues the metaphor of a sentence as a "picture" or "painting" where every word must fit the composition.</note> nor alter its harmony and unity. It is therefore necessary that they relate to the words that constitute the foundation

--- Page 396 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 325, which concluded with the idea that supplementary words must relate to the fundamental words of the "sentence-picture." This page explains the nature of this "dependency" and the three ways it is signaled in language.</meta>

<page-num>326</page-num>
<header>NATURAL HISTORY OF SPEECH.</header>

of the picture, and so that the picture may derive more strength and interest from them: for this purpose, let them be in a state of dependency upon those words.

A sentence will therefore be composed, besides the words in agreement <note>original: *concordance*; the grammatical harmony of gender and number discussed on the previous page</note>, of words in dependency upon the Noun, or the Verb, or the Adjective, or all three together; and each must link with those upon which it depends, in a different manner and always relative to the nature of these words.

Furthermore, one recognizes the words of a sentence that are in dependency by three different marks: 1st. By the place they occupy; 2nd. By the words that link them with those on which they depend; 3rd. By their ending <term>Termination: The author refers here to "terminaison," or inflectional endings like those found in Latin cases, which signal a word's role</term>; as we are about to see in detail.

## CHAPTER IV.

### Words in Dependency of the Noun or the Subject.

Words that are in a state of dependency upon the Noun or the subject are those which develop its nature, reveal its origin, or indicate the Beings to whom the object it designates belongs.

These words link with the subject:

1st. By an adjective, as in these verses by La Fontaine <note>Jean de La Fontaine (1621–1695), the famous French fabulist. These lines are from "The Raven and the Fox."</note>,

> Master Crow, on a tree perched, <note>original: "Maître Corbeau, sur un arbre perché"</note>
> Held in his beak a cheese.

These words, *on a tree perched*, refer to *Crow*; they are in its dependency by making its situation known, and are linked to it by an adjective <note>The past participle "perched" functions here as an adjective modifying the noun "Crow."</note>.

2nd. They link with it by the preposition *of* <note>original: *de*</note>, as in these verses by Racine <note>Jean Racine (1639–1699), a major 17th-century French dramatist. These lines come from his play *Esther*.</note>.

> The fierce aspect of her proud captors, <note>original: "Le farouche aspect de ses fiers ravisseurs"</note>
> Heightened the timid sweetness of her eyes.

Here, *proud captors* determine the words *fierce aspect*, and are linked with them by the preposition *of*.

<vocab>Universal Grammar, Noun, Subject, Adjective, Preposition, La Fontaine, Jean Racine, dependency, syntax</vocab>

<summary>This page explains how words depend on a Noun or Subject to provide more detail, illustrating two primary methods: using an adjective (as seen in La Fontaine) and using the preposition "of" (as seen in Racine).</summary>
<keywords>grammar, syntax, noun dependency, adjectives, prepositions, literary examples, La Fontaine, Racine, French literature, sentence structure</keywords>

3rd. These words link with the Noun by means of *who*, *which*, or *that* <note>The original French uses *qui* or *que*, the relative pronouns.</note>.

->Rome *which* commanded almost the entire<-
->Universe, etc.<-
->The Poems *that* Homer composed still<-

# CHAPTER V.

## *Words in dependency of the Verb.*

THE Verb, whatever its nature may be, has under its dependency all the words that designate the circumstances accompanying the scene, of whatever nature they may be. These circumstances indeed designate the object, the goal, the place, the time, the cause, the means, the state, or the manner of being. There are few discourses that do not offer most of these circumstances.

These verses, for example, that Racine <note>Jean Racine (1639–1699), the French dramatist mentioned on the previous page.</note> puts

> What does this dark sadness presage to my eyes,
> And these somber looks wandering at random?
> Everything smiles upon you, Fortune obeys your wishes. <note>original: "Que présage à mes yeux cette tristesse obscure / Et ces sombres regards errans à l'aventure ? / Tout vous rit, la Fortune obéit à vos vœux."</note>

These verses, I say, contain a great number of words in dependency of the verb, and which designate as many circumstances.

*What*, marks the object of the verb *presages*.
*To my eyes*, marks the end-point <note>The author uses "terme" to mean the destination or indirect object of the action.</note> of this object.
*At random*, marks the manner in which these looks wander.
*You*, marks the end-point to which the verb *smiles* relates.
*To your wishes*, marks the end-point of Fortune's obedience; it is to your wishes that she obeys.

*Fortune obeys your wishes*, are words whose whole offers a circumstance of cause.

> He is cherished *of* his Parents: he is battered *by* the storm.
> He was plundered *by* his neighbors: he was punished *by* his Judges.

## CHAPTER VI.

### *Words Dependent on the Adjective.*

The ADJECTIVE likewise brings in its wake words that serve to further define it; and these similarly designate circumstances or accessories.

1°. Adverbs of comparison:
->*He reigns with the greatest equity.*<- <note>In this context, "equity" refers to the quality of being fair and impartial, especially in the administration of justice.</note>

2°. Circumstances linked to it by Prepositions:
->*Rich in virtues; great without ostentation.*<-

3°. Often, circumstantial words seem to take the place of the adjective, because the adjective is omitted through <term>ellipsis: the omission of a word that is understood by the reader but not explicitly stated</term> as being unnecessary: in this phrase,
->*Alexander was King of Macedonia,*<-

These words *King of Macedonia* are not adjectives; they are words dependent on an adjective that has disappeared because it added nothing to the clarity of the sentence: it is as if one said,
->*Alexander was invested with the title of King of Macedonia.*<- <note>original: "revêtu de la qualité de Roi de Macédoine." In the 18th century, "quality" often referred to one's social rank or official status.</note>

<summary>This page explains how adjectives are modified by adverbs and prepositional phrases. It also introduces the concept of ellipsis, where a title or status functions as a modifier for an implied adjective.</summary>
<keywords>grammar, adjective, syntax, ellipsis, adverbs, prepositions, Alexander the Great, linguistics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of linguistic ellipsis, moving from the previous discussion of titles functioning as adjectives to the reverse phenomenon: adjectives that appear to be nouns due to the omission of the noun they modify.</meta>

<page-num>331</page-num>
<header>UNIVERSAL GRAMMAR</header>

At other times, on the contrary, the adjective appears to be a noun because the noun upon which it depends has disappeared: when one says, for example, *Paris is the Capital of France*,

*Capital* appears to be a noun, yet it is merely an adjective: the full sentence would be, *Paris is the Capital City of France*.

This is how <term>ellipsis: the omission of words that are understood by the reader but not explicitly stated</term> reigns everywhere and influences the entire body of Language.

One may find in our *Universal Grammar* some specific observations regarding circumstantial and dependent phrases, which form simple and complex <term>complements: words or phrases that complete the meaning of another term</term>; and regarding the distribution once applied to the words of a sentence into *governing* and *governed* <note>original: "en régissans & en regis." In traditional grammar, "governing" words determine the grammatical form (such as case or ending) of the "governed" words.</note>.

In that same section, one may also see what concerns the arrangement that the complements of a single **Table** <note>original: "Tableau." This likely refers to the grammatical charts or visual depictions of sentence structure common in 18th-century linguistic texts.</note> may undergo.

# CHAPTER VII.

->*Of the Constituent Parts of a Sentence.*<-

The constituent parts of a **Table** <note>original: "Tableau." In 18th-century linguistics, the author uses this term to describe the sentence as a "picture" of a thought, where all parts are arranged to create a single mental image.</note>, whether in agreement or in dependence, are seven in number <note>While the text specifies seven parts, only six are listed here. In similar grammars of this period, the seventh is often the Interjection, or the author may be including the underlying "Thought" as the silent seventh element.</note>.

1. The SUBJECT, that subject of which we have already spoken so much and to which the entire Table relates.

2. The ATTRIBUTE, always composed of a Verb and an Adjective expressed separately, or merged into the Verb <note>For example, in "The sun shines," the attribute "shines" is seen as a merger of the verb "to be" and the adjective "shining."</note>.

3. The OBJECT, which expresses the Beings that receive the impressions of the actions expressed by the Verb.

4. The TERM, which represents the goal reached by these actions, or toward which the attribute is directed.

5. The CIRCUMSTANCE, which serves to define the attribute, stating its qualities in relation to this or that object.

6. The CONJUNCTION, which serves to unite two parts of a sentence.

<vocab>Subject, Attribute, Object, Term, Circumstance, Conjunction, Table, Sentence, Grammar</vocab>

<summary>The author defines the six primary components that constitute a sentence, conceptualizing the sentence as a "Table" or visual representation of thought. These parts include the subject, attribute, object, goal, circumstance, and conjunction.</summary>
<keywords>sentence structure, grammar, subject, attribute, object, linguistics, 18th-century philosophy, Tableau</keywords>

--- Page 403 ---
<meta>This page concludes the list of the seven constituent parts of a sentence (or "Tableau") introduced on the previous page. It resolves the ambiguity of the "seventh part" by defining it as the Adjunction.</meta>

<page-num>333</page-num>
<header>UNIVERSAL GRAMMAR</header>

7th. The ADJUNCTION, which enters into speech only as a form of accompaniment, and which is not linked to any of its members <note>In modern grammar, we might call this a "vocative" or a "parenthetical expression"—words that are grammatically independent of the rest of the sentence.</note>.

All seven are visible in these verses from *Andromaque* <note>A masterpiece of French classical theater written by Jean Racine in 1667. The scene depicts Orestes speaking to Hermione.</note>:

> No; I shall deprive you of this fatal pleasure:
> Madam, he shall die only by the hand of Orestes.
> Your enemies shall be sacrificed to you by me,
> And you will acknowledge my cares, if you wish.

*I* <note>original: "Je"</note> is the subject who deprives.
*You* <note>original: "vous"</note> the object being deprived.
*Shall deprive* <note>original: "priverai"</note> the attribute.
*Of this fatal pleasure* <note>original: "De ce plaisir funeste"</note> the term of the deprivation.
*Madam* <note>original: "Madame"</note> an adjunction.
*By the hand of Orestes, by me* <note>original: "De la main d'Oreste, par moi"</note> etc. are circumstances.
*And* <note>original: "Et"</note> a conjunction that unites two Pictures <note>original: "Tableaux." The author views the two halves of the sentence as two distinct mental images joined together.</note>.

The subject, the object, and the term are designated by nouns and by pronouns.
The attribute by the verb alone, or by the verb and its adjective.
The adjunction by interjections.
The circumstance by prepositions and by adverbs.

The **Subjective** corresponds to the <term>Nominative</term> <note>original: "Nominatif." The case for the subject performing the action.</note>.
The **Objective** to the <term>Accusative</term> <note>original: "Accusatif." The case for the direct object receiving the action.</note>.
The **Terminative** to the <term>Dative</term> <note>original: "Datif." The case for the indirect object, usually indicating "to" or "for" someone.</note>, or to the Accusative with a preposition.
The **Circumstantial** to the <term>Ablative</term> <note>original: "Ablatif." A versatile Latin case often used to indicate the instrument, location, or time of an action.</note>.
The **Adjunctive** to the <term>Vocative</term> <note>original: "Vocatif." The case used when addressing or calling out to someone directly.</note>.

# ARTICLE II.
## *On Construction.*

The rules relative to construction are all the more advantageous, since the force & intelligence of discourse depend absolutely on the arrangement given to the various portions that compose it, especially when it is a question of a Spoken Tableau <note>original: "Tableau parlé." The author views a sentence as a "picture" or "painting" of a thought, where words must be arranged in the right order to be understood.</note> and not a written one; for each successive word must link both with those already pronounced, & with those which must follow it; in such a way that there is no gap <note>original: "vuide," an archaic spelling of "vide."</note> and no displacement.

These rules, moreover, vary according to the particular genius of Languages; which makes them more difficult to grasp: one is indeed so struck by the differences perceived in this regard between Languages, that one easily loses

sight <note>The text begins mid-word, completing "facilement" (easily) from the previous page: "one easily loses sight..."</note> of what they have in common, and one finds it difficult to be persuaded that the progression of each is as natural as the other.

However, all Languages can be divided into two classes regarding **Construction** <note>In this context, "construction" refers to the arrangement and sequence of words in a sentence.</note>; into Languages that have no **cases**, or that have them only for pronouns, like the **French** Language; and into Languages that have **cases**, like **Latin** and **Greek** <note>A "case" is a change in the ending of a word (like a noun or adjective) to show its grammatical role, such as whether it is the subject or the object of a sentence. Because the word ending tells you its role, the order of the words can be shuffled without losing the meaning.</note>.

In these latter languages, where the value of words does not depend on their position, one is infinitely less constrained in **Construction**; one can vary it in every direction, and thereby instill it with more grace, harmony, and power.

# CHAPTER ONE.

->*Rules of Construction Required by the French Language.*<-

Such are the principal rules required by French Construction <note>The term "Construction" refers to the arrangement or order of words in a sentence.</note>, which are all the more interesting because they almost all apply to languages without cases, and because they are almost all employed in languages that have cases whenever the speaker has no particular reason to follow an arrangement different from the one they prescribe.

### *Rules relative to the construction of the Subject.*

The position of the Subject varies depending on whether the sentence is narrative, imperative, interrogative, or optative <note>The "optative" mood is used to express a wish, hope, or desire.</note>.

1. In the narrative or expository sentence <note>An "expository" sentence is one that simply states a fact or provides information.</note>, the Subject is placed before the Verb:

->Columbus made known a new World.<-

It is the same in the imperative form for the third person:

->Let EVERYTHING obey its Laws.<-

2°. But in the interrogative form, the Subject only comes first when it is expressed by *who*, or by a noun preceded by the word *which* <note>The original uses "quel," which can be translated as "which" or "what" depending on the context.</note>:

->*Who* will find the true system of Nature?<-
->*Which* reason triumphs over prejudice?<-

3°. In every other case, the Subject in interrogative phrases is placed after the Verb:

->Have you not flattered me with a false hope?<-
->Can I, based on your account, establish some assurance?<-

4°. The same applies to pronouns that serve as the Subject in phrases with the form of a parenthesis, which are called *incised* phrases <note>Original: "phrases incises." These are interjected clauses, often used to attribute speech, such as "he said" or "she replied."</note>:

->Heaven, says he, crowns your virtues.<-

->First appears a distinguished Knight, etc.<-

->*Verb must occupy.*<-

## CHAPTER II.

### *Rules of Latin Construction.*

The Latin Language, achieving through the use of cases <term>cases</term> <note>Grammatical cases are noun endings that indicate the role of a word (such as subject, direct object, or possessor). Because the ending tells you the word's function, the word order can be more flexible than in English or French.</note> what we achieve regarding the arrangement of Nouns by their position, will have far fewer rules relating to construction. It will, however, have some, because the arrangement of words in any Language whatever cannot be left to chance: and these rules will be the same as those of other languages, except for those occasions where the advantage of cases provides the means to follow a more pleasing arrangement.

first; but it is often separated from its verb by both the object and the terminative <term>terminative</term> <note>A term used by the author to describe what we now call the indirect object or the dative case, which indicates the end-point or recipient of an action.</note>: then the verb acts as the closure of the Picture, which finds itself entirely enclosed between the Subject and the Verb.

But if the object or some complement offers a very great interest, then it precedes the Subject, which is followed in turn by the Verb.

Circumstantials <note>Words or phrases that describe the circumstances of an action, such as time, place, or manner (adverbs and prepositional phrases).</note> are placed where they interrupt the discourse the least.

A necessary precaution in this Language is not to separate the words that belong to the same portion of a phrase; to a subjective, an objective, an adjunctive, etc. <note>The author refers to the components of a sentence: the subject (subjective), the direct object (objective), and modifiers like adjectives (adjunctive).</note> for otherwise, there would no longer be any cohesion or harmony.

The Latins have some words, moreover, whose place is always the same: thus the Preposition *with* <note>original: "cum"</note> is constantly placed after the pronouns it governs <note>In Latin, instead of saying "with me" (*cum me*), one says *mecum*. The preposition is attached to the end of the word.</note>.

An ablative <term>ablative</term> <note>A Latin grammatical case used to indicate movement away from, tools used, or the "by/with/from" relationship.</note> joined to a genitive <term>genitive</term> <note>The grammatical case used to show possession or relationship, usually translated with "of."</note> which serves as its complement, is likewise placed after that genitive; thus one says *for the sake of example* <note>original: "exempli gratiâ"</note>, and not *gratiâ exempli*.

## CHAPTER IV.

### *On the Ellipsis.*

THE <term>ELLIPSIS</term> <note>From the Greek word for "omission," an ellipsis occurs when words are left out of a sentence because the meaning is already clear from the context.</note>, of which we have so often spoken, is an abbreviated construction from which various words have been removed that the meaning implies, and which it was unnecessary to express, because stating them would add nothing to the clarity of the sentence and would thereby make it cold and sluggish.

It is through ellipsis that the disdainful Heron... <note>A reference to the famous fable "The Heron" by Jean de La Fontaine, where the poet uses concise, elliptical language to describe the bird's behavior.</note>

->**Ellipsis, Grammar, Language, abbreviated construction, disdainful Heron**<-

<summary>The author explains that the "severe" nature of certain languages reflects the harsh environments of their creators, then defines the ellipsis as a tool for linguistic efficiency and energy.</summary>
<keywords>environmental linguistics, ellipsis, grammatical economy, Jean de La Fontaine, clarity in speech</keywords>

--- Page 416 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the grammatical concept of ellipsis, specifically illustrating it through literary examples from Jean de La Fontaine’s fables and explaining why language naturally tends toward brevity to match the speed of human thought.</meta>

<page-num>346</page-num>
<header>NATURAL HISTORY OF SPEECH.</header>

...by La Fontaine <note>Jean de La Fontaine (1621–1695) was a famous French fabulist; the author is quoting his fable "The Heron."</note>, cries out upon seeing Tenches <note>The tench is a freshwater fish, traditionally considered of lower quality than the heron's preferred prey.</note> pass by:

> *Me, eat Tenches! said he; I, a Heron, to make*
> *Such poor cheer! For whom do they take me?*

And he adds regarding the Gudgeons <note>Another small, common freshwater fish.</note>:

> *Gudgeon! This is truly a Heron's dinner!*
> *Would I open my beak for so little!*

This way of expressing one's ideas is drawn from Nature itself, which desires nothing useless, especially when one is in a hurry, and when feelings, succeeding one another with rapidity, do not allow one to dwell upon each: it leads us then to the **Ellipsis**, by tracing only the essential features, and suppressing all those which would prevent the mind from following the speed with which ideas succeed one another.

Thus, one is almost always obliged to speak a barbaric and ridiculous language when one wishes to explain these elliptical formulas and present the effect that would be produced by expressing everything that is suppressed there. Our Language, however, is full of them.

These words *my*, *thy*, *his* <note>original: "mon, ton, son"</note>, as we have already seen, are elliptical words, holding...

# CHAPTER V.
## On Pleonasm.

Pleonasm is the opposite of Ellipsis: it is a superabundance of expressions that seem superfluous, or a repetition of the same ideas.

Sometimes, however, this superabundance is useful; Pleonasm then becomes a beauty in language; but when it is useless, adding nothing to the scope or energy of the phrase, it is a defect; it is no longer a Pleonasm, but a *perissology* <note>original: "périssologie," from the Greek word for "excessive." In rhetoric, this refers to wordiness that provides no additional meaning or clarity.</note>, a sterile abundance that must be suppressed.

In saying, *I saw it with my eyes, I heard it with my ears*, one makes use of Pleo- <note>The text cuts off here, mid-word, as the author begins to explain how "I saw it with my eyes" is a classic example of a pleonasm used for emphasis.</note>

<vocab>Pleonasm, Ellipsis, perissology, language, grammar, Natural History of Speech</vocab>

<summary>This page defines the pleonasm as the opposite of the ellipsis, distinguishing between "beautiful" pleonasms that add energy to speech and "sterile" wordiness (perissology).</summary>
<keywords>pleonasm, ellipsis, rhetoric, perissology, linguistic beauty, repetition, grammar</keywords>

--- Page 419 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of pleonasm from page 348, specifically explaining how certain redundancies serve to emphasize the truth or magnitude of a statement, while others are merely grammatical errors.</meta>

<page-num>349</page-num>
<header>UNIVERSAL GRAMMAR</header>

-nasm; <note>This completes the word "Pleonasm" from the previous page.</note> for one can only see with eyes, and one can only hear with ears: but as one adds the words *my eyes*, *my ears* to make the matter more certain, this Pleonasm is necessary and increases the energy of the discourse, by giving more force to what one affirms.

The *King of Kings*, the *Age of Ages*, <note>original: "Siécle des Siécles," a phrase often used in liturgy and biblical texts to denote eternity.</note> are Pleonasms that add new ideas to those offered by the first of these words. <note>The author refers to the "Hebrew superlative" construction, where repeating a noun indicates the highest possible degree of that thing.</note>

But these expressions *I am going* TO GO, *to have a pain in* HIS *head*, <note>In French, the possessive pronoun "sa" (his/her) is considered redundant here, as one usually says "mal à la tête" (pain at the head). Using "his head" is seen as a redundant "perissology" in this specific linguistic context.</note> are faulty expressions; they are *perissologies* that must be avoided.

# CHAPTER VI.

->*On the Sentence considered in itself.*<-

EVERY expressed idea is a SENTENCE; <note>The author uses the word "PHRASE," which in the grammatical tradition of this period refers to a complete thought or what we now call a sentence.</note> a word derived from the Greek which signifies *the expression of an idea*. This word has become a name designating the smallest "pictures" that Speech can present. Thus, any statement composed of a Subject, a Verb, and an Attribute is a sentence; *the Sun is bright.* <note>In 18th-century grammar, the "Attribute" refers to the quality or state being described (the predicate), while the "Verb" (usually the verb "to be") acts as the link.</note>

A sentence can be expressed in two words, as happens when the Verb and the Attribute are contained within a single word: *I read.* <note>In the phrase "I read," the word "read" contains both the action and the state of the subject.</note>

It can even be expressed by a single word when the Pronoun or Subject is joined with the Verb, as in the Latin *I read.* <note>original Latin: "lego." In Latin, the suffix of the verb indicates the subject "I," making a separate pronoun unnecessary.</note>

Two or more sentences joined together to form a single whole constitute what is called a PERIOD.

Several periods forming a connected whole <unclear/> bear the name of DISCOURSE.

<vocab>grammar, linguistics, sentence, period, discourse, subject, verb, attribute</vocab>

<summary>The author defines the "sentence" as the fundamental expression of an idea and explains how subjects, verbs, and attributes combine to form sentences, periods, and larger discourses.</summary>
<keywords>sentence structure, grammar, period, discourse, Latin grammar, linguistic units, definitions</keywords>

--- Page 421 ---
<meta>This page continues from the discussion of the sentence, moving from its structural components (subject, verb, attribute) to its logical function as a proposition, and then transitioning into the rules of punctuation.</meta>

<page-num>351</page-num>
<header>UNIVERSAL GRAMMAR</header>

Every sentence is also a PROPOSITION, <note>In the grammatical theory of the Enlightenment, a "proposition" is the logical expression of a mental judgment, whereas a "sentence" is the grammatical structure of that thought.</note> when it is viewed as the result of a judgment made regarding the subject of the sentence.

The Proposition is *affirmative*, when one decides that a certain quality belongs to or is found within a certain object.

It is *negative*, when one decides that the quality being discussed does not belong to or is not found within that object.

## CHAPTER VII.

### On Punctuation.

A Discourse being, as we have just seen, a composition of a great number of various parts, it was necessary to invent marks that would make known the extent of each of these parts; where they begin; where they end; the greater or lesser relationship they have with one another; and the tone that should be given to each. These are essential objects that give to Discourse its clarity,

<keywords>proposition, affirmative vs negative, punctuation, discourse, mental judgment, grammar theory</keywords>

<page-num>352</page-num>

->NATURAL HISTORY OF SPEECH.<-

clarity, speed, and the overall cohesion it should have.

The signs used in writing to distinguish different types of sentences, and to indicate the tone one must give them, as well as the greater or lesser interval one should leave between them when reading—these signs, I say, are called **POINTS** <note>In historical grammar, the term "points" referred to all punctuation marks collectively, not just the period.</note>: from which results **PUNCTUATION**, that is to say, that portion of Grammar which takes *Points* as its object.

Punctuation indicates the places where one must rest, and how much time should be spent at each pause: it contributes to the clarity of meaning and prevents the obscurity of style.

The rules of Punctuation are found in every Grammar; which spares us from repeating them here. We will content ourselves with a single remark.

It would be desirable if we had a greater number of punctuation signs; if we had some to determine the tone one must give to certain sentiments distinct from in-

> Which know how to stir the slowest souls.
> He made the dead speak, thundered, said what he could;
> The wind carried it all away; no one was moved.

->The Subject is an Orator.<-
->Its Attribute is *ran*.<-
->The Term—the place to which he ran—is<-
->*the Tribune* <note>original: "Tribune." Referring to the raised platform or *bema* used by speakers in ancient Athens.</note>.<-

And the relationship of this word with the attribute *ran* is designated by the preposition *to*.

*Seeing his Country in danger* is another Circumstantial <note>In this system, a "Circumstantial" (circonstanciel) is a phrase that describes the conditions—the why, where, or when—surrounding the main action.</note> that marks the motive which prompted the Orator to run.

Having thus divided this Scene <note>original: "Tableau." The author treats a sentence like a visual "picture" or "painting" of a thought.</note> into its various parts, let us move on to the analysis of each of these parts.

The first circumstantial is composed of seven words.

1st. *In*, a Preposition which marks the relationship of internal containment, of a place where one is enclosed.

2nd. *Athens* marks the place where the Orator was enclosed.

The name of this City always ends with an S; but this letter has been omitted here so that the name could fit into the verse <note>In French, the city is spelled "Athènes." La Fontaine used the singular "Athène" to maintain the poetic meter of the line.</note>.

3rd. *Of old* is an adverb that marks the time when this event took place, and which

indicates it in a distant but very vague manner, without designating the era with precision.

4th. *A vain and fickle people*, this is an appositive phrase <note>original: "phrase incise." A parenthetical insertion used to describe a noun more fully.</note>, which serves as an epithet for the inhabitants of the City just mentioned. They are called *a vain and fickle people*. This epithet is not useless: it makes known the character of these people, and it prepares the reader for the fickleness with which they will be seen behaving in this Fable.

But here, the Poet has changed his figure of speech; he transfers his epithet to the inhabitants, while he is only speaking of the City. This way of expressing oneself is not at all admitted in prose: it is forgiven in Poets when they are not working in the high style <note>The "high style" (genre élevé) refers to formal, serious poetry like epics or tragedies, which had stricter linguistic rules.</note>; even then, they must not abuse the permission. Our Author could have substituted these words *in Athens of old* with these, *among the Athenians*: but the verse would have abounded too much in nasal sounds <note>original: "nazales." The French words "chez les Athéniens" create several nasal vowel sounds which the author finds unappealing here.</note>, it would have been too dull: whereas instead it is very sonorous.

Let us add that ***sa*** <note>In French, *sa* means "his," "her," or "its" depending on the gender of the noun possessed; here it refers to the Orator's homeland.</note> is an elliptical word that also takes the place of three others: it is as if one had said, *seeing in danger the Country of himself*.

***Courut*** <note>ran</note> is in the singular because the subject is in the singular. It is the third Person of the preterite; *I ran*, *you ran*, *he ran*: one can also call it, with Mr. Beauzée <note>Nicolas Beauzée (1717–1789) was a famous French grammarian known for his work on "General Grammar," which sought the logical underpinnings of all languages.</note>, *the present anterior*. It comes from the Verb ***courir*** <note>to run</note>, which was formed from the Latin *CURRERE* <note>original: "CURRERE," meaning "to run"</note>, and which signifies the same thing. It is related to our words *course*, *coursier* <note>steed</note>, *coureur* <note>runner</note>; and to our Verbs *accourir* <note>to run toward</note>, *recourir* <note>to have recourse</note>, *secourir* <note>to give aid/succor</note>.

***La***, which precedes *Tribune* <note>The Rostrum or speaker's platform</note>, is the feminine indicative article; it determines as known the object of which one speaks.

...conjunctive, and: 2°. a **highly complex circumstantial phrase, with a tyrannical art wanting to force hearts in a Republic**: 3°. a **subject, he**: 4°. a **predicate, spoke strongly**: 5°. **the object of this discourse, the common safety**. <note>The author is breaking down the sentence "And with a tyrannical art... he spoke strongly on the common safety" into its logical parts.</note>

***With a tyrannical art*** indicates the means by which the Orator wished to force hearts. This expression is an ellipsis; we understand it to mean, ***by means of***: ***by means of a tyrannical art***. Thus these words, ***with an art***, serve as a complement to words that are ***implied***.

***Art*** is a masculine noun <term>noun: Original "substantif."</term>, for which ***a*** <note>original: "un"</note> is the article, and ***tyrannical*** <note>original: "tyrannique"</note> is the adjective. The first of these words is the Latin *SKILL* <note>original: "ART-e"</note>, and the Greek *VIRTUE* <note>original: "Areté"</note>; all come from the primitive word **AR**, the Earth. It is this force, this value, this virtue with which one develops the **Earth**, making it produce admirable things—even men themselves. <note>This reflects an 18th-century linguistic theory that many abstract words originate from agricultural roots; here, "Art" is linked to the "plowing" or cultivation of the Earth.</note>

***Tyrannical*** comes from ***tyrant***: but this word is Greek and Latin; it comes from the primitive *Tyr*, *Tur*, *Tour*, a Castle, a Fortress. <note>These variations refer to the roots for "tower" or "stronghold."</note> A ***Tyrant*** was he who dominated over all...

->HIST. NATUR. DE LA PAROLE.<-

the Citizens govern themselves without depending on a Master: thus their country is called from two words ***RE-PUBLIC***, ***the public thing, the thing that belongs to all the people, to the Nation.***

***He*** is the subject; it is the masculine singular pronoun of the third person: it indicates the person being spoken of, and who is named in the first phrase, the ***Orator***.

***Speaks*** is the Verb and the quality, standing in for ***speaking***. This Verb, to whose family ***speech*** <note>original: "parole"</note> and ***speaker*** <note>original: "parleur"</note> belong, comes from the primitive root *bar*, *var*, *par*, which has become the root of the word ***speech*** in every Language.

***Strongly*** is an adverb; it serves to determine the manner in which the Orator speaks, it is ***strongly***, that is to say, in an extremely strong manner; it belongs to the same family as ***to force***, ***effort***, ***reinforcement***, etc.

***On the common salvation*** <note>In this context, "salut commun" refers to the public welfare or the collective safety of the state</note> is the topic of his discourse, the object about which he discoursed: this object is the ***common salvation***: this is shown by the preposition ***on*** <note>original: "sur"</note>, which marks the relationship of these words with the Verb ***he speaks***.

<page-num>363</page-num>
<header>UNIVERSAL GRAMMAR</header>

*Salvation* <note>original: "salut"; in this context, it refers to the safety or well-being of the state</note> is a masculine noun; it is of Latin and Hebrew origin.

*Common* is its adjective, and it is likewise Latin; it designates that which belongs to the whole of Society, which was called *COM* in the primitive Language. From this comes the name *Comitia* <note>original: "Comices," referring to the Roman legal assemblies</note>, given in Latin to the assembly of the People, and the preposition *cum* <note>Latin for "with"</note>, which signifies *with* or *together*.

->III.<-

Our Poet <note>referring to Jean de La Fontaine, whose work is being analyzed</note> has placed the adjective before the noun here; he would have placed it last had he been writing in prose. One says *the common salvation*, *the common good*. However, many adjectives in French are placed before the noun: they would even offend the ear if they were placed after: thus we say, *little sheep*, *innocent animals*, *proud reason*, *sweet idleness*, *vast Universe*, and not *sheep little*, *reason proud*, *idleness sweet*, etc.

Our Grammarians have never indicated the cause of this. Who would not be astonished to see that Monsieur du Marsais <note>César Chesneau, sieur Dumarsais (1676–1756), a famous French grammarian known for his work on the "Encyclopédie"</note> contents himself with saying on this subject: "because the mind perceives in...

<meta>This page continues the author's argument from page 364, where he asserts that the placement of adjectives in French is governed by "the ear"—specifically the rhythmic and phonetic qualities of the words—rather than arbitrary rules of usage.</meta>

<page-num>365</page-num>
<header>UNIVERSAL GRAMMAR</header>

...harmony than the opposite arrangement offers; if one says, on the contrary, *a criminal care* <note>original: "un criminel soin"</note>, *a cruel wolf* <note>original: "un cruel loup"</note>, *a violent fire* <note>original: "un violent feu"</note>, the ears will be irritated and torn by them, just as they are by false notes. But what is the nature of these adjectives and these nouns whose placement is displeasing? It is that they are preceded by longer words; it is that a dry and brittle sound follows a full sound; it is that the <term>pause</term> <note>The author uses the term "repos," referring to the natural rhythmic rest or cadence at the end of a spoken phrase.</note> occurs at the wrong time: put the dry tone first, let the full and mellow tone follow and create the pause, and all will be well. 

Does one want another proof? It is that when the tones of the noun and the adjective are of the same nature, it is a matter of great indifference which one is placed first. One says equally well, *deceptive appearance* and *deceptive appearance* <note>original: "apparence trompeuse & trompeuse apparence"</note>, *solid pleasures* and *solid pleasures* <note>original: "plaisirs solides & solides plaisirs"</note>.

It is for the same reason that these nouns *man* and *woman* <note>original: "homme" and "femme"</note> usually precede the adjective: their sound is too dull <note>The author likely refers to the "e muet" or "silent e" at the end of these French words, which creates a soft, trailing sound.</note> to feature suitably as the final word. Thus, one says *a strong man* <note>original: "un homme fort"</note>, *a courageous man* <note>original: "un homme courageux"</note>, *a prudent woman* <note>original: "une femme prudente"</note>, *a generous woman* <note>original: "une femme généreuse"</note>;

## IV.

->They did not listen to him.<-
<note>original: "On ne l'écoutoit pas."</note>

This sentence is composed of three parts: a subject, *on*; a negative <term>attribute</term> <note>In 18th-century grammar, the "attribute" refers to the verb and its modifiers which describe the state or action of the subject.</note>, *did not listen* <note>original: "n'écoutoit pas"</note>; and an object, *him* <note>original: "le"</note>.

*On* was originally the word *man* <note>original: "homme"</note>, and in the plural: the Ancients would have said, *men did not listen to him* <note>original: "homs ne l'écoutoient pas." The author is referencing the Old French "hom" or "on" derived from the Latin "homo."</note>.

This word became so common that it was altered and changed into *on*, which no longer signified anything specific, and which was placed in the singular as if it were a singular noun; and then it became a *pronoun*. In effect, it is "someone" whom we call *on*; but this "someone" here refers to all those to whom the Orator was speaking.

*Le* <note>The direct object "him."</note> is one of the cases of the third-person masculine singular pronoun, *he* <note>original: "il"</note>. We

### V.

> .... The Orator had recourse
> To those violent figures of speech
> Which know how to stir the slowest of souls.
<note>original: "L'Orateur recourut / A ces figures violentes / Qui savent exciter les ames les plus lentes."</note>

This sentence is composed of only three parts: a subject, an <term>attribute</term>, and a term; but the last part is highly complex.

The subject is *the Orator* <note>original: "l'Orateur"</note>: it unites two words, a noun and its article *the* <note>original: "le"</note>. Here, we say *the Orator*, and not *an Orator*, because we are speaking of a known Orator; it is the one who was at the Podium <note>original: "Tribune"</note>, who spoke forcefully; thus, it is enough to indicate that it is the same person; this is the function of the article *the*.

*To those violent figures* <note>original: "A ces figures violentes"</note> is the term <note>In 18th-century linguistics, the "term" often refers to the object or the prepositional phrase that completes the action of the verb.</note>, composed of four words: the Preposition *To* <note>original: "A"</note>, which shows that these figures are what the Orator had recourse to; and the Article *those* <note>original: "ces"</note>, feminine plural, which indicates the object to which the Orator had recourse: *those violent figures*. It is not to *a* figure, nor to *some* figures, but to

<meta>This page continues the grammatical dissection of the sentence introduced on page 367: "The Orator had recourse to those violent figures of speech which know how to stir the slowest of souls." The author is currently explaining the specific functions of the noun "figures" and its modifiers.</meta>

<page-num>368</page-num>
<header>NATURAL HISTORY OF SPEECH</header>

those specific, well-known <term>figures</term> <note>rhetorical devices or tropes</note>, which are presented in such a way that they cannot be mistaken. Next come the noun *<term>figures</term>*, feminine plural, and its <term>adjective</term>, *<term>violent</term>*. This noun and this <term>adjective</term> come to us from the Latin language <note>original: "figura" and "violentus"</note>; but the latter was common to that language as well as Greek <note>original: "βιαῖος" (biaios)</note>.

This term, *to these violent <term>figures</term>*, is accompanied by a complement which itself forms a new Scene <note>original: "Tableau"; the author views sentences as visual arrangements of ideas</note> contained within the first, and which for this reason is called *an <term>incise</term>* <note>a subordinate or parenthetical clause</note>. It is this phrase: *which know how to stir the slowest of souls*. Within it, we see a <term>subject</term>, *which*; a <term>Verb</term>, *know*; the complement of this <term>Verb</term>, *to stir*; and an <term>object</term>, *the slowest of souls*. This <term>object</term> is itself composed of a noun and an <term>adjective</term>, and this <term>adjective</term> is a <term>relative superlative</term>, to distinguish it from the <term>absolute superlative</term> *very slow*.

*Which* <note>original: "Qui"</note> is a word that has been called a *<term>relative pronoun</term>*, and which we have seen is an <term>elliptical conjunctive</term>: indeed, when one says *he had recourse to those violent figures which know how to stir the slowest of souls*, it is as

ject; *he* is the subject; *made*, *thundered*, *said*, is its attribute; it is like three phrases in one: *he made*, *he thundered*, *he said*. By making only one of them, the scene becomes more rapid.

The first Verb has a complement, *to speak*; and an object, *the dead*. The third is accompanied by an object that forms an <term>elliptical clause</term> <note>original: "incise elliptique"; a phrase where some words are omitted but understood by the reader</note>, *what he could*; it stands in place of this: *he said many things; those things that he could say*. *Ce* <note>meaning "that" or "what"</note> is therefore an article here, and its noun is implied. Here again, we find an indeterminate singular instead of a plural; just as in *on* <note>the French indefinite pronoun for "one" or "they"</note>, and as in *tout* <note>meaning "all" or "everything"</note>: EVERYTHING THAT *you do*, *is well*. Here, even in French, are Verbs in the singular that should be in the plural: for one ought to say, *all the things that you do are well*. This will serve to explain a Greek construction <note>The author refers to a rule in ancient Greek where a neuter plural subject takes a singular verb, known as the "Attic Schema."</note>, for which it would otherwise be difficult to provide a reason.

Two Verbs in this phrase are irregular, *made* <note>original: "fit"</note> and *could* <note>original: "put"</note>. The first is conjugated thus: *I make* <note>original: "je fais"</note>, *I made* <note>original: "je fis"</note>, *I will make* <note>original: "je ferai"</note>, *to make* <note>original: "faire"</note>, *made* <note>original: "fait"</note>. The second: *I can* <note>original: "je peux"</note>, *I was able* <note>original: "je pouvois"</note>, *I could* <note>original: "je pus"</note>, *I will be able* <note>original: "je pourrai"</note>, *to be able* <note>original: "pouvoir"</note>, *been able* <note>original: "pu"</note>.

### VII.

*Nobody* <note>original: "Personne"</note> is used here in an absolute sense, instead of *any of the Spectators*.

*The animal with frivolous heads*. An ingenious figure of <term>Rhetoric</term> <note>the art of persuasive speaking or writing</note>. From the entire Athenian People, the Poet creates *an animal with several heads* who breathe nothing but frivolity.

*What did the Orator do?* <note>original: "Que fit le Harangueur?"</note> *What* instead of *what thing*; the *Orator* is the subject of the sentence, and yet he follows the Verb; this is because the sentence is interrogative.

> And of the peril that threatens him,
> He alone among the Greeks, he neglects the effect.

This sentence is the only one where there is an <term>inversion</term> <note>a reversal of the normal word order, often used in poetry for emphasis or rhythm</note>. *Of the peril that threatens him* is the <term>complement</term> <note>a word or phrase that completes the meaning of another</note> of the word *effect*—the effect of the peril. Thus, the complement far precedes the word it completes. But it was able to be separated because the preposition *of* <note>original: "de"</note> which is at the head, proves that it is a complement.

## CHAPTER IX.

### *Analysis of several Verses from the first Fable of Phaedrus in Latin.*

These same principles will serve equally to analyze the Latin Language, and to compare it with the French Language. To be convinced of this, let us analyze several verses from the first Fable of Phaedrus: that Fable which all those who have even a slight knowledge <note>original: "quelque teinture"</note> of Latin know by heart, just as one knows, *the Grasshopper having sung all Summer* <note>The author is referring to the famous opening line of Jean de La Fontaine’s "The Grasshopper and the Ant," implying that the Latin fable of the Wolf and the Lamb is equally foundational in education.</note>.

->I.<-

> *To the same stream the Wolf and the Lamb had come,* <note>original Latin: "Ad rivum eumdem Lupus & Agnus venerant"</note>
> *Driven by thirst.* <note>original Latin: "Siti compulsi."</note>

This phrase is composed of four parts <note>The original text says "four words" (quatre mots), but the author clearly refers to four grammatical components or groupings.</note>. A term <term>term (the goal of an action)</term>, *to the same stream* <note>original: "ad rivum eumdem"</note>; a compound subject, *the Wolf and the Lamb* <note>original: "Lupus & Agnus"</note>; a Verb, *had come* <note>original: "venerant"</note>; a circumstance, *driven by thirst* <note>original: "siti compulsi"</note>. The term is recognized by the preposition *to* <note>original: "ad"</note> and by the <term>accusative</term> <note>the grammatical case used for the direct object or the goal of a motion</note>, which is the case of the word *stream* <note>original: "rivum"</note>. The subject, *the Wolf and...*

NATURAL HISTORY OF SPEECH.

The *Lamb* <note>original Latin: "Agnus"</note> is recognized by the <term>nominative case</term> <note>the grammatical case used for the subject of a sentence</note>. The *Verb* <note>original Latin: "venerant" (had come)</note> is recognized because it is in the third person and in the plural, having two singular subjects. The circumstantial element is recognized because *driven* <note>original Latin: "compulsi"</note> is a participle; and *by thirst* <note>original Latin: "siti"</note> being in the <term>ablative case</term> <note>the grammatical case often used to express cause, manner, or means</note>, marks the cause by which the Wolf and the Lamb were driven.

There should have been a fifth part here, which would designate the purpose of the Wolf and the Lamb coming to the same stream; but it was omitted because it cannot be mistaken: when one is thirsty and goes to a stream, it is to drink—does one need to say it? What would the Poet take us for?

## II.

->The Wolf was standing higher up,<- <note>original Latin: "Superior stabat Lupus"</note>
->And the Lamb much lower down.<- <note>original Latin: "Longèque inferior Agnus"</note>

> "The Wolf was placed high up, & the Lamb much lower."

Here are two phrases joined into a single scene by the conjunction *and* <note>original: the Latin suffix "-que" attached to "longe"</note>. Each of these phrases is composed of a subject and an attribute. The Wolf is the subject of the first, and the Lamb is the subject of the second; they are recognized because they are in the nominative case.

# UNIVERSAL GRAMMAR

The attribute in the first phrase,
composed of these two words, *was standing higher* <note>original Latin: "superior stabat"</note>, was
placed higher up.
The attribute of the second is formed from
the same word *was standing* <note>original Latin: "stabat"</note> which has been implied as
unnecessary, and *much lower* <note>original Latin: "longé inferior"</note>.
*Higher* <note>original: "superior"</note> and *lower* <note>original: "inferior"</note> are in the <term>nominative case</term> <note>the grammatical case used for the subject of a sentence</note>
just like the subject, because they form an
essential part of its attribute, and thus
they are in <term>agreement</term> <note>the grammatical rule where words change their endings to match the word they describe</note> with it.

> Then, driven by his wicked greed,
> the Thief provoked a reason for a quarrel. <note>original Latin: "Tunc fauce improbâ Latro incitatus, jurgii causam intulit."</note>

> "Then by his cruel voracity this brigand
> "driven on, stirred up a cause for dispute."

This sentence is composed of five
members.
An adverb of circumstance, *then* <note>original Latin: "tunc"</note>.
A subject, *the Thief* <note>original Latin: "Latro"</note>; thus
it is in the nominative case.
A circumstance, *driven by his wicked greed* <note>original Latin: "fauce improbâ incitatus"</note>,

<meta>This page continues the grammatical breakdown of the Latin sentence from Phaedrus's fable: "Tunc fauce improbâ Latro incitatus, jurgii causam intulit." # NATURAL HISTORY OF SPEECH

An attribute, *stirred up* <note>original Latin: "intulit"; French translation in text: "suscita"</note>.

An object, *a cause for a quarrel* <note>original Latin: "jurgii causam"</note>.

The circumstantial element, *driven by his wicked greed* <note>original Latin: "fauce improbâ incitatus"</note>, is composed of a participle, *driven* <note>original Latin: "incitatus"</note>, and a noun, *throat/greed* <note>original Latin: "fauce"</note>, which expresses the motive by which the Wolf was pushed, *by his cruel voracity*. This is why this noun is in the <term>ablative case</term> <note>a grammatical case in Latin often used to show the cause, instrument, or manner of an action</note>, this case always being dedicated to the cause by which a thing takes place. It comes from *throat* <note>original Latin: "faux, faucis"</note>, which literally means *gullet* or *maw*: but we do not say *a cruel gullet*; thus we substitute *voracity* for *gullet*, while the effect of the "Tableau" <note>the mental image or scene described by the words</note> remains the same.

*Wicked* <note>original Latin: "Improbus, -a, -um"</note>, an adjective modifying the Latin word *throat* <note>original Latin: "faux"</note>, literally means *wicked* or *reprehensible*; it comes from *not* <note>original Latin: "in"</note> and *good, upright, or honest* <note>original Latin: "probus"</note>.

The object is identified by the <term>accusative case</term> <note>the grammatical case used for the direct object of a action</note> *cause* <note>original Latin: "causam"</note>, the case which is dedicated to it; and the <term>complement</term> <note>a word that completes the meaning of another, here showing what the "cause" belongs to</note> of *cause* is recognized by the <term>genitive case</term> <note>the case usually indicating possession or relationship, often translated using "of"</note> presented by *of a quarrel* <note>original Latin: "jurgii"</note>; this case is dedicated to the complement, as we saw when we explained its nature.

### VI.

->Why, he said, have you made the water muddy for me as I drink?<-
<note>original Latin: "Cur, inquit, turbulentam fecisti aquam mihi Bibenti?"</note>

> "Why," he said, "have you made the water
> "troubled for me who drinks *peacefully*; or
> "why do you disturb the water for me while
> "I am drinking?

This phrase, whose harshness—*why, he said, have you made the water muddy for me as I drink* <note>original Latin: "cur, inquit, turbulentam fecisti aquam mihi bibenti"</note>—perfectly depicts the quarrelsome and sour tone of the Wolf, is composed of a Conjunction, an <term>incise</term> <note>a short phrase inserted into a sentence, such as "he said" or "she replied," to indicate the speaker</note>, a Verb, an object, and a term.

*Why* <note>original Latin: "Cur"</note> is the Conjunction. It is an <term>ellipsis</term> <note>a shorthand where certain words are omitted because they are understood from the context</note> used in place of the phrase *for what reason*. *He said* <note>original Latin: "Inquit"</note> is the incise. One recognizes the object by the <term>accusative case</term> <note>the grammatical form used for the person or thing receiving the action of the verb</note> *water* <note>original Latin: "aquam"</note>; its adjective is recognized by that same feminine accusative form, *muddy* <note>original Latin: "turbulentam"</note>; and the term <note>the person toward whom the action is directed</note> is identified by the <term>dative case</term> <note>the grammatical case usually used for indirect objects</note> *to me* <note>original Latin: "mihi"</note>, for that is its proper case.

The Lamb's response is depicted with no less energy than the fierce complaint...

->The wool-bearing animal, fearing in response,<-

->How can I, I pray you, do what you complain of, Wolf?<-

->From you the liquid flows down to my drinking place.<-

The first of these three phrases contains a subject; *Laniger*, the wool-bearing animal; its adjective *timens*, seized with fear; its <term>attribute</term> <note>the part of a sentence that expresses what is said about the subject</note> partially implied and partially expressed by *contra*, meaning "on the contrary" or "in response": the word *replied* expresses the sense of the whole.

It is no more difficult to analyze the rest of this Fable in the same manner, and to understand the reason why the parts of each sentence are not always arranged in Latin in the same way as in English. Let us not omit that this arrangement is exactly the same in the first of the two verses spoken by the Lamb: *How can I, I pray you, do what you complain of,* <note>original Latin: "Quî possum, quæso, facere quod quereris,"</note>

<meta>catchword: how</meta>

<summary>This page analyzes the Lamb's gentle response in Phaedrus's fable, breaking down the Latin grammar and comparing its syntax to modern language structures. It highlights how the Lamb's fearful but logical defense contrasts with the Wolf's previous aggression.</summary>

<keywords>Phaedrus, Fable, The Wolf and the Lamb, grammatical analysis, syntax, Latin translation, word order, Laniger</keywords>
