The previous page ended mid-sentence with "how"; this page completes that sentence and provides the author's concluding thoughts on the flexibility of Latin syntax compared to French or English.</meta>

<header>UNIVERSAL GRAMMAR</header>

...how can I, I pray you, do what you
complain of? Indeed, the Latin Language—
having the mastery to follow our <term>construction</term> <note>the logical or "natural" word order of subjects, verbs, and objects</note> and
to deviate from it to follow another—at-
taches itself to whichever best serves the har-
mony of each <term>Tableau</term> <note>literally a "painting" or "picture"; the author views a sentence as a visual scene where words can be arranged like figures in a painting</note>: having known how to
make both styles equally natural to itself,
it has secured for itself
greater resources.

# EXPLANATION OF THE PLATES.

---

## 1. REGARDING THE FRONTISPIECE.

Mercury, led by Love <note>represented here as the god Cupid</note>, comes to teach mankind the art of expressing their ideas through Speech, and that of painting them through Writing: such was the source of the Arts and Society, according to the Ancients. Until then, men had been reduced to a wandering and nomadic life, or to hunting; and this is the way of life in which we see them occupied in the distance of the Painting.

Osiris showered Mercury with honors, Diodorus Siculus <note>a Greek historian from the 1st century BCE</note> tells us, because he saw in him extraordinary talents for everything that can be advantageous to human Society. It is Mercury who first formed an exact and regular language, instead of the coarse and unformed sounds that were used before; he invented the first characters <term>characters</term> <note>meaning the written letters or symbols of an alphabet</note>, and regulated even the harmony of words and phrases.

This allegory proves the infinite value that the Ancients placed on Speech and Writing; what indeed would men do without these two vehicles of thought? But what inspired them to use them...

<summary>This page introduces the "Explanation of the Plates," beginning with an allegorical interpretation of the frontispiece. It describes how Mercury, guided by Cupid, brought the gifts of speech and writing to humanity, marking the transition from a primitive, nomadic existence to a civilized society.</summary>
<keywords>Mercury, Cupid, Speech, Writing, Osiris, Diodorus Siculus, allegory, origins of language, civilization, frontispiece</keywords>

--- Page 453 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on the frontispiece from page 382, concluding the philosophical discussion on the origins of the arts before transitioning to the description of the second plate. The text continues a sentence regarding the motivations behind the development of human communication.</meta>

<header>OF THE PLATES.</header>

...and the practice [of these skills], if it is not social love and the desire to make one another mutually happy? It is only this desire for the common happiness that can ignite genius and lead it to produce those marvelous Arts which are the glory of the human spirit, the foundation of society, and the wings upon which man rises even unto the Heavens and ceaselessly expands the empire of his intelligence.

The Gauls held Mercury in no less esteem; they worshiped him, Julius Caesar <note>the Roman general and author of the *Gallic Wars*</note> tells us, as the inventor of the Arts; they depicted him with a golden chain emerging from his mouth, with which he led everyone by their ears <note>This is a reference to the "Gallic Hercules" or Ogmios; the golden chains represent the power of eloquence to bind and lead an audience</note>.

---

## EXPLANATION
### *OF THE SECOND PLATE.*

This Plate presents the primitive and hieroglyphic Alphabet of sixteen <note>original: "XVI."</note> letters. Beside each of these letters, one can see the name of the physical object they represent; the figure of this physical object painted from nature; its abbreviated form or simple outline, which reduces these figures to mere letters and prevented the discovery of their origin; and finally, their relationship to the writing and language of the Chinese. These objects and relationships are more fully developed in our major Work <note>Likely referring to the author’s multi-volume series, *Monde primitif*</note>; however, this provides a sufficient idea of the origin of the Alphabet, especially when—

### NOTICE.

THE *Figures in this Plate were drawn from life and engraved in color by Mr.* GAUTHIER DAGOTY *the Elder* <note>Jacques Fabien Gautier d'Agoty (1716–1785), a French painter and anatomist famous for his early developments in four-color mezzotint printing</note>*, known for his skill in this field. ->O F  T H E  P L A T E S.<-

## FIRST FIGURE.

This figure represents the front part of the tongue, the <term>larynx</term> <note>the voice box containing the vocal folds</note>, and the top of the <term>trachea</term> <note>the windpipe; originally called the "trachée-artère" or "rough artery" because of its ridged texture</note>, viewed from below and from the right side.

(*a*) The *Tongue*, composed of muscles, vessels, nerves, and membranes; it is one of the organs used for the grasping of food, chewing, swallowing, and primarily for the articulation of sounds.

(*b b*) The *right genioglossus muscle*, whose fibers originate from the inner side of the <term>symphysis of the chin</term> <note>the central point where the two halves of the lower jaw bone fuse</note> and spread out as they move; the lower fibers reach the base of the <term>hyoid bone</term> <note>a U-shaped bone in the neck that supports the tongue</note>, which they pull upward and forward; the middle fibers reach the base of the tongue, pulling it forward; the upper fibers reach the tip, which they pull down behind the lower incisor teeth.

(*c d e*) The *right hyoglossus muscle*, turned outward and detached from the hyoid bone. (*c*) The anterior portion, called the <term>basioglossus</term> <note>original: "basioglosse"</note>, named because it originates from the base of the hyoid bone and blends into the sides of the tongue. (*d*) The middle portion, or <term>chondroglossus</term> <note>original: "chondroglosse"</note>, so named because it comes from the small cartilaginous horns <note>the lesser horns of the hyoid</note> and is distributed to the tongue. (*e*) The posterior portion, or <term>greater ceratoglossus</term> <note>original: "grand-ceratoglosse"</note>, which starts from the greater horns of the hyoid bone and connects to the same organ. The hyoglossus lowers the tongue or raises the hyoid bone.

->E X P L A N A T I O N<-

(*f*) End of the right *<term>styloglossus</term>* muscle <note>a muscle that pulls the tongue upward and backward during swallowing</note>, originating from the tip of the <term>styloid process</term> <note>a slender, pointed piece of bone extending down from the skull</note> and the stylo-maxillary ligament, and proceeding to the sides of the tongue, which it carries backward, to the right, and upward.

(*g g*) Right *lingual muscle*, positioned between the <term>hyoglossus</term> and the <term>genioglossus</term> <note>major muscles that move the tongue</note>, with which its fibers interlace. It shortens the tongue.

(*h*) End of the *<term>mylohyoid</term>*. The Engraver has depicted it poorly. To be well represented, instead of ending at the tongue as in this figure, it should terminate at the sides of the base of the <term>hyoid bone</term> <note>the U-shaped bone in the neck that supports the tongue</note>. Its origin is at the interior of the branch and the body of the lower jaw, at the *<term>mylo-hyoid line</term>* <note>original: "apophyse mylo"; a ridge on the inner surface of the jawbone</note>; it elevates the hyoid bone and the tongue at the same time, or lowers the jaw.

(*i*) Another end of a muscle on the left side of the genioglossus: this is perhaps the left genioglossus.

(*j j*) Portions of the two *<term>digastric muscles</term>* <note>named from the Greek for "two-bellied," because the muscle has two distinct fleshy sections</note>, poorly represented. They originate from the <term>mastoid notch</term> <note>a groove on the bone behind the ear</note>; descend fleshy toward the sides of the hyoid bone, where they are tendinous and attached to that bone; then ascend fleshy below the <term>symphysis of the chin</term> <note>the center of the lower jaw</note>, where they terminate. These muscles raise the upper jaw, lower the lower jaw, and can elevate the hyoid bone.

(*k*) Lower part of the right *<term>geniohyoid muscle</term>*, shown too narrow. It originates from the *<term>genial tubercle</term>* <note>original: "apophyse geni"; a small bony projection on the inside of the chin</note> by a slender start and terminates at the base of the hyoid bone by a very wide insertion. It elevates this bone by carrying it forward.

# O F  T H E  P L A T E S.

(**ll**) Portion of the right **stylohyoid muscle** <note>a slender muscle that draws the hyoid bone up and back during swallowing</note>. It originates from the outer side of the **styloid process** <note>a pointed bone extension from the skull</note>, descends to the sides of the **hyoid bone** <note>the U-shaped bone in the neck that supports the tongue</note>, where it is split for the passage of the digastric muscle. It ends beside the **lesser horns** <note>original: "petites cornes"</note> of the hyoid bone, which it carries upward and backward.

(**m**) Right **greater horn** <note>original: "grande corne"</note> of the hyoid bone, which is situated between the tongue and the **larynx** <note>the voice box</note>. It has the shape of an **upsilon** <note>original: "υ"; the twentieth letter of the Greek alphabet</note>, placed horizontally, with the legs facing backward. It serves as a mobile base for the tongue and the larynx.

(**n**) **Posterior hyothyroid ligament**, depicted as too large. It starts from the top of the greater horns of the hyoid bone and ends at the top of the superior horns of the **thyroid cartilage** <note>the large cartilage of the larynx, often forming the "Adam's apple"</note>.

(**o**) Anterior part of the **thyroid cartilage**, which is situated in front of the larynx and above the **cricoid cartilage** <note>a ring-shaped cartilage that forms the lower part of the larynx</note>. It is flattened, quadrilateral, and curved backward. It forms a part of the larynx and, by its movements, changes the size of the **glottis** <note>the opening between the vocal folds</note>.

(**p**) Anterior part of the **cricoid cartilage**, situated below the thyroid and the **arytenoids** <note>paired cartilages that help move the vocal cords</note>. It is circular, wide at the back, narrow in front, and forms the largest part of the cavity of the larynx.

(**q**) The **thyroid gland**.

(**rr**) Right **inferior constrictor muscle** of the **pharynx** <note>the throat</note>, or the **cricopharyngeus** and **thyropharyngeus**. It constricts the pharynx while elevating the larynx, which it also pulls backward.

(**ss**) **Anterior hyothyroid ligament**, which begins

...of the base of the **hyoid bone** <note>the U-shaped bone at the root of the tongue</note>, and ends in the middle of the upper border of the **thyroid cartilage** <note>the large cartilage of the larynx that forms the "Adam's apple"</note>.

( *t t* ) *Upper portion of the two **omohyoid muscles**.* They originate from the upper border of the **scapula** <note>the shoulder blade</note>; they ascend forward, narrowing as they go; then they widen again and attach to the base of the **hyoid bone**, which they lower by pulling it backward.

( *u u* ) *Upper portion of the two **sternohyoid muscles***, which originate from the inner surface of the upper end of the **sternum** <note>the breastbone</note>, and end at the base of the **hyoid bone**, which they lower.

( *x* ) *Right **sternothyroid muscle**.* It originates from the upper part of the **sternum**, above the previous muscles, and ends at the **thyroid cartilage**, though differently than shown in this figure. It lowers the **larynx** <note>the voice box</note>.

( *y* ) *Right **thyrohyoid muscle*** <note>original: "hyo-thiroïdien"</note>, which runs from the base of the **hyoid bone** to the side of the **thyroid cartilage** in an oblique direction. It raises the **larynx** or lowers the **hyoid bone**.

( *z* ) *Left **cricothyroid muscle***, beside the right one. Both originate from the front and upper part of the **cricoid cartilage** <note>the ring-shaped cartilage at the base of the larynx</note> and end at the **thyroid**, further outward than is visible here. They are otherwise well represented.

# OF THE PLATES.
B b iij

(a) Internal part of the base of the **hyoid bone** <note>the U-shaped bone at the root of the tongue</note>.

(c c) The two **posterior hyothyroid ligaments** <note>connective tissues joining the hyoid bone and the thyroid cartilage</note>, (fig. 1. n.)

(d) Left portion of the **thyroid cartilage** <note>the largest laryngeal cartilage, forming the Adam's apple</note>, curved forward, (fig. 1. o.)

(e e) The two **arytenoid cartilages**, placed side by side at the posterior and superior part of the **larynx**, above the **cricoid**. They are pyramidal in shape, make up a small portion of the **larynx**, and change the size of the **glottis** <note>the opening between the vocal folds</note> through their movements.

(f) Posterior and wide portion of the **cricoid cartilage** <note>the ring-shaped cartilage at the base of the larynx</note>, (fig. 1 p).

(g) Membranous part of the **trachea** <note>original: "trachée-artère"; the windpipe</note>, which begins at the **larynx** and divides into two branches that terminate in the lungs for the passage of air during breathing.

(h) **Cricoarytenoid muscle**, shown too long and too narrow: it is better illustrated in fig. 3 ll. It originates from the depression behind the **cricoid cartilage** and ends at the posterior part of the base of the **arytenoids**, which it pulls backward and outward, dilating the **glottis** and stretching the **vocal cords**.

(i) This letter indicates the location of the **transverse arytenoid muscle**, which occupies the interval between the two **arytenoid cartilages** to which it is attached: it brings them together and decreases the width of the **glottis**, (fig. 3. between n n).

->E X P L A N A T I O N<-

(*k*) **Thyro-arytenoid muscle** <note>original: "Muscle thyro-aryténoïdien"</note>, shown a bit too long. It originates from the internal angle of the **thyroid cartilage** <note>the large cartilage forming the Adam's apple</note> and from the **thyrocricoid ligament**; it terminates, in front, at the upper part of the **arytenoid cartilage**, which it pulls forward and inward, shortening the **vocal cords** and narrowing the **glottis** <note>the opening between the vocal folds</note>.

(*l*) **Thyro-epiglottic muscle**, too large and slightly displaced. It is formed only of a few fibers, which originate from the internal angle of the **thyroid cartilage**, above the **thyro-arytenoid**, and terminate on the sides of the **epiglottis**, which they can lower.

(*m*) The **epiglottic cartilage** (or the *epiglottis*), located at the base of the tongue and in front of the upper opening of the **larynx**, which it covers during **deglutition** <note>the act of swallowing</note>.

This figure represents the posterior part of the head, the **larynx**, and the top of the **trachea** <note>original: "trachée-artère"; the windpipe</note>.

(*d*) **Blind opening** <note>original: "Ouverture cécale"; known in modern anatomy as the *foramen cecum*, a small depression on the tongue</note>, located toward the middle of the base of the tongue.

(*ee*) The **uvula** <note>original: "la luette"</note>, which occupies the middle of the **soft palate**, formed of muscles, membranes, vessels, and nerves. It directs the air into the nose or into the mouth, according to its movements.

(*d*) Right portion of the same cartilage, cut.
(*e*) Right posterior part of the **cricoid cartilage** <note>the ring-shaped cartilage situated at the base of the larynx</note>, (*fig. 1. p.*)
(*f*) Right posterior part of the top of the **trachea** <term>trachea-artery: the windpipe that carries air to the lungs</term>, (*fig. 2. g.*)
(*g*) Right **thyro-arytenoid muscle** <note>a muscle that helps regulate the tension of the vocal cords</note>, originating here too high and too far outward.
(*h*) Right **posterior crico-arytenoid muscle**, (*fig. 2 h*, *fig. 3. l. l.*) which could be better represented, as could the left one (*i.*)
(*kk*) The two **crossed arytenoid muscles** <note>muscles that work to close the larynx during swallowing</note> (*fig. 3. n. n.*)

### F I G U R E V.

Here we see the interior of the left side of the **larynx** <note>the voice box</note>, cut vertically from front to back.
(*a*) The **epiglottis** <note>the leaf-shaped flap that prevents food from entering the windpipe</note>, (*fig. 2. m.*, *fig. 3. i.*, *fig. 4. a.*)
(*b*) The left part of the **thyroid cartilage** (*fig. 1. o. fig. 2. g*, *fig. 3. c.*)
(*c*) Left **arytenoid cartilage** <note>one of a pair of small cartilages to which the vocal cords attach</note>, (*fig. 2. e. e.*).
(*dd*) Edges of the section of the **cricoid cartilage**, (*fig. 1. p.*)
(*ee*) Left **ventricule of the larynx** <note>a small pocket or cavity between the vocal folds</note>, located between the superior and inferior ligaments.
(*ff*) The left superior and inferior **ligaments of the larynx** <note>these correspond to the "false" and "true" vocal cords</note>, of which the superior is more distant from

<summary>This page provides a detailed anatomical legend for Figures IV and V of the larynx, identifying the cartilages, muscles, and internal structures like the vocal ligaments and epiglottis.</summary>
<keywords>larynx, thyroid cartilage, cricoid cartilage, trachea, thyro-arytenoid muscle, epiglottis, laryngeal ventricle, vocal ligaments</keywords>

...its counterpart than the lower one. It is the gap between these latter ones that is called the **glottis** <term>glottis: the opening between the vocal folds in the larynx</term>, or the opening in which the air is modified and forms sounds.

## F I G U R E VI.

The left side of the **larynx** <note>the voice box</note> is represented here, of which the left portion of the **thyroid cartilage** <note>the largest cartilage of the larynx, which forms the Adam's apple</note> has been cut away.

(*a*) The **epiglottis** <note>the flap of cartilage that covers the windpipe during swallowing</note>, (*fig. 2. m, fig. 3. i, fig. 4. a, fig. 5. a*.)
(*b*) The **thyroid cartilage**, or the edge where it was cut.
(*c*) Left **arytenoid cartilage** <note>one of the two small cartilages that help move the vocal cords</note>, alongside the right one, which is unlabeled.
(*d*) The **cricoid cartilage** <note>the ring of cartilage that forms the lower part of the larynx</note>.
(*e*) **Thyro-epiglottic muscle** <note>a muscle that helps pull the epiglottis down</note>, (*fig. 2. l.*) depicted much too large.
(*f*) The **arytenoid-epiglottic muscle**, again shown too large and displaced. These are only a few fibers, which originate from the summit of the arytenoid cartilage and disappear into the sides of the epiglottis, which they pull down.
(*g*) **Lateral crico-arytenoid muscle** <note>a muscle that helps rotate the arytenoid cartilages to close the vocal cords</note>. It could be better rendered.

->T H E E N D.<-

## A P P R O B A T I O N.

I HAVE read, by order of My Lord Chancellor, a Manuscript titled *Natural History of Speech*: I have found nothing therein that should prevent its printing. At Paris, April 5, 1776. RIBALLIER <note>Ambroise Riballier (1722–1776) was a doctor of theology and a royal censor.</note>.

## K I N G’ S  P R I V I L E G E.

LOUIS, by the grace of God, etc. To our beloved and loyal Counselors, the People, etc. Greeting. Our beloved Sieur <term>Sieur: A title of respect, similar to 'Sir' or 'Mr.'</term> COURT DE GEBELIN has explained to us that he desires to have printed and given to the Public *The Primitive World analyzed and compared with the Modern World*, etc. FOR THESE REASONS, wishing to treat the Petitioner favorably, We have permitted him, etc. We forbid all Printers, Booksellers, and other persons, of whatever quality and condition they may be, from introducing any foreign printing [of this work] into any place under our obedience, etc. For such is our pleasure. GIVEN at Paris, June 26, in the year of grace 1772, and of our Reign the fifty-seventh. By the King in his Council.
->LE BEGUE.<-
